Nevada Cannabis & Hemp Research

Audited rewrite — Parts 0–12 complete. Research date / date accessed for every fact: 31 October 2026, unless a source itself is dated otherwise. Audit corrections from Part 1 above are applied in this text.

How to read this file. Official government sources were opened directly wherever the research notes say so; where a page could not be opened, or a fact rests on a search extract, a mirror, or a secondary source, it is flagged [VERIFY] or [UNAVAILABLE] and is not presented as confirmed. Secondary sources are used only for non-law facts (market figures, polling, events) and are labelled as such. Nevada’s fiscal year is 1 July–30 June; calendar-year figures are a different series and are never mixed with fiscal-year figures without saying so. Same-name traps avoided: Las Vegas NV ≠ Las Vegas NM; Henderson NV ≠ Henderson KY/TN; Carson City NV (a consolidated municipality, no separate county) ≠ Carson City MI; Boulder City NV ≠ Boulder CO; Mesquite NV ≠ Mesquite TX; Ely NV ≠ Ely MN/UK. The Las Vegas Strip and Paradise, Spring Valley, Enterprise and Winchester are unincorporated towns governed by Clark County, not the City of Las Vegas.

Federal baseline — fixed context, as given in the commission (not re-researched)

  • Split scheduling: state-licensed medical marijuana and FDA-approved products moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714). All adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I. The broader 2024 rescheduling proposal is not final.
  • IRS §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity; adult-use remains fully subject to §280E.
  • Hemp: PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026. After 11 December 2026 the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply.
  • USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026.
  • The federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026.
  • There is no federal cannabis banking law.

Nevada application, and a contradiction check. No official Nevada source opened for Run 1 contradicts that baseline. The Cannabis Compliance Board (CCB) Licensee Update, 20 May 2026, confirms it for Nevada and adds the key Nevada caveat (see Part 1.0): because each adult-use establishment is deemed a dual licensee under NRS 678D.490, only the medical slice of a dual licensee’s activity can be Schedule III / §280E-relieved; the adult-use slice — including every consumption-lounge sale, which is adult-use only — remains Schedule I and fully subject to §280E. The CCB expressly said it “cannot presently represent that the DEA has adopted, or will adopt” a reading that dual-licence deeming alone qualifies an establishment, and that establishments holding a separate medical licence under NRS 678B.215 (mainly in jurisdictions prohibiting adult-use) “appear to present the clearest case”. Source: CCB Federal Rescheduling Licensee Update, 20 May 2026, accessed 2026-10-31. A further risk flag: CCB minutes, 18 June 2026, record that the April 2026 final order was under active challenge in the US Court of Appeals for the DC Circuit with a stay motion filed; if a stay were granted, medical marijuana could return to Schedule I and §280E could be restored. Source: CCB Meeting Minutes, 18 June 2026 — current DC Circuit status as of 31 Oct 2026 is [VERIFY]. Tribal medical activity under a state compact: [VERIFY] whether it counts as “state-licensed” under the federal rule. The CCB’s October 2026 split-payment guidance says tribal establishments are not licensed by the CCB; authority arises from tribal law plus compact. That cuts against an automatic “state-licensed” reading, but it is not a federal determination. Source: CCB Supplemental Split-Payment Guidance, Oct 2026, accessed 2026-10-31.

Part 0 — Headlines

  • Market in decline, officially. FY2025 taxable sales $757,714,911, down roughly 8.6% from $829,225,193 in FY2024. FY2025 excise collected: wholesale (15%) $37,300,235; retail (10%) $74,549,473; transferred to the State Education Fund $95,898,378 ($74,549,473 retail + $21,348,905 wholesale). Source: CCB / Dept. of Taxation joint release, 5 Feb 2026, accessed 2026-10-31. FY2026 taxable sales $695,995,234 (DoT FY26 table, last updated 17 Aug 2026) — see Part 6 for the monthly table. The decline coincides with falling Las Vegas visitation (38.5 million in 2025, down 7.5% from 41.6 million in 2024, LVCVA via secondary reporting 29 Jan 2026), price compression, and a 20–30% illicit market (see Part 6.7); no official source apportions the decline among those causes.
  • Possession limits are now 2.5 oz / ¼ oz. SB 277 (2023) raised adult-use limits from 1 oz / ⅛ oz to 2.5 oz flower and ¼ oz concentrate, effective 1 Jan 2024. Many secondary pages still show the old limits; they are superseded. Source: SB 277 enrolled text and NRS 678D.200, accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Lounges: law in 2021, trickle in practice. AB 341 (2021) created retail and independent consumption lounges; the first state-licensed lounge opened in February 2024 (Smoke and Mirrors (Thrive) — audit correction; not Dazed!). On the CCB List of Licensees updated 26 Oct 2026, 3 lounges are listed (Dazed!, Society, Smoke and Mirrors), but Smoke and Mirrors is reported closed in 2025 in secondary guides — so the corroborated operating state-licensed total is 2, plus 1 operating tribal lounge (NuWu Sky High, Las Vegas Paiute Tribe, not CCB-counted). See Parts 1.5 and 6.4. The 2022 round issued 40 prospective licences (20 retail-attached, 20 independent) on secondary summaries; about 23 conditional licences were still in the inspection/local-approval pipeline in spring 2026 on secondary summaries — [VERIFY] against CCB minutes.
  • Tax structure unchanged in rate, changed in administration. 15% wholesale excise + 10% retail excise + county sales tax (Clark 8.375%, Washoe 8.265%) remain. From 1 Jan 2024 the wholesale tax is on the first wholesale sale only — fair market value for affiliate sales, contracted sales price for non-affiliate sales (AB 430, 2023). A proposal to scrap the wholesale tax and raise retail tax to 12–15% was studied by the 2026 interim Taxation of Cannabis Subcommittee, which recommended no specific change. AB 307 (2025), which would have eliminated wholesale tax and raised retail tax to 14.25%, failed — it is not law. SB 41 (2025) creates a separate Dept. of Taxation Cannabis Tax Permit, effective 1 Jan 2027; existing sellers had to apply by 1 Sep 2026. See Part 2.
  • CCB enforcement and rulemaking. The CCB took over regulation on 1 July 2020 from the Dept. of Taxation. The former Nevada Cannabis Compliance Regulations (NCCR 1–15) are being incorporated into NAC 678A–678D under SB 328 (2023). A sweeping package, LCB File R152-24 (delivery, testing, advertising, packaging, discipline, possession/purchase limits), was reported approved by the Legislative Commission in September 2026 — secondary only, [VERIFY] the filed text. Source: CCB FAQ, ccb.nv.gov/faq, accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Hemp: the open question, not a dispensary-only law. No 2025 statute limits intoxicating hemp to CCB licensees. SB 356 (2025), described in secondary sources that way, died 12 Apr 2025. What did enact is AB 504 (2025, Ch. 484, eff. 1 Oct 2025): signage and deceptive-trade rules for unlicensed sellers of certain hemp products. The Cannabis Advisory Commission Hemp Subcommittee Report, 10 Sep 2026 (created by AB 76 §54.3), finds: no comprehensive programme for hemp-derived cannabinoid products sold outside the licensed system; no statewide minimum purchaser age or ID check for hemp-derived products (a Clark County unincorporated-area 21+ rule is local only); no hemp-specific excise tax (sales tax only); and no retail pathway for intoxicating hemp-derived THC beverages. Source: CAC Hemp Subcommittee Report, 10 Sep 2026, accessed 2026-10-31. Nevada’s implementation of the federal 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 hemp dates is [UNAVAILABLE] in opened official sources — see Part 1.6.
  • Tribal. SB 375 (2017) authorises the Governor to enter marijuana compacts with federally recognised tribes. The Las Vegas Paiute Tribe compact is published (CCB-hosted PDF); NuWu Cannabis Marketplace and its Sky High Lounge operate on tribal land under tribal regulation. A Moapa Band of Paiutes / Mint Cannabis opening on 20 June 2026 is secondary only — [VERIFY] compact/authorisation. See Part 10 in Run 2.
  • Key numbers at a glance. Adult-use possession 2.5 oz / ¼ oz / 6 plants; medical possession 2.5 oz / 12 plants (patient + caregiver collectively); operational licences Dec 2024: 387 (+95 conditional); active licences Dec 2025: 353, of which 107 dispensaries (different snapshot/definition — see Part 4.14, do not read as a verified net closure count); active medical patients Sep 2026: 8,718; hemp growers 1 July 2026: 5 registered growers, 9 handlers, 0 seed producers (NDA via CAC report) — down from 213 growers in 2019.

Part 1 — Laws

1.1 Legal status: exactly what is legal for adults

Both medical and adult-use are legal, under different chapters. Medical: NRS Chapter 678C. Adult-use: NRS Chapter 678D. Establishments are regulated by the CCB under NRS Chapters 678A (administration) and 678B (licensing/control). Sources: NRS 678D and NRS 678C (Rev. 4/15/2026), accessed 2026-10-31.

An adult 21+ is exempt from state prosecution for possession, delivery or production of cannabis and paraphernalia within the limits in §1.3, if not a POST-certified state/local officer in a covered position and if engaging in adult use in accordance with Title 56 (NRS 678D.200(1)–(3)). Delivery without remuneration of up to 2.5 oz / ¼ oz to another person 21+ is covered if not advertised or promoted to the public (NRS 678D.200(3)(f)). Mere presence or vicinity is not constructive possession or conspiracy on its own (NRS 678D.200(2)). Exempt acts cannot be the basis for seizure, forfeiture or a civil penalty (NRS 678D.200(6)).

What is not legal: public consumption, consumption in a retail store or a vehicle (except a licensed lounge), driving under the influence, delivery to under-21s, cultivation within 25 miles of a retail store, cultivation visible from a public place, or cultivation on property without lawful possession or consent, and any amount over the §1.3 limits (NRS 678D.200(4), 678D.310).

Hotels and casinos — the practical ban, stated carefully. No Nevada statute found in Run 1 bans cannabis in every hotel room by itself. The ban is the combination of: (a) public-place law — a casino gaming floor is a public place and consumption there is a misdemeanour, fine up to $600 (NRS 678D.310); (b) owner rights — NRS 678D.510(1)(c) expressly preserves a private property owner’s right to prohibit or restrict smoking, cultivation, processing, manufacture, sale, delivery or transfer on that property; and (c) gaming policy — the Gaming Control Board has maintained since 2014 that gaming licensees must have no involvement with cannabis while federally illegal. Gaming licensee rules are covered in Run 2, Part 9. [VERIFY] the Gaming Control Board memorandum text before citing it as a rule.

Purchase channel: adults must buy from a state-licensed retail store / dual-licensee dispensary. Unlicensed sale is outside the exemption. [VERIFY] the current NRS 678A civil-penalty section number for unlicensed sales (AB 326, 2021, was reported as allowing a civil penalty up to $50,000 pursued by the district/city attorney — secondary summary).

1.2 Constitutional provisions, statutes and regulations

A. Constitution — Nev. Const. Art. 4, §38 (medical only)

Origin: Question 9, approved in 1998 and again in 2000 (Nevada constitutional amendments require two successive general-election approvals). Implementing statute: AB 453 (2001). Official link: Nevada Constitution, accessed 2026-10-31 (§38 text reproduced from a secondary reproduction — [VERIFY] wording against the leg.state.nv.us page before quoting in a filing). Plain summary: directs the Legislature to provide by law for physician-advised medical use for listed conditions, minor restrictions, anti-forfeiture protection, a confidential patient/attendant registry verifiable by law enforcement, and appropriate supply methods. It does not authorise non-medical use or public medical use, and does not require insurer reimbursement or workplace accommodation.

“1. The legislature shall provide by law for: (a) The use by a patient, upon the advice of his physician, of a plant of the genus Cannabis for the treatment or alleviation of cancer, glaucoma, acquired immunodeficiency syndrome; severe, persistent nausea or cachexia resulting from these or other chronic or debilitating medical conditions; epilepsy and other disorders characterized by seizure; multiple sclerosis and other disorders characterized by muscular spasticity; or other conditions approved pursuant to law for such treatment. (b) Restriction of the medical use of the plant by a minor to require diagnosis and written authorization by a physician, parental consent, and parental control of the acquisition and use of the plant. (c) Protection of the plant and property related to its use from forfeiture except upon conviction or plea of guilty or nolo contendere for possession or use not authorized by or pursuant to this section. (d) A registry of patients, and their attendants, who are authorized to use the plant for a medical purpose, to which law enforcement officers may resort to verify a claim of authorization and which is otherwise confidential. (e) Authorization of appropriate methods for supply of the plant to patients authorized to use it. 2. This section does not: (a) Authorize the use or possession of the plant for a purpose other than medical or use for a medical purpose in public. (b) Require reimbursement by an insurer for medical use of the plant or accommodation of medical use in a place of employment.” — Nev. Const. Art. 4, §38. Last amended: not amended since adoption in 2000 on the evidence found [VERIFY].

B. The 3-year amendment bar — Nev. Const. Art. 19, §2(3)

“An initiative measure so approved by the voters shall not be amended, annulled, repealed, set aside or suspended by the legislature within 3 years from the date it takes effect.”

Question 2 passed 8 Nov 2016, effective 1 Jan 2017, so the bar ran to 1 Jan 2020. The 2019 recodification (AB 533, creating Title 56) and later amendments (AB 341, SB 277, AB 76, etc.) are therefore valid legislative amendments. Do not describe post-2020 Title 56 text as “Question 2 text” without noting it is as amended.

C. Question 2 (2016) as codified

Ballot Question 2, 8 Nov 2016, reported at 54% (secondary explainer, June 2025 — [VERIFY] against Secretary of State results in Run 2). Originally codified as NRS Chapter 453D. In 2019, AB 533 repealed/recodified the medical and adult-use schemes into new Title 56, NRS Chapters 678A–678D, and created the CCB. The old 453A (medical) and 453D (adult-use) numbers are historical. What Question 2 originally did: legalised adult-use possession/use for 21+ within limits, created adult-use establishments and the 15% wholesale + 10% retail excise taxes, and directed revenue initially to schools / the Distributive School Account. Later amendments changed destinations, limits, licensing and lounge law. Verbatim initiative text is not reproduced here because the current operative text is Title 56 as amended; [VERIFY] initiative text against the Secretary of State 2016 ballot pamphlet if verbatim initiative language is required.

D. CCB statutes — NRS Title 56, Chapters 678A–678D (Rev. 4/15/2026)

ChapterSubjectOfficial link / key content
678AAdministration; CCB; Cannabis Advisory Commission; definitions; enforcementNRS 678A. Creates the CCB (5 members, Governor-appointed, 4-year terms, no elected state office, no interest in / business with a licensee, max 3 from same party — CCB FAQ). Amended 2025 by AB 76 (Ch. 459) and others. Chapter page itself not separately opened — [VERIFY] link pattern.
678BLicensing and controlNRS 678B. Licence required (678B.210 medical, 678B.250 adult-use); location buffers; agent cards (678B.340/350/355); packaging/labelling (678B.520); sales-facility hemp/CBD sales (678B.520(10)); lounge licensing (678B.320–327); inventory/tracking. Amended 2023 (SB 277 dual-licence deeming), 2025 (AB 76, SB 168).
678CMedical useNRS 678C. Registry cards/letters (678C.220/230), exemption (678C.200/210), prohibited acts (678C.300), delivery (678C.450), nonresident dispensing (678C.470), provider-board protections (678C.650/660), confidentiality (678C.800/850). Added 2019; amended 2021 and 2025 (Ch. 459).
678DAdult useNRS 678D. Exemption (678D.200), prohibited acts (678D.300), violations (678D.310), packaging limits (678D.420), sales/transport (678D.430/440), lounges (678D.455–480), dual-licence deeming (678D.490), local zoning preserved (678D.510). Added 2019; amended 2021 (AB 341 lounges), 2023 (SB 277 limits), 2025 (Ch. 380; Ch. 459).
NRS 678D.200(3)(d)–(e) (adult-use limits + home grow): “(d) Does not, at any one time, possess, deliver or produce more than: (1) Two and one-half ounces of usable cannabis; (2) One-fourth of an ounce of concentrated cannabis; (3) Six cannabis plants, irrespective of whether the cannabis plants are mature or immature; and (4) A maximum allowable quantity of adult-use cannabis products as established by regulation of the Board; (e) Cultivates, grows or produces not more than six cannabis plants: (1) Within an enclosed area that is not exposed to public view that is equipped with locks or other security devices which allow access only by an authorized person; and (2) At a residence or upon the grounds of a residence in which not more than 12 cannabis plants are cultivated, grown or produced.” History: “(Added to NRS by 2019, 3834; A 2021, 2154; 2023, 2570).”
NRS 678D.490 (dual-licence deeming, SB 277, 2023): “1. Each adult-use cannabis establishment shall be deemed to be a dual licensee for the purposes of this title and may engage in any activity relating to the medical use of cannabis in the same manner and to the same extent as if the adult-use cannabis establishment also held a medical cannabis establishment license of the same type for which the establishment holds an adult-use cannabis establishment license.” History: “(Added to NRS by 2023, 2570).” Exception [VERIFY]: separate medical licences may still be issued in a “covered jurisdiction” that prohibits adult-use establishments (NRS 678B.215).
NRS 678D.510 (employer / property / local-government preservation): “1. The provisions of this chapter do not prohibit: (a) A public or private employer from maintaining, enacting and enforcing a workplace policy prohibiting or restricting actions or conduct otherwise permitted under this chapter; (b) A state or local governmental agency that occupies, owns or controls a building from prohibiting or otherwise restricting the consumption, cultivation, processing, manufacture, sale, delivery or transfer of cannabis in that building; (c) A person who occupies, owns or controls a privately owned property from prohibiting or otherwise restricting the smoking, cultivation, processing, manufacture, sale, delivery or transfer of cannabis on that property; or (d) A local government from adopting and enforcing local cannabis control measures pertaining to zoning and land use for adult-use cannabis establishments, including, without limitation, a measure which prohibits the operation of adult-use cannabis establishments.”

E. Consumption-lounge law — AB 341 (2021) + later changes

AB 341, 81st Session (2021), signed June 2021, effective 1 Oct 2021 (secondary coverage — enrolled text and chapter number [VERIFY] on NELIS). It added lounge licensing to Title 56 (now NRS 678B.320 et seq. and NRS 678D.455–480), two licence types, social-equity provisions for independent lounges, and Board regulation of single-use / ready-to-consume products. Local governments may adopt more restrictive lounge rules. CCB lounge regulations were finalised late 2023 (secondary). AB 76 (2025) and SB 168 (2025) touched lounge-adjacent packaging/testing rules; AB 203 (2025), which would have revised lounge provisions, failed (see §1.10). No post-2021 statute found in Run 1 changes the two lounge types or the no-alcohol understanding — [VERIFY] against current NRS 678D.455–480 history lines. Verbatim lounge sections are in §1.5.

F. Controlled substances — NRS Chapter 453

NRS Chapter 453 (Uniform Controlled Substances Act), including NRS 453.096 (“marijuana” defined, excluding hemp within the hemp definition), NRS 453.139 (“THC” defined), NRS 453.336 (possession), NRS 453.3393 (cultivation), NRS 453.560/566 (paraphernalia). Verbatim NRS 453.139 from a FindLaw extract (official NRS 453 page not separately opened — [VERIFY] against leg.state.nv.us):

“‘THC’ means delta-9-tetrahydrocannabinol and any structural, optical or geometric isomer thereof, including, without limitation: 1. Delta-8-tetrahydrocannabinol; 2. Delta-7-tetrahydrocannabinol; and 3. Delta-10-tetrahydrocannabinol.”

This broad isomer-inclusive definition is the legal reason delta-8 cannot be treated in Nevada as if it were delta-9-only hemp — see §1.6. Possession outside Title 56 is under NRS 453.336. Over-limit adult-use possession penalty class is in conflict in secondary sources (category D vs E felony for a first over-limit offence) — [VERIFY]; do not republish a penalty class without opening NRS 453.336 / 678D.310 in full.

G. Hemp — NRS Chapter 557

Official link: NRS Chapter 557 (Rev. 4/15/2026), accessed 2026-10-31. History: added 2017 (SB 305, industrial hemp pilot); amended 2019 (SB 209, SB 347 — renaming “industrial hemp” to “hemp” and aligning with the 2018 Farm Bill); amended 2021; NRS 557.190 amended 2025.

NRS 557.160 (“Hemp” defined): “1. ‘Hemp’ means any plant of the genus Cannabis sativa L. and any part of such a plant, including, without limitation, the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a THC concentration that does not exceed the maximum THC concentration established by the Department for hemp. 2. ‘Hemp’ does not include any commodity or product made using hemp.” History: “(Added to NRS by 2017, 1344; A 2019, 2352, 2583; 2021, 3156).”
NRS 557.180 (“THC” defined): “‘THC’ has the meaning ascribed to it in NRS 453.139.” (Added 2017, 1344; A 2019, 3883.) Combined effect: Nevada hemp THC = all isomers in NRS 453.139, not delta-9 alone, capped at the Department-established maximum.

Other Chapter 557 sections (opened index): 557.140 Grower, 557.150 Handler, 557.170 Producer (agricultural hemp seed), 557.200 Registration (application by 1 July, expiry 31 Dec, fingerprints/background check per 7 CFR §990.6, fees by regulation), 557.205 Site certification/registration, 557.240 Records/inspection/remediation, 557.250 Grower land description, 557.255 Prohibition against synthetic cannabinoid, 557.260 Regulations, 557.270 Testing, 557.280 Department actions, 557.290 Penalties for failure to register. Verbatim text of 557.255 was not reached in the opened lines — [VERIFY] exact wording before quoting. Related law: NRS 439.532 (Dept. of Health and Human Services testing/labelling for hemp/CBD products for human consumption — verbatim in §1.6), NRS 446.844 (approved hemp component in food) and NAC 439.668–670 (hemp-product testing, cited in the CAC report — [VERIFY] NAC text). Regulation: NAC 557, Adopted Regulation R188-24 (Dept. of Agriculture), defines “total THC” (post-decarboxylation / conversion-factor method per 7 CFR §990.1) and revises the acceptable hemp THC level and testing (NAC 557.042, 557.011, 557.136) — search extract only, [VERIFY] full regulation. Whether Nevada currently operates under a USDA-approved State Plan vs USDA direct licensing was not confirmed from a USDA State Plan list in the Part 1 pass; Part 5.11 records the USDA list position (Nevada — NV Approved Plan, updated 17 Feb 2026) — the two passes must be reconciled in Run 2, Part 7, and neither statement should be treated as final until that check.

H. CCB regulations — NCCR → NAC 678A–678D

Nevada Cannabis Compliance Regulations (NCCR) 1–15 (pre-2023) are being incorporated into NAC 678A–678D under SB 328 (2023); CCB rulemaking must now follow the Nevada Administrative Procedure Act (public notice, comment, Board approval at a public meeting). Source: CCB FAQ. Key regulations as cited: NCCR 6 (now NAC 678B.2xx) — production/distribution; 6.010 maximum allowable quantity; 6.080 inventory control; 6.082 seed-to-sale system + fees; 6.100 quality-assurance testing before sale/transfer; NCCR 12 (now NAC 678B.590–626) — packaging/labelling/advertising; compliance labels 12.030–12.045; warnings 12.070; NCCR 15 (now NAC 678D lounge provisions) — consumption lounges (NAC numbers [VERIFY]). Last-amended date for NCCR as a set: [UNAVAILABLE] — individual regulations carry their own adoption dates (e.g. Reg. 6 version 01/03/2023). R152-24 package: see Part 0.4, secondary only.

1.3 Possession limits, home grow, public consumption, vehicle, casino/hotel, product types

ItemAdult-use (NRS 678D.200)Medical (NRS 678C.200)
Flower / usable cannabis2.5 oz at any one time2.5 oz (patient + caregiver collectively)
Concentrate¼ oz at any one timeBoard-regulated maximum quantity of cannabis products (see below)
Plants6 per person; max 12 per residence/grounds; enclosed, locked, not exposed to public view12 collectively, when authorised (see home-grow rule below); enclosed/secure
Products“A maximum allowable quantity of adult-use cannabis products as established by regulation of the Board” (678D.200(3)(d)(4)). The older NCCR 6.010 formulation (1 oz / ⅛ oz equivalent, 3,543 mg THC) is pre-SB 277 and superseded for flower/concentrate; the current NAC maximum-allowable-quantity table must be checked against post-SB 277 / R152-24 text — [VERIFY]. Do not republish 3,543 mg as the current adult-use product cap without that check.Board-regulated maximum quantity; medical purchase tracking by dispensaries is prohibited by the SB 277 provision noted in §1.4 — [VERIFY] current NRS 678C.440 text.

Single-package limits — NRS 678D.420 (current, post-SB 168). A single package must not contain: more than 2.5 oz usable cannabis; for concentrated cannabis, more than 3,500 mg THC; for a capsule, more than 100 mg THC per capsule or 1,000 mg per package; for a tincture, more than 1,000 mg; for an edible, more than 100 mg; for a suppository/transdermal, more than 100 mg each / 1,000 mg per package; for any other adult-use product, more than 1,000 mg. Per-serving edible limit: servings of a maximum of 10 mg per serving (NRS 678D.420(1)). Sources: NRS 678D.420 and SB 168 amendment (800 mg → 1,000 mg increases), A_SB168_R1_658, accessed 2026-10-31. Ready-to-consume lounge products are excepted to the extent inconsistent with Board lounge regulations (NRS 678D.420(3) in the pre-2025 version — [VERIFY] current (3) text). Medical package limits are separate — [VERIFY] NRS 678C / NAC medical caps; do not apply adult-use caps to medical products.

Home grow — adult-use, the 25-mile rule (verbatim, NRS 678D.310(1)):

“Except as otherwise provided in chapter 678C of NRS, a person shall not: (a) Cultivate cannabis within 25 miles of an adult-use cannabis retail store licensed pursuant to chapter 678B of NRS, unless the person is an adult-use cannabis cultivation facility or is a cannabis establishment agent volunteering at, employed by or providing labor to an adult-use cannabis cultivation facility; (b) Cultivate cannabis plants where they are visible from a public place by normal unaided vision; or (c) Cultivate cannabis on property not in the cultivator’s lawful possession or without the consent of the person in lawful physical possession of the property.”

Penalties (NRS 678D.310(2)): 1st — misdemeanour, fine ≤$600; 2nd — misdemeanour, fine ≤$1,000; 3rd — gross misdemeanour; 4th+ — category E felony. Practical effect (secondary, labelled as inference, not statute): because most Nevadans live in Clark/Washoe counties within 25 miles of a retail store, lawful adult-use home grow is in practice rural-only.

Public consumption / vehicle / retail store (verbatim, NRS 678D.310(3)):

“Except as otherwise provided in subsection 10 or by regulations adopted by the Board pursuant to NRS 678B.645, a person who smokes or otherwise consumes cannabis or a cannabis product in a public place, in an adult-use cannabis retail store or in a vehicle is guilty of a misdemeanor punished by a fine of not more than $600.”

Lounge exception — NRS 678D.310(10) (index line): “A person may smoke or otherwise consume cannabis or a cannabis product in a cannabis consumption lounge.” Subsection numbering [VERIFY]. Under-21 falsely representing age to obtain cannabis — misdemeanour with community service / meeting / evaluation options (678D.310(4)); under-21 knowingly entering/loitering/remaining on adult-use premises — fine ≤$500 unless authorised under Chapter 678C and the establishment is a dual licensee (678D.310(5) extract, mirror — [VERIFY] against leg.state.nv.us).

Driving. NRS 678D.300(1)(a)–(b) removes the exemption for driving/operating/being in actual physical control of a vehicle, power-driven vessel or sailing vessel under the influence of cannabis, and for conduct prohibited by NRS 484C.110, 484C.120, 484C.130, 484C.430, NRS 488.400(2), 488.410, 488.420, 488.425 or 493.130. Per se THC thresholds: [VERIFY] post-AB 400 (2021). AB 400 is reported in secondary coverage as removing trace-amount THC/metabolite per se violation; older pages citing 2 ng/ml blood / 10 ng/ml urine must not be republished as current without opening current NRS 484C.110.

Allowed product types. Flower/usable cannabis, concentrated cannabis, edible cannabis products, cannabis-infused products, capsules, tinctures, suppositories, transdermal patches, beverages (as a form of edible/infused product — no separate beverage THC cap was found in NRS 678D.420; beverages fall under the edible/infused 10 mg serving / 100 mg package framework on the evidence found — [VERIFY] beverage-specific NAC rule), vapes (as concentrated-cannabis products), pre-rolls (AB 203 would have expressly authorised production-facility sale of non-infused pre-rolls and failed, so current authority rests on general production/sales provisions — [VERIFY]), topicals (statute’s “any other adult-use cannabis product” 1,000 mg package cap applies). Prohibited product forms (NRS 678B.520, LawServer extract — [VERIFY] against leg.state.nv.us): a production facility shall not produce a product made by applying concentrated cannabis to a commercially available candy or snack food item other than dried fruit, nuts or granola, nor a product modelled after a brand primarily consumed by/marketed to children. Sales-facility additional products (NRS 678B.520(10), SB 168 amendment extract): a cannabis sales facility may sell (a) any commodity or product made using hemp, as defined in NRS 557.160; (b) any commodity or product containing cannabidiol with a THC concentration of not more than 0.3 percent; and (c) any other product specified by Board regulation. This is a licensed-sales-facility permission, not a general-retail intoxicating-hemp permission — see §1.6.

1.4 Medical programme

Qualifying conditions — verbatim, NRS 678C.030 (“Chronic or debilitating medical condition” defined):

“1. An anxiety disorder; 2. An autism spectrum disorder; 3. An autoimmune disease; 4. Anorexia nervosa; 5. Cancer; 6. Dependence upon or addiction to opioids; 7. Glaucoma; 8. A medical condition or treatment for a medical condition that produces, for a specific patient, one or more of the following: (a) Cachexia; (b) Muscle spasms, including, without limitation, spasms caused by multiple sclerosis; (c) Seizures, including, without limitation, seizures caused by epilepsy; (d) Nausea; or (e) Severe or chronic pain; 9. The human immunodeficiency virus and any medical condition related to the human immunodeficiency virus; 10. A neuropathic condition, whether or not such condition causes seizures; or 11. Any other medical condition or treatment for a medical condition that is: (a) Classified as a chronic or debilitating medical condition by regulation of the Division; or (b) Approved as a chronic or debilitating medical condition pursuant to a petition submitted in accordance with NRS 678C.810.” History: “(Added to NRS by 2019, 3812; A 2021, 3197).”

Note: the older DPBH “Approved Conditions” PDF lists only AIDS, cancer, glaucoma, PTSD, cachexia, muscle spasms, seizures, severe nausea, severe pain + “additional conditions, subject to approval” — that PDF is stale relative to current NRS 678C.030, which adds anxiety, autism, autoimmune, anorexia, opioid dependence, neuropathic conditions, etc., and does not list PTSD as a standalone numbered item. PTSD’s current route (symptom/condition framework and/or Division approval) is [VERIFY]; do not list PTSD as a verbatim NRS 678C.030 item. Adding conditions: petition + regulation under NRS 678C.810 (Division = Division of Public and Behavioral Health, Dept. of Health and Human Services, NRS 678C.050).

Practitioner certification. “Attending provider of health care” = a provider as defined in NRS 629.031 who (1) is licensed/certified to practise a profession authorising prescription for a chronic/debilitating condition, and (2) has responsibility for the patient’s care/treatment (NRS 678C.010). The application must include the provider’s signature affirming: diagnosis of a chronic/debilitating condition; medical use may mitigate symptoms/effects; risks/benefits explained; provider will keep valid written documentation and make it available to the Division on request (NRS 678C.220(2)(a)). A licensing board is prohibited from disciplining an attending provider (NRS 678C.650) or licensee (678C.660) solely for participation in accordance with Chapter 678C.

Patient / caregiver cards and fees. Who can apply: Nevada resident only (NRS 678C.220(2); “resident” has the NRS 483.141 meaning, NRS 678C.220(10)). Proof of residency + provider signature + name/address/telephone/DOB required. Minors: age 10 to <18 — registry card; <10 — letter of approval; both require a custodial parent/legal guardian written statement (provider is a Ch. 630/633 physician who explained risks/benefits to child + guardian; guardian consents; guardian agrees to serve as designated primary caregiver; guardian agrees to control acquisition, dosage and frequency) (NRS 678C.220(3)), implementing Art. 4 §38(1)(b). “Designated primary caregiver” = 18+, significant responsibility for managing the patient’s well-being, designated under NRS 678C.270; does not include the attending provider (NRS 678C.040). Card contents: serially numbered card with name, address, photograph, DOB, issuance/expiration dates (NRS 678C.230(1)–(2)). Duration in years and renewal procedure: [VERIFY] — opened lines stop before the duration subsection; secondary sources state 1- or 2-year cards tied to provider certification. State fee — secondary, consistent across multiple secondary sources, [VERIFY] against the DPBH registry page: $50 for 1 year; $100 for 2 years, paid to DPBH; provider evaluation fee is separate. DPBH administers the registry; the CCB does not issue patient cards. Deemed-card protection while pending: if the Division has not yet approved/denied, the applicant (and caregiver, if any) “shall be deemed to hold” a card/letter and may present a copy of the application as proof (NRS 678C.220(8)). Denial grounds are listed in NRS 678C.220(5); denial is a final decision for judicial review under Ch. 233B, limited to arbitrary/capricious/abuse-of-discretion (678C.220(6)); reapplication barred for 6 months unless the Division/court allows shorter (678C.220(7)).

Purchase limits (medical). Possession (collective, patient + caregiver): 2.5 oz usable + 12 plants + Board-regulated maximum quantity of cannabis products, safeguarded in an enclosed, secure location (NRS 678C.200(3)). SB 277 digest notes the Board “shall not adopt a regulation or in any other way require a medical cannabis dispensary to track the purchases of a person or determine whether the person has exceeded the legal limits on the possession of cannabis for medical purposes, as set forth in NRS 678C.200” — [VERIFY] current NRS 678C.440 text. Tax advantage (for Part 2 cross-reference only): medical purchases are exempt from the 10% retail excise tax; ordinary sales taxes still apply — [VERIFY] against NRS 372A.290.

Patient home grow (verbatim, NRS 678C.200(6)):

“Notwithstanding any other provision of law and except as otherwise provided in this subsection, after a medical cannabis dispensary opens in the county of residence of a person who holds a registry identification card, including, without limitation, a designated primary caregiver, such a person is not authorized to cultivate, grow or produce cannabis. The provisions of this subsection do not apply if: (a) The person … was cultivating, growing or producing cannabis in accordance with state law on or before July 1, 2013; (b) All the medical cannabis dispensaries in the county of residence … close or are unable to supply the quantity or strain of cannabis necessary for the medical use of the person to treat his or her specific medical condition; (c) Because of illness or lack of transportation, the person … is unable reasonably to travel to a medical cannabis dispensary; or (d) No medical cannabis dispensary was operating within 25 miles of the residence … at the time the person first applied for his or her registry identification card.” History: “(Added to NRS by 2019, 3814; A 2021, 2152).”

The adult-use 25-mile rule and the medical rule are different tests — adult-use asks about an adult-use retail store; medical asks about a medical dispensary in the county / 25 miles at first application, plus the three other exceptions. Do not conflate them.

Visiting patients / reciprocity. NRS 678C.470 — “Medical cannabis dispensary authorized to dispense cannabis to nonresidents of this State under certain circumstances.” Effect (secondary summary, [VERIFY] verbatim 678C.470 text — index lines were not opened): a visitor authorised under home-state medical law is treated as holding a Nevada card while staying within Nevada limits, and a Nevada medical dispensary may sell to them on proof of that authorisation; Nevada itself issues cards to residents only, so a visitor cannot apply for a Nevada card. Because adult-use is legal for 21+, reciprocity matters mainly for under-21 visitors, tax-exempt medical purchases, and medical possession protections — but do not state a visitor tax exemption as fact without opening NRS 372A.290 — [VERIFY].

How medical changed since adult-use began. 2017: adult-use sales began 1 July 2017 through existing medical dispensaries (early-start programme — secondary MPP summary, [VERIFY] early-start statute). 2020: CCB took over both programmes (1 July 2020) from the Taxation/DPBH split (DPBH kept the patient registry). 2023 (SB 277): dual-licence deeming (NRS 678D.490) collapsed the separate medical/adult-use establishment distinction for most operators; adult-use limits raised to match medical flower limits (2.5 oz); fee maxima lowered; medical purchase-tracking prohibition noted above. 2025 (AB 76 / SB 168): packaging, testing and fee/licence administration changes applied to both programmes via Title 56 amendments. What did not change: patient registry remains DPBH-administered, residency-gated, provider-certified, and confidential (NRS 678C.800); medical possession remains 2.5 oz / 12 plants; medical home-grow remains exception-gated (678C.200(6)), not open as of right.

1.5 Consumption lounges

TypeStatutory basisWhat it may do
Retail cannabis consumption loungeNRS 678D.470; licensing NRS 678B.320 et seq.Attached or immediately adjacent to an adult-use retail store. May (a) obtain single-use products from that store for resale, and cannabis/products from that store for producing ready-to-consume products; (b) sell single-use products to lounge customers; (c) prepare ready-to-consume products using that cannabis and sell them to lounge customers. Must ensure only single-use / ready-to-consume products purchased from that lounge are consumed there (678D.470(2)).
Independent cannabis consumption loungeNRS 678D.475; licensing NRS 678B.320 et seq.Standalone. Must enter into a contract with one or more adult-use retail stores to sell it single-use products for resale and cannabis/products for preparing ready-to-consume products (678D.475(1)). May then sell single-use and prepare/sell ready-to-consume products on the same terms (678D.475(2)). Must ensure only products purchased from that lounge are consumed there (678D.475(3)). Board may require submission of the contract for review (678D.475(4)).
NRS 678D.465 (prohibited acts, all lounges): “A cannabis consumption lounge shall not allow: 1. The consumption of cannabis or cannabis products at any place which is within view of a public place; 2. The entry of any person who is less than 21 years of age to the cannabis consumption lounge; 3. The consumption of any cannabis or cannabis product in the cannabis consumption lounge that is not a single-use cannabis product or ready-to-consume cannabis product; or 4. A single-use cannabis product or ready-to-consume cannabis product that was purchased at the cannabis consumption lounge to be removed from the premises of the cannabis consumption lounge.” History: “(Added to NRS by 2021, 2375).”
NRS 678D.460 (food / entertainment): “A cannabis consumption lounge may: 1. Sell food and beverages to customers of the cannabis consumption lounge; 2. Sell any other item which does not contain cannabis or cannabis products and is not intended for use with cannabis or cannabis products to customers of the cannabis consumption lounge; and 3. Provide live entertainment at the cannabis consumption lounge.” Alcohol: lounges are alcohol-free under AB 341 as reported in secondary coverage; the opened NRS 678D.455–480 text does not itself state “no alcohol” in the lines reached — [VERIFY] alcohol prohibition section (likely NRS 678D.455 / NAC lounge regs) before stating as statute.

NRS 678D.455 (operations, index description): installation of ventilation and exhaust system; training of employees; submission of certain plans to Board; disposal of cannabis and cannabis products; compliance with local ordinances and rules and regulations of Board. Full 678D.455 text not reproduced — [VERIFY].

NRS 678D.480 (Board regulation duty): “The Board shall adopt regulations governing the sale and consumption of single-use cannabis products and ready-to-consume cannabis products at a cannabis consumption lounge. Such regulations must, without limitation: 1. Prescribe a list of single-use cannabis products comprising each type of cannabis and adult-use cannabis product that the Board has determined to be appropriate for consumption at a cannabis consumption lounge; 2. Establish standards for the content, quality and potency of ready-to-consume cannabis products, including, without limitation, the maximum THC concentration for such products; 3. Prescribe procedures and protocols for the preparation and safe handling of ready-to-consume cannabis products…; 4. Establish requirements relating to the sale of ready-to-consume cannabis products, including, without limitation, requirements relating to notifications that must be provided to a purchaser…; and 5. Set forth any other requirements…” Ready-to-consume maximum THC / session limit (secondary guides cite a 50 mg THC session concept) — [VERIFY] against current NAC lounge regs; do not state 50 mg as statute.

Social equity priority for independent lounges. Definition — NRS 678B.065 (Justia extract, [VERIFY] against leg.state.nv.us): “‘Social equity applicant’ means an applicant for the issuance or renewal of an adult-use cannabis establishment license for an independent cannabis consumption lounge who has been adversely affected by provisions of previous laws which criminalized activity relating to cannabis, as determined by the Board in accordance with the regulations adopted pursuant to NRS 678B.323. Such adverse effects may include, without limitation, adverse effects on an owner or officer of the applicant.” History: “(Added to NRS by 2021, 2357).” Allocation rule — NRS 678B.327 (LawServer extract, [VERIFY]): for each local jurisdiction that limits the number of business licences for lounges, the Board determines the allocation for retail vs independent; not more than 50% may be retail; at least 50% must be independent; at least 50% of independent licences must go to social equity applicants; if insufficient social-equity applicants, issue to all qualified social-equity applicants and hold the remainder in reserve for future social-equity issuance; if qualified applicants exceed allocation, separate lotteries per licence type for non-social-equity applicants. Fee relief — secondary only, [VERIFY]: 75% reduction in the administrative processing fee, technical assistance, and priority processing; at least 10 of the first 20 independent consumption lounge licences were reserved for social equity applicants. Application-fee figures in secondary coverage ($100,000 retail / $10,000 independent, one-time non-refundable) are also [VERIFY] against the current CCB fee schedule — but see Part 4.9, where the same figures appear in current NRS 678B.390 / CCB FAQ tables. AB 203 (2025) would have exempted social-equity independent-lounge applicants from the liquid-assets evidence requirement and removed Board transfer restrictions for independent lounges — it failed, so those changes are not law.

Local approval. Lounges must comply with local ordinances (NRS 678D.455) and obtain a local business licence; a local jurisdiction that caps lounge business licences triggers the NRS 678B.327 allocation/lottery above. Local governments may be more restrictive than state rules (AB 341 Senate amendment, secondary coverage). Specific city/county lounge caps are Part 3 territory. Location bars (from establishment buffers, §1.7): not on airport property; 1,000 ft from schools; 300 ft from community facilities; 1,500 ft from nonrestricted gaming in counties ≥100,000. A Clark County Title 30 proposal would set 1,500 ft from schools and community facilities for lounges, with a Waiver of Development Standards down to the NRS 678B minimum — [VERIFY] adopted vs proposed.

How many licensed / operating — [VERIFY] against the CCB list. See Part 0.2 and Part 6.4: state-licensed operating 2 on Sept-2026 secondary evidence (Dazed!, Society); tribal operating 1 (NuWu Sky High). Conditionally approved / in buildout: “about two dozen” / “roughly 23” in secondary guides — do not publish a conditional-licence total without the CCB list.

1.6 Hemp and intoxicating hemp

Definition + THC measurement (current Nevada law). Hemp = NRS 557.160 verbatim (§1.2G): Cannabis sativa L. plant/part/derivative/extract/cannabinoid/isomer/acid/salt, with THC concentration not exceeding the Department-established maximum, excluding commodities/products made using hemp. THC = NRS 453.139: delta-9 plus structural/optical/geometric isomers including delta-8, delta-7, delta-10. Nevada therefore measures hemp THC on a total-isomer basis, not delta-9-only. This predates the federal total-THC change and is stricter in that respect. Crop testing: NRS 557.270 + NAC 557.136 (R188-24): testing per 7 CFR §990.3, including conversion of THCA to THC by post-decarboxylation or similarly reliable method. Acceptable level: NAC 557.042 / 557.011 — when measurement uncertainty is applied, the range includes ≤ the federal maximum delta-9 level (0.3% dry weight under pre-Dec-2026 federal law).

Delta-8 / delta-9 / THCA / other hemp-derived THC — status, stated as mechanism, not as a settled court holding. Delta-8, delta-10, THC-O, HHC, THCP and other converted/synthetic isomers are treated as THC / cannabis, not hemp, when they exceed the hemp THC maximum, and artificially produced cannabinoids are separately prohibited under NRS 557.255 (synthetic cannabinoid prohibition — exact text [VERIFY]). Secondary synthesis attributes this to SB 49 (2021), which redefined THC to include all THCs and banned production/distribution/sale of artificially produced cannabinoids — SB 49 enrolled text not opened, [VERIFY]. The CAC report’s framing is more cautious: it describes a market for delta-8, hemp-derived delta-9, gummies, beverages and vapes sold outside the licensed system, and a gap in comprehensive regulation — not a court-tested ban on every retail sale. Do not state “delta-8 is banned outright in Nevada” as a settled court holding; state the statutory mechanism (isomer-inclusive THC + synthetic-cannabinoid prohibition + hemp-maximum) and flag enforcement/retail-sale scope as [VERIFY]. Hemp-derived delta-9 within the hemp maximum is lawful as hemp only if the product’s THC (all isomers) does not exceed the Department maximum and, for human-consumption products, it meets NRS 439.532 testing/labelling (below). A high-THC hemp-derived delta-9 gummy/beverage that exceeds the maximum is cannabis, saleable only through a CCB licensee — that is the effect of the definitions, not a separate “dispensary-only hemp law” enacted in 2025. THCA: THCA itself is not listed as a separate isomer in NRS 453.139, but (a) crop testing converts THCA → THC (NAC 557.136), and (b) the federal post-11-Dec-2026 total-THC definition will count THCA. Nevada’s current finished-product THCA treatment outside crop testing: [VERIFY] — no NAC finished-product total-THC rule was found in opened sources.

“Rule limiting them to CCB licensees” — clarification. No enacted 2025 statute was found that says “intoxicating hemp may only be sold by CCB licensees.” What exists is: (1) the definitional route above (over-maximum = cannabis = CCB system); (2) NRS 678B.520(10) permission for licensed sales facilities to sell hemp/CBD products ≤0.3% THC; (3) AB 504 signage/deceptive-trade rules for unlicensed hemp sellers; (4) the CAC recommendation (not law) to consider limiting sales to Board-licensed businesses or, for beverages, alcohol-licensed businesses. Any report claiming a current Nevada “dispensary-only” intoxicating-hemp rule must be corrected to this four-part picture. CBD / non-intoxicating hemp: lawful within the hemp definition + NRS 439.532 testing/labelling; no state age/possession limit for CBD was found in an opened official source — secondary CBD pages claim “no age or possession limit for CBD oil” — [VERIFY]; do not rely on that secondary page for a legal conclusion.

Minimum age. Cannabis (Title 56): 21+ (678D.200, 678D.465(2) for lounges). Hemp-derived cannabinoid products (general retail): no statewide minimum age in statute (CAC report Finding 6, opened PDF). Clark County unincorporated-area hemp-retail ordinance prohibits sale to <21, but only in unincorporated Clark County (CAC report; Clark County Code §§7.220.200–7.220.210 cited therein). Do not state a statewide 21+ hemp age rule — it does not exist on current evidence.

Testing and labelling (hemp, human consumption) — verbatim, NRS 439.532(1) (public.law mirror, [VERIFY] against leg.state.nv.us):

“Unless federal law or regulation otherwise requires, a person shall not sell or offer to sell any commodity or product containing hemp which is intended for human consumption or any other commodity or product that purports to contain cannabidiol with a THC concentration that does not exceed the maximum THC concentration established by federal law for hemp unless such a commodity or product: (a) Has been tested by an independent testing laboratory and meets the standards established by regulation of the Department pursuant to subsection 3; and (b) Is labeled in accordance with the regulations adopted by the Department pursuant to subsection 3.”

Subsection (2) allows submission to a cannabis independent testing laboratory; (3) requires Dept. regulations on testing protocols, contaminant tolerances for foods with an approved hemp component, and non-false/misleading labelling per NRS Chapters 446/585; (4) defines “intended for human consumption” as ingestion, inhalation, or topical application. Note the federal-law cross-reference in 439.532(1) (“maximum … established by federal law”) vs the state-Department maximum in 557.160 — the two maxima must be reconciled after 11 Dec 2026; [VERIFY] which controls for finished products. CAC gap finding: NRS 439.532 “does not expressly create hemp product registrations, retailer or manufacturer licenses, routine inspection procedures, recall procedures, penalties … or a clear [enforcement system]”. Online/out-of-state sellers are covered in text but enforcement is described as challenging, with no in-state-agent/registration requirement.

THC beverages. No Nevada retail pathway for intoxicating hemp-derived THC beverages (CAC Finding 10, opened PDF): no licensing framework, agency, age/potency/tax/distribution/registration/recall/enforcement rules specifically for such beverages through bars, restaurants, liquor stores, grocery/convenience stores. Cannabis-system beverages (as adult-use edible/infused products sold by licensed sales facilities / lounges as ready-to-consume products) are a different category and are regulated under Title 56 / NCCR — do not conflate the two beverage markets. Food/beverage adulteration overlay: CAC report notes state/federal food-safety/adulteration standards “appear to prohibit” adding CBD/THC to food/beverages sold in Nevada, yet CBD gummies/ingestibles remain widely available — [VERIFY] NAC 439.6xx food rules before advising on any beverage product.

State response to federal PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019. As given: §781 eff. 12 Nov 2026; §2019 limits §781 until 11 Dec 2026 (only the synthetic/non-naturally-producible cannabinoid paragraphs apply in the interim); after 11 Dec 2026, full total-THC definition + 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply. CAC report (Sept 2026) confirms this staging and quotes §2019, and recommends maintaining alignment with PL 119-37 (total THC + final-product restrictions). No Nevada statute, regulation, Dept. of Agriculture guidance or CCB guidance implementing the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 dates was found in an opened official source in Run 1 — [UNAVAILABLE / VERIFY]. In particular: whether Nevada will amend NRS 557.160 / NAC 557.042 to adopt total THC (including THCA) for finished products (vs crops only) — [VERIFY]; whether the 0.4 mg/container cap will be adopted, enforced as a hemp exclusion, or left to federal enforcement — [VERIFY]; whether the USDA DEA-lab testing delay to 31 Dec 2026 changes Nevada’s NAC 557.136 lab requirements — [VERIFY]. Do not advise that Nevada hemp products automatically become federally unlawful on 12 Nov or 11 Dec 2026 without that Nevada implementation check, and do not advise the reverse.

1.7 Packaging, labelling, advertising (incl. billboard/Strip), delivery, zoning/buffer

Packaging. Pre-approval required: “Packaging must be pre-approved by Board staff. Only licensed establishments or their designees may request approval.” Source: CCB FAQ. Exit / opaque child-resistant layer: NAC 678B.596(2) (NCCR 12.015(2)) — any cannabis/product sold at a sales facility must be placed in a package that is opaque, child-resistant, and can maintain its child-resistant effectiveness for multiple openings before leaving the facility; satisfied by adequate single-product packaging or an exit bag. Source: CCB FAQ. Child-resistant standard (secondary, [VERIFY] against NAC): compliance with 16 CFR Part 1700 federal child-resistant standards, tamper-evident, opaque for non-individually-wrapped products. Statutory packaging duties — NRS 678B.520(1) (LawServer extract, [VERIFY]): products must be labelled clearly/unambiguously “THIS PRODUCT CONTAINS CANNABIS” in bold type + as required by Chapters 678C/678D; regulated/sold on THC concentration, not weight; packaged/labelled to allow inventory-control tracking; not modelled after a children’s brand. Multi-packs (CCB FAQ): allowed only if all units are the same product type, all passed full compliance testing, and total THC does not exceed the regulation limit for that type (e.g. infused pre-rolls ≤7 g net weight / 1,000 mg THC in the FAQ example).

Labelling. Compliance labels must be present on / included with all items from cultivation/production, or with the consumer after purchase; must comply with NCCR 12.030–12.045 (now NAC 678B.605–614 per CCB FAQ labelling Q&A). If information is already on packaging it may be withheld from the compliance label. Warnings (CCB FAQ): all packaging must contain “Keep out of reach of children” and “THIS PRODUCT CONTAINS CANNABIS”; all advertising must contain warnings + establishment information per NCCR 12.070.3. Edible-specific: number of servings at max 10 mg/serving (NRS 678D.420(1)); delayed-intoxication, driving-impairment, alcohol/prescription interaction, health, pregnancy and child-storage warnings in ≥12-point non-italic text are described in secondary summaries — [VERIFY] warning text/font rule against NAC 678B.599. Extraction solvents/gases must be listed on the compliance label; zero/non-detect cannabinoids (e.g. CBG/CBD) may be omitted (CCB FAQ). Single-package THC caps: see §1.3. AB 76 packaging changes (2025): CCB briefing summarises AB 76 as revising packaging/labelling, including a ban on character-based packaging (secondary summary — [VERIFY] AB 76 Ch. 459 packaging sections).

Advertising — CCB FAQ (official) prohibitions list (verbatim paraphrase): no false/misleading statements/illustrations; no promotion of overconsumption; no depiction of consumption by / child or person <21 consuming; no objects suggesting a child (toys, characters, cartoons, mascot, action figure, balloon, or other depiction designed to appeal to / encourage consumption by <21); no product modelled after a brand primarily consumed by/marketed to children; no advertising in any publication/radio/TV/medium if 30% or more of the audience is reasonably expected to be <21; no sponsorship of youth sports/entertainment events including team uniforms; no advertisement within 1,000 feet of a public/private school, playground, public park, or library (grandfathered if placed before the protected use arrived); no advertisement on/inside a public-transportation vehicle or shelter (taxis excepted); no advertisement at a sports/entertainment event to which <21s are allowed entry; no advertisement where prohibited by local ordinance; no advertising/offering cannabis as “free” or “donated” without a purchase. Source: CCB FAQ. Pre-approval: “Pre-approval of advertising is no longer required; however, board staff may review advertising upon request. Civil penalties may be issued for advertising that does not comply with NRS 678A-D and/or NCCR Regulation 6 and 12.” Billboard / Strip advertising — [VERIFY] specific rule: no Nevada statute or CCB rule was found in opened sources that separately bans billboards on the Strip or in Paradise/Enterprise townships beyond the general 1,000-ft / 30%-audience / local-ordinance rules above and Clark County sign/zoning codes. Do not state a Strip-specific billboard ban without opening Clark County Title 30 / sign code. The Scenic Nevada billboard-initiative case (Reno) is about general billboard ordinances, not cannabis advertising — do not cite it for cannabis ads.

Delivery. Medical delivery: NRS 678C.450 (requirements concerning delivery) + NRS 678C.440 (dispensary sale duties). Adult-use delivery: NRS 678D.430 (sales / third-party/intermediary/broker rules) + NRS 678D.440 (transport requirements). Full delivery conditions (vehicle, manifest, address restrictions, delivery to hotels/gaming, age verification at door) were not opened in full — [VERIFY] NAC 678B delivery sections + R152-24 delivery changes. Secondary summaries claiming adult-use delivery was “pandemic-only” are stale and must not be republished. AB 203 delivery proposals (failed): would have allowed delivery to a consumer at any location except school/community-facility/airport property or (with exceptions) within a distance of a gaming establishment, and barred Board pre-inspection of delivery vehicles — not law.

Zoning / buffer distances — statute (verbatim from NRS 678B.210(3)(a)(2)(II) via FindLaw extract, [VERIFY] against leg.state.nv.us):

“…the locations of which may not be on the property of an airport, within 1,000 feet of a public or private school that provides formal education traditionally associated with preschool or kindergarten through grade 12 and that existed on the date on which the application … was submitted to the Board, within 300 feet of a community facility that existed on [that date] or, if the proposed … establishment will be located in a county whose population is 100,000 or more, within 1,500 feet of an establishment that holds a nonrestricted gaming license described in subsection 1 or 2 of NRS 463.0177 and that existed on [that date]”

The same buffers are stated for adult-use licences in NRS 678B.250. Measurement (secondary, [VERIFY]): front door of proposed establishment to nearest lot line of the protected use, certified by a Nevada-licensed professional surveyor (Clark County Title 30 proposal text). Local zoning preserved: NRS 678D.510(1)(d) (verbatim §1.2D) + proof of compliance with city/county zoning + building requirements in the licence application.

1.8 Seed-to-sale tracking — Metrc

System: Metrc (Marijuana Enforcement Tracking Reporting Compliance) is the Board-approved seed-to-sale tracking system. Source: CCB June 2026 split-payment guidance — Split-Payment Guidance, June 2026, accessed 2026-10-31: “All cannabis establishments must use the Board-approved seed-to-sale tracking system (currently METRC) for all cannabis transactions. Every transfer must be fully and accurately reflected in that system.” Regulation: NCCR 6.082 — “Use of seed-to-sale tracking system; payment of fees”; NAC 678B.211 (acquisition policies) and NAC 678B.214 (inventory documentation) are cited in the June 2026 guidance for compliant recording. Full Metrc requirements (tagging, plant/product lifecycle, transfer manifests, daily inventory reconciliation, loss reporting, fee payment) were not opened in full — [VERIFY] current NAC 678B inventory/tracking sections. Scope: cultivation, production, distribution, retail, testing and lounges all report into the same system (secondary summary consistent with CCB guidance). A licence without an activated/functional Metrc account shall not operate (same secondary summary — [VERIFY] NAC wording). Dual-licensee inventory: NRS 678C.430 — “Combined inventory tracking and reporting for dual licensees.” This combined-tracking rule is the operational counterpart to the federal split problem in the federal baseline box: combined state tracking does not equal federal medical/adult-use cost segregation — [VERIFY]. Hemp exclusion: hemp-derived products sold outside the licensed cannabis system are not in Metrc (CAC report Finding 2, opened PDF). Hemp crops are tracked via Dept. of Agriculture registration/inspection, not Metrc.

1.9 Employment and drug-testing protections

A. Pre-employment screening — NRS 613.132 (AB 132, 2019, eff. 1 Jan 2020). Verbatim operative rule (AB 132 First Reprint, legislative exhibit — [VERIFY] codified NRS 613.132 text against leg.state.nv.us):

“1. It is unlawful for any employer in this State to fail or refuse to hire a prospective employee because the prospective employee submitted to a screening test and the results of the screening test indicate the presence of marijuana.”

Exceptions — NRS 613.132(2) (AB 132 extract + secondary summaries, consistent): subsection (1) does not apply if the applicant is applying for: (a) firefighter (NRS 450B.071); (b) emergency medical technician (NRS 450B.065); (c) a position requiring operation of a motor vehicle for which federal/state law requires screening tests; or (d) a position that, in the employer’s opinion, could adversely affect the safety of others. Rebuttal / retest: if an employer requires a screening test within the first 30 days of employment, the employee has the right to submit an additional test at their own expense to rebut the initial result; the employer shall accept and give appropriate consideration to / (in the enrolled summary) accept as conclusive the retest result — wording varies between AB 132 versions in extracts; [VERIFY] codified (3) wording. Other limits: does not apply to the extent inconsistent with an employment contract / collective bargaining agreement, or to federally funded positions. “Screening test” = blood, urine, hair or saliva test for general presence of a controlled substance or other drug. Scope limit — critical: the rule restricts hiring decisions based on a pre-employment positive, not testing itself, and not for-cause / post-accident / random testing of current employees, and it does not require an employer to permit use/possession/impairment at work. Lawful off-duty use — NRS 613.333 as amended by AB 132 §3.5: the existing ban on discriminating for lawful off-duty product use applies to marijuana use — [VERIFY] codified 613.333 text + “does not adversely affect ability/safety” qualifier.

B. Medical-patient workplace protections — NRS 678C.850. Secondary summary states employers must make reasonable accommodations for medical users with valid cards so long as they do not use in the workplace (citing NRS 678C.850(3)). Full 678C.850 text not opened — [VERIFY] accommodation scope, safety-sensitive exceptions, and whether accommodation includes modified duties vs mere non-discrimination. Art. 4 §38(2)(b) expressly does not require workplace accommodation — the statute (if it does so) goes beyond the constitutional floor; do not cite the constitution for an accommodation right. Employer workplace policy preserved: NRS 678D.510(1)(a) (verbatim §1.2D) — adult-use chapter does not prohibit an employer from maintaining/enforcing a workplace policy prohibiting/restricting otherwise-permitted conduct. This and NRS 613.132 / 678C.850 must be read together: 678D.510 preserves policies in general; 613.132 and 678C.850 carve specific hiring/accommodation limits.

C. Cannabis-industry employment (agent cards) — for Part 4 cross-reference only. CCB agent card required per category (production, cultivation, retail, distribution, laboratory, contractor); $150 fee + background check via Dept. of Public Safety; temporary card up to 90 days while background results pend. Source: CCB FAQ. No Nevada residency/US-citizenship requirement for agent cards (same FAQ).

1.10 Pending legislation — NELIS status, biennial sessions

Session note: Nevada Legislature meets in biennial regular sessions in odd years (Nev. Const. Art. 4, §2). The most recent regular session is the 83rd (2025); there is no regular session in 2026 (special sessions possible but none identified for cannabis in Run 1). The next regular session is 2027. Bills that did not pass in 2025 are dead for that session unless carried into a special session or reintroduced in 2027 — they are not “pending” in the ordinary sense. All 2025 statuses below must be re-checked on NELIS (NELIS 83rd (2025) bills) before republication, because LegiScan/secondary trackers were used where NELIS bill pages were not directly opened.
