Part 2 — Taxes

2.1 The three state layers on a retail cannabis sale

LayerRate / baseWho bears it / who pays itMedical treatment
Wholesale excise tax — NRS 372A.290(1)–(2)15% of fair market value at wholesale (affiliate sale) or 15% of sales price (non-affiliate sale), on the first wholesale sale onlyObligation of the cultivation facilityApplies to medical and adult-use cultivation — see §2.2
Retail excise tax — NRS 372A.290(3)10% of sales price, on each retail sale by an adult-use retail store or cannabis consumption loungeObligation of the seller, but may be recovered from the purchaser; separate from and in addition to general sales/use taxExempt — see §2.5
State + local sales/use tax — NRS chs. 372, 374, 377Statewide minimum 6.85% combined; Clark County 8.375%; Washoe County 8.265% — see §2.4Collected from the consumer at saleApplies to medical sales — see §2.5

Sources: NRS 372A.290, opened 2026-10-31; Dept. of Taxation Cannabis Tax Revenue FY27 PDF, NV-Cannabis-Revenue-FY27.pdf, opened 2026-10-31 (headings: “State Wholesale Cannabis Excise Tax (15% Rate – Adult-Use and Medical)” and “State Retail Cannabis Excise Tax (10% Rate – Adult-Use)”).

2.2 Wholesale excise tax — 15%, first sale only, and how taxable value is set

“1. An excise tax is hereby imposed on the first wholesale sale in this State of cannabis by a medical cannabis cultivation facility to another cannabis establishment at the rate of 15 percent of: (a) The fair market value at wholesale of the cannabis, if the sale is made to an affiliate of the medical cannabis cultivation facility; or (b) The sales price of the cannabis, if the sale is made to a cannabis establishment that is not an affiliate of the medical cannabis cultivation facility. … The excise tax imposed pursuant to this subsection is the obligation of the medical cannabis cultivation facility.” — NRS 372A.290(1), opened 2026-10-31. Subsection (2) is the same structure for an adult-use cannabis cultivation facility. History line on the same page: added 2013, amended 2015, 2017, 2019, 2021, 2023.

“Wholesale sale” definition — NRS 372A.290(9)(k): “‘Wholesale sale’ means the first sale or transfer of cannabis by a cannabis cultivation facility to another cannabis establishment. The term does not include a transfer of cannabis by a cannabis cultivation facility to another cannabis cultivation facility when both cannabis cultivation facilities share identical ownership.” “Affiliate” — NRS 372A.290(9)(b): “a person who, directly or indirectly through one or more intermediaries, controls, is controlled by or is under common control with, a specified person.”

Change history — the key change, not a rate change. Before 1 Jan 2024 the wholesale tax was levied on fair market value at wholesale generally. Assembly Bill 430 (2023) revised it so that, from 1 Jan 2024, the tax is 15% on the first wholesale sale, calculated on fair market value for affiliate sales and on the contracted sales price for non-affiliate sales. Sources: Dept. of Taxation, “Fair Market Value at Wholesale of Cannabis” summary (Jan 2026 rates), Fair-Market-Value Summary Jan 2026, accessed 2026-10-31; Dept. of Taxation Cannabis Tax Overview presented 25 Feb 2025 to the Legislature, AREV302E, accessed 2026-10-31. The same Dept. summary states: “Effective January 1, 2024, seeds are no longer subject to the Wholesale Cannabis Tax pursuant to Senate Bill 277.” [VERIFY] the SB 277 seed provision against the enrolled bill text before quoting it as law; it is reported here exactly as the Dept. summary states it. No change to the 15% rate itself was found in current NRS 372A.290. AB 307 (2025) would have eliminated the wholesale tax — it failed 3 June 2025 and is not law (see §2.11). A 2026 interim subcommittee studied replacing the wholesale tax with a 12–15% retail tax but recommended no specific change and took no action, with possible revisiting in the 2027 session — secondary report, MJBizDaily, accessed 2026-10-31. Do not present either proposal as current law.

How fair market value (FMV) is actually calculated. Definition, NAC 372A.115: “‘Fair market value’ means the value established by the Department based on the price that a buyer would pay to a seller in an arm’s length transaction for cannabis in the wholesale market.” Source: NAC ch. 372A, NAC 372A — search extract of that page; the NAC page itself was not fully opened — [VERIFY] wording against the live NAC page. Method, NAC 372A.155 (as amended by LCB File R052-23, eff. 19 Dec 2024): the Dept. calculates FMV at wholesale using reported sales/transfers for each category of cannabis. Practical method stated in the Dept.’s Jan 2026 FMV summary: FMV rates are calculated from cultivator transactions recorded in the CCB’s seed-to-sale system (Metrc) for 1 Sep–30 Nov 2025; for each category the FMV is “the median of the standardized price per pound or per unit, as applicable, after internal transfers, samples/promos, and outliers were removed from the dataset.” The next determination was to be conducted in March 2026, with rates effective 1 April 2026. The Dept. states it levies the wholesale tax on eight product categories, of which the extract names Small Bud, Flower and Trim. [VERIFY / UNAVAILABLE in Run 1] the full eight-category list and the dollar FMV values effective 1 Jan 2026 and 1 Apr 2026: the FMV PDF extract was truncated before the rate table, so no per-pound/per-unit dollar values are reported here. Do not fill them in from secondary calculators. Industry complaint recorded in the interim study (secondary): cultivators argue the FMV formula lags falling prices, causing over-taxation as flower fell from $9.02/g in 2019 to $4.46/g in 2025 — a secondary claim, not a Dept. finding. Filing: wholesale tax is due monthly, by the end of the month following the reporting period, per the Dept.’s Tax Fundamentals guide for cannabis establishments (Jan 2025), Tax Fundamentals Jan 2025, accessed 2026-10-31. Wholesale return form EXC-F069.

2.3 Retail excise tax — 10%, adult-use only

“Except as otherwise provided in subsection 8, an excise tax is hereby imposed on each retail sale in this State of cannabis or cannabis products by an adult-use cannabis retail store or cannabis consumption lounge at the rate of 10 percent of the sales price of the cannabis or cannabis products. The excise tax imposed pursuant to this subsection: (a) Is the obligation of the seller of the cannabis or cannabis product but may be recovered from the purchaser. (b) Is separate from and in addition to any general state and local sales and use taxes that apply to retail sales of tangible personal property.” — NRS 372A.290(3), opened 2026-10-31.

Mechanics confirmed by the Dept.: retail tax is collected from the end-user/consumer at the time of sale, due monthly by the end of the month following the reporting period, must be separately stated on the receipt, and does not apply to medical cannabis sales, to paraphernalia (papers, grinders, water pipes), to other tangible personal property that is not cannabis, or to product below the 0.3% THC threshold (hemp). Source: Tax Fundamentals guide above. The Dept. warns that monthly retail excise tax will not equal exactly 10% of reported “taxable sales,” because taxable sales include adult-use cannabis, medical cannabis, tangible personal property transferred for value, and all other amounts subject to sales/use tax, and because of delinquencies. Source: FY27 revenue PDF footnote, opened 2026-10-31.

2.4 Sales and use tax — state minimum and county totals

  • Statewide minimum combined rate: 6.85%. Composed of a 2% state general-fund rate, a 2.60% Local School Support Tax, and a 2.25% city-county relief component (0.50% Basic + 1.75% Supplemental City-County Relief Tax). Sources: Dept. return form REV-F013 and secondary breakdown corroborating the components — [VERIFY] the component split against NRS 372.105 / ch. 377 text before republication.
  • Clark County (including Las Vegas, Henderson, North Las Vegas, and unincorporated Clark County including the Strip): 8.375% combined — i.e. 6.85% + 1.525% local.
  • Washoe County (including Reno and Sparks): 8.265% combined — i.e. 6.85% + 1.415% local.
  • Other counties, partial official figures only (from REV-F013): Churchill 7.600%, Storey 7.600%, White Pine 7.725%. All other Nevada counties’ combined rates: [UNAVAILABLE in Run 1] — do not assume 6.85% or 7.60% for them; the correct route is the Dept.’s current county rate table / My Nevada Tax lookup by address.
  • Stacking / order of calculation: sales tax is calculated on the subtotal including the 10% retail excise tax (i.e. excise-inclusive). The retail excise statute itself says it is “separate from and in addition to” sales tax (NRS 372A.290(3)(b)). The “on top of excise” ordering is stated in secondary tax guides and is consistent with the Dept.’s “taxable sales” definition, but [VERIFY] the ordering against a Dept. worked example before presenting it as a Dept. rule.
  • Illustrative combined adult-use burden in Clark County (arithmetic from the rates above, not a Dept. example): 10% retail excise + 8.375% sales tax on the excise-inclusive price ≈ 19.2% on top of the pre-tax price, before the embedded 15% wholesale tax already in the wholesale price and before any local gross-revenue licence tax (up to 3%, see §2.8). Secondary sources round this to “roughly 18%” or “around 23% under a restructured model” — those roundings differ because of whether sales tax is applied to the excise-inclusive subtotal; do not quote a single “total tax %” without stating the base. [VERIFY] any published total-tax figure against a Dept. example.
  • No city-level sales tax: Nevada sales-tax add-ons are county-level; cities do not levy a separate sales tax. This is stated in secondary guides and is consistent with NRS ch. 377 (City-County Relief Tax) — [VERIFY] against NRS ch. 377 before asserting it unqualified.

2.5 Medical cannabis tax treatment

  • Retail excise: exempt. NRS 372A.290(8), opened 2026-10-31: “The excise tax imposed pursuant to subsection 3 does not apply to a sale of cannabis for the medical use of cannabis or a medical cannabis product to the holder of a registry identification card or letter of approval by an adult-use cannabis retail store that has been deemed to be a dual licensee pursuant to NRS 678D.490.”
  • Wholesale excise: applies. The 15% wholesale tax in NRS 372A.290(1) (medical cultivation) and (2) (adult-use cultivation) has no medical exemption; the Dept.’s FY27 table heads it “(15% Rate – Adult-Use and Medical)”.
  • Sales tax: applies to medical sales. The Dept.’s Tax Fundamentals guide states sales tax “is due on the sale of all tangible personal property, including Medical Cannabis sales, where the transfer of possession/ownership occurs within the State of Nevada.”
  • History (from legislative testimony, not current law): a 2% medical cannabis tax effective 1 April 2014 (2013 session; 75% to the Distributive School Account, 25% to the Division of Public and Behavioral Health) was eliminated in 2017 and replaced by the 15% wholesale structure. Source: Assembly Committee on Revenue minutes, 25 Feb 2025. Do not present the 2% tax as current.
  • Dual-licensee consequence: because most adult-use stores are dual licensees, the same store must designate each sale as medical (ch. 678C) or adult-use (ch. 678D) for tax purposes. This designation is what drives the federal Schedule III / §280E split in §2.9 as well.

2.6 Revenue distribution — State Education Fund (formerly Distributive School Account), CCB costs, and the $5 million local share

Statute — NRS 372A.290(4)–(7), opened 2026-10-31, verbatim in substance: (4) Wholesale tax from medical cultivation (subsection 1): distributed “(a) To the Cannabis Compliance Board and to local governments in an amount determined to be necessary by the Board to pay the costs of the Board and local governments in carrying out the provisions of chapter 678C of NRS; and (b) If any money remains … to the State Treasurer to be deposited to the credit of the State Education Fund.” (5) Wholesale tax from adult-use cultivation (subsection 2): same structure, for chapter 678D costs, remainder to the State Education Fund. (6) “… a total amount of $5,000,000 of the revenues collected from the excise tax imposed pursuant to subsection 1 and the excise tax imposed pursuant to subsection 2 in each fiscal year shall be deemed sufficient to pay the costs of all local governments to carry out the provisions of chapters 678C and 678D of NRS. The Board shall, by regulation, determine the manner in which local governments may be reimbursed…” (7) Retail tax (subsection 3): “must be paid over as collected to the State Treasurer to be deposited to the credit of the State Education Fund.”

History of the destination. SB 545 (2019) directed all 10% retail excise revenue to the Distributive School Account (DSA); SB 543 (2019) created the Pupil-Centered Funding Plan and directed retail excise plus remaining wholesale proceeds into the new State Education Fund (SEF), effective 2021. “Distributive School Account” and “State Education Fund” are successive names for the school-funding destination — do not treat them as two separate current funds. The Dept. of Education is responsible for distributions from the SEF to school districts (CCB/Taxation joint release, 5 Feb 2026). Sales-tax revenue is not earmarked to the SEF in the same way; it goes through the normal state/local sales-tax distribution. No cannabis-specific sales-tax earmark was found. [VERIFY] if a Part 2 table claims otherwise.

What the $5 million is and is not: it is a statewide total deemed sufficient for all local governments combined per fiscal year, distributed by the CCB by regulation — not $5 million per jurisdiction. The CCB also transfers residual wholesale revenue plus residuals from civil penalties, licensing and other fees to the SEF. Per-jurisdiction breakdown of the $5 million: [UNAVAILABLE in Run 1] — no official per-city/per-county distribution table was located.

Source: FY27 PDF footnote, opened 2026-10-31.

[VERIFY] against Dept. cumulative tables if a cumulative figure is needed. Calendar-year secondary totals ($723,845,835 for 2025; $812,005,882 for 2024, secondary compilation citing DoT) differ from fiscal-year totals because they are calendar-year aggregations — do not present either as the other.

2.8 Local licence taxes and business taxes — the 3% cap

State cap statute — counties: NRS 244.35253. A board of county commissioners may not impose a licence tax on a cannabis establishment inside an incorporated city/town, but may, for establishments outside incorporated city/town limits, impose a licence tax as (a) a flat fee, (b) a percentage of gross revenue, or (c) a combination — but the total must not exceed 3% of gross revenue. It may also collect ch. 278 fees, a one-time application flat fee no higher than a similar business fee, and a licence tax on non-cannabis-licensed business activity at no more than the rate for similar businesses. Source: search extract — [VERIFY] against NRS 244.

State cap statute — cities: NRS 268.0977. Identical structure for incorporated cities — a city governing body may impose a licence tax on a cannabis establishment within its limits as a flat fee, percentage of gross revenue, or combination, capped at 3% of gross revenue in total, plus ch. 278 fees, a one-time application fee capped at the similar-business fee, and a licence tax on non-678B-licensed activity at no more than the similar-business rate. Source: search extract — [VERIFY] against the official NRS page.

Consequence: no Nevada county or city may lawfully impose a cannabis gross-revenue licence tax above 3%. Any local figure above 3% in older guides is either a pre-cap figure, a different fee (application/origination), or an error. Origination/application flat fees are separate from the 3% licence tax and are not capped by that 3% rule — see Part 3.4.

Other business taxes that apply to cannabis businesses generally (not cannabis-specific). Commerce Tax (NRS ch. 363C): due once a year, 45 days after the end of the fiscal year, on Nevada gross revenue; entities with under $4,000,000 Nevada gross revenue in the fiscal year are exempt from filing; tax is imposed only on revenue above $4,000,000; rate depends on NAICS code. Source: Dept. Tax Fundamentals guide, Jan 2025. Modified Business Tax (MBT, NRS ch. 363B for general business; ch. 363A for financial institutions/mining): payroll-based. Secondary sources state the general-business rate as 1.475% on quarterly wages exceeding $50,000 (no tax, but a return still required, at or below $50,000), and 2% with no exemption for financial institutions/mining — [VERIFY] current MBT rates/thresholds against NRS ch. 363B and the Dept.’s MBT page before republication; MBT is not a profit tax, so §280E does not operate on it in the same way as on an income tax (see §2.9). State Business License: Nevada requires a state business licence (Secretary of State) before a sales-tax permit is typically issued; the standard annual fee is commonly cited as $200 for most entities ($100 for certain nonprofits) — [UNAVAILABLE / VERIFY in Run 1] — no official fee figure was opened, so it is not asserted here. Property tax, room tax, etc. apply under general law and are not cannabis-specific; no cannabis-specific property-tax rate was found — [UNAVAILABLE] for any cannabis-specific property-tax claim.

2.9 IRS §280E and Nevada “decoupling” — why the question is largely moot at state level, and what is not moot federally

Verified state position. Nevada Constitution, Article 10, §1(9): “No income tax shall be levied upon the wages or personal income of natural persons. Notwithstanding the foregoing provision … taxes may be levied upon the income or revenue of any business in whatever form it may be conducted for profit in the State.” (Secondary reproduction — [VERIFY] against the official Constitution on leg.state.nv.us.) Nevada has no personal income tax and no traditional corporate income tax. It funds itself through sales/use tax, excise taxes (including cannabis excise), gaming/mining taxes, property tax, the payroll-based MBT and the gross-revenue-based Commerce Tax. Therefore there is no Nevada state income-tax return on which a §280E “decoupling” statute could operate. A secondary blog claim listing Nevada among states that have “decoupled” from §280E is not accepted here: no NRS income-tax decoupling provision was located, and the claim appears to confuse the absence of a state income tax with an affirmative decoupling statute. [VERIFY / CONFLICT — flag for the parent researcher: do not publish “Nevada decoupled from 280E” without locating an NRS provision or a Dept. statement saying so.] The correct formulation is: Nevada levies no state income tax to decouple from; its business taxes (Commerce Tax on gross revenue above $4m, MBT on payroll) are not computed from federal taxable income with §280E disallowances in the way a corporate income tax is. This does not mean Nevada cannabis businesses escape §280E: §280E is a federal provision (IRC §280E) barring deductions for businesses trafficking in Schedule I/II substances. It applies at federal level regardless of Nevada’s tax structure.

Federal split — per the fixed baseline, confirmed by the CCB’s May 2026 update. CCB Licensee Update, 20 May 2026: “Because Schedule III substances are not subject to Section 280E, qualifying medical cannabis activity potentially may become eligible for different federal tax treatment. However, substantial uncertainty remains regarding the treatment of mixed-use operations, allocation of expenses between medical and adult-use activity, retroactive application … and implementation by the IRS…” and “Licensees should consult qualified tax professionals before modifying tax positions…” Source: CCB update, 20 May 2026, accessed 2026-10-31 (search extract of the official CCB PDF). The same update states Nevada law and Board regulations do not change. An earlier joint CCB/Dept. of Taxation statement dated 23 April 2026 (reported in secondary summaries) is said to have confirmed Nevada state tax requirements would not change in response to federal rescheduling and cautioned dual licensees about §280E relief — [VERIFY] the 23 April joint statement text itself; it was not opened directly in Run 1; the 20 May CCB update above is the safer citation. Practical consequence for dual licensees: because a dual licensee’s store sells both medical (potentially Schedule III, §280E-free) and adult-use (Schedule I, fully §280E) product, and because NRS 372A.290(8) already requires each sale to be designated medical vs adult-use, the federal question is allocation — how shared rent, payroll, marketing and other expenses are split between the two streams. The CCB in May 2026 expressly said the IRS had not yet told licensees how to allocate shared expenses. Do not advise a dual licensee that all its expenses are now deductible, or that its adult-use expenses are deductible — both would be wrong on current guidance. Consumption lounges: adult-use lounge sales are retail sales under NRS 372A.290(3) and are adult-use activity; under the fixed baseline they remain Schedule I and fully subject to §280E. No lounge-specific §280E relief was found. [VERIFY] if a lounge claims medical-activity treatment. Tribal operations: whether tribal medical activity counts as “state-licensed” under the federal Schedule III rule is marked [VERIFY] in the commission itself and was not resolved by any source opened in Run 1. Do not assert it either way.

2.10 Hemp product tax

No hemp-specific excise tax in Nevada. The Cannabis Advisory Commission’s Subcommittee on Consumable Hemp Products, report 10 Sep 2026 (submitted under AB 76 (2025) §54.3), states verbatim in substance: “Nevada imposes a 15 percent wholesale excise tax and a 10 percent retail excise tax on cannabis sales, in addition to sales tax and fees imposed by local jurisdictions. Purchasers of hemp products, however, pay only sales tax. There is no hemp-specific excise tax.” Source: CAC Hemp Subcommittee Report, 10 Sep 2026, accessed 2026-10-31. Consequence: hemp/CBD products at retail pay only general sales/use tax at the county rate (e.g. 8.375% Clark, 8.265% Washoe), subject to general sales-tax exemptions (e.g. unprepared food, prescription drugs — applicability to a given hemp product is fact-specific and [VERIFY] per product). The Dept.’s Tax Fundamentals guide confirms the retail cannabis tax does not apply to product “below the 0.3% THC threshold (hemp).” Failed proposal — not law: SB 281 (2021) as proposed would have imposed a 1% retail excise tax on consumable hemp products. Source: 2021 legislative exhibit. It did not become the current hemp tax — do not present a 1% hemp tax as current law. Its final disposition (dead/failed) should be confirmed on NELIS if cited in Part 1. Hemp grower/handler licence fees are Dept. of Agriculture fees, not taxes, and belong in Run 2, Part 7, not here. No hemp acreage tax or production tax was found. [UNAVAILABLE] for any hemp production tax claim. Federal hemp dates (12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026) do not change the Nevada tax rates above as of 31 Oct 2026; whether Nevada enacts a hemp excise or moves hemp-derived THC products into the CCB excise system is a 2027-session question flagged in the CAC hemp subcommittee report. [VERIFY] in the 2027 session.

Part 3 — Local Rules

3.1 What authority counties, cities and Carson City have — the state framework

  1. State licence is necessary but not sufficient — local approval is a second gate. A CCB licence does not authorise operation without local compliance. The Clark County application packet states the sequence plainly: applicants for cannabis stores, cultivation/production and testing facilities “must have a valid license from the State of Nevada Cannabis Compliance Board and approval from the Clark County Department of Comprehensive Planning,” and “No business may be conducted until a Clark County Business License has been approved and issued.” Source: Clark County Cannabis Establishment Checklist and Application Packet, accessed 2026-10-31.
  2. Local governments may ban adult-use establishments outright (zoning/land-use power). NRS 678D.510(1)(d), as amended by AB 341 (2021) §30.3: “The provisions of this chapter do not prohibit: … (d) A local government from adopting and enforcing local cannabis control measures pertaining to zoning and land use for adult-use cannabis establishments, including, without limitation, a measure which prohibits the operation of adult-use cannabis establishments.” Source: search extracts — [VERIFY] against NRS 678D. Subsection (2) preserves ch. 678C (medical) — i.e. the adult-use ban power does not by its terms rewrite the medical programme, which is why several jurisdictions’ histories distinguish medical bans from adult-use bans.
  3. Local governments may impose a gross-revenue licence tax up to 3% — and no more. See Part 2.8: NRS 244.35253 (counties, outside incorporated limits) and NRS 268.0977 (cities, inside limits). A county may not impose that licence tax on an establishment inside an incorporated city.
  4. Local governments may not be more restrictive than state law on certain subjects. NRS 244.35253(5) (counties) provides a county “shall not enact or enforce any ordinance which is more restrictive than or conflicts with a law or regulation of this State relating to” — among other things — “the packaging, labeling, testing, dosage or potency of marijuana, edible marijuana products, marijuana products or marijuana-infused products.” The full list of pre-empted subjects was truncated in the extract — [VERIFY] the complete subsection before quoting it.
  5. State siting buffers are a floor, not a ceiling — localities may require more. State buffers (NRS 678B.210 for medical, NRS 678B.250 for adult-use — see §3.2) are conditions of the state licence application. Local zoning (special-use permits, conditional-use permits, distance-separation analyses) can add spacing, zoning-district, hours, and operating conditions. Example: Henderson’s local rule extends the state 300-foot community-facility buffer to 1,000 feet (reported in 2014 coverage of Henderson’s proposed ordinance — see §3.3, Henderson).
  6. Local lounge approval is a separate opt-in/opt-out gate. For consumption lounges specifically, the CCB published an official opt-out list (see §3.3, “Lounge opt-outs”). A jurisdiction on that list “will not appear as an option on the CCB’s consumption lounge license application.” The CCB expressly warns: “The CCB makes no representations that a local jurisdiction selected by the applicant will allow consumption lounges or if they will implement stricter regulations than what the CCB has promulgated. The applicant should check with the local jurisdiction…” Source: CCB, “Local Jurisdictions Opted-Out of Cannabis Consumption Lounge Licenses,” 14 Oct 2022, Opted-Out PDF, accessed 2026-10-31. Critical distinction: being on the lounge opt-out list does not mean the jurisdiction bans cannabis establishments generally. North Las Vegas, Henderson, Sparks, Carson City, Boulder City, Mesquite, Elko, Douglas County and Lyon County are all on the lounge opt-out list but have different establishment histories (see table in §3.3). Do not conflate the two.
  7. Carson City’s dual role. Carson City is a consolidated municipality: its Board of Supervisors exercises both city and county functions, and there is no separate Carson County. Its cannabis rules therefore appear in the Carson City Municipal Code (Titles 4 and 18) rather than in a county code.
  8. Unincorporated towns. In unincorporated Clark County, the “towns” of Paradise, Spring Valley, Enterprise, Winchester, etc. are governed by the Clark County Commission, not by the City of Las Vegas. A business address on the Strip (e.g. Las Vegas Boulevard South in Paradise) is a Clark County unincorporated address for licensing, zoning and sales-tax purposes, even though its mailing address says “Las Vegas, NV.”

3.2 State caps, buffers and zoning floors that bind every locality

These are state rules, included here because every local rule sits on top of them: school buffer: 1,000 feet from a public or private school providing preschool–grade 12 education that existed on the application date; community-facility buffer: 300 feet from a community facility that existed on the application date; gaming buffer (counties ≥100,000 population — currently Clark and Washoe): 1,500 feet from an establishment holding a nonrestricted gaming licence under NRS 463.0177(1) or (2) that existed on the application date; airport property: prohibited — an establishment may not be located on airport property. Source: NRS 678B.250(3)(a)(2)(II) (adult-use) and NRS 678B.210(3)(a)(2)(II) (medical), via search extract — [VERIFY] measurement method — front door to closest lot-line point — against the current NCCR text, as secondary guides state that method but the statute extract does not spell it out. Retail licence caps by county population (NRS 678B.260, as reported in secondary summaries): 80 adult-use retail licences in counties with 700,000+ population; 20 in counties 100,000–699,999; 4 in counties 55,000–99,999; 2 in smaller counties. [VERIFY] against NRS 678B.260 text on leg.state.nv.us before publishing the cap table — the cap provision was not opened directly in the Part 3 pass, and later amendments (including the dual-licence consolidation) may affect how it is counted. Local caps (e.g. Carson City’s 4-outlet cap, Mesquite’s population-based dispensary cap) are separate from the state caps and are listed in §3.3.

3.3 Jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction table — what was checked, what was found, what is still Verify

There is no single official statewide list of jurisdictions that allow or ban cannabis establishments. That negative finding is itself a result: the only official statewide list located in Run 1 is the CCB’s lounge-only opt-out list of 14 Oct 2022 (see below). Everything else must be checked jurisdiction by jurisdiction in its own code.

Official lounge opt-out list (CCB, 14 Oct 2022) — lounges only: Boulder City; Carson City; City of Elko; City of Fernley; City of Henderson; City of Mesquite; City of North Las Vegas; City of Sparks; City of Yerington; Douglas County; Eureka County; Lander County; Lincoln County; Lyon County; Mineral County; Pershing County; City of Winnemucca. Source: CCB Opted-Out PDF, accessed 2026-10-31. Consequence: as of that list, the jurisdictions in southern Nevada that did not opt out of lounges were the City of Las Vegas and unincorporated Clark County — corroborated by contemporary reporting that “Las Vegas and unincorporated Clark County will be the only jurisdictions in the southern half of the state to allow the lounges, with North Las Vegas, Henderson, Boulder City and Mesquite all opting out” (Marijuana Venture, Sept 2022, secondary). [VERIFY] whether any jurisdiction has since opted back in or out — the CCB list is dated October 2022 and no updated list was located in Run 1.

3.4 Local licence fees and local tax revenue — where officially reported

General rule (see §2.8): local licence tax capped at 3% of gross revenue (NRS 244.35253 counties; NRS 268.0977 cities). Within that cap, jurisdictions choose flat, percentage, or combined structures, plus separate application/origination fees and ch. 278 (planning/zoning) fees.

Clark County (unincorporated) — official County documents. Application fee for a cannabis establishment: $1,500; cannabis distribution: $250; additional line of service: $500; change of location: $1,500 (+ $500 per additional identical change). Quarterly licence fee (County Code 8.60/8.65, per County renewal guidance letter 20 Oct 2022): for each establishment other than testing laboratories and distributors — 1% of gross revenue up to $150,000 per calendar quarter, plus 2% of gross revenue above $150,000, plus 3% of gross revenue above $400,000 per quarter (tiered, not a flat 3% on all revenue); plus $250 per quarter if the establishment transfers/delivers to a commonly owned establishment with no sales transaction. Each distributor: $250 per quarter. Each independent testing laboratory: semiannual fee per County Code §6.12.995 schedule based on gross revenue. Gross revenue excludes state sales/excise tax paid and (per the letter) transfers to other establishments — full definition truncated in extract, [VERIFY] against current County Code 8.60.090 / 8.65.090. Renewal cadence: quarterly or semiannually as prescribed in County Code.

City of Las Vegas — official City fee list (partial) + historical fees. City Business License Category List (5 Aug 2026) — search extract, truncated: shows cannabis categories including Cannabis Cultivation Facility (recreational) at 3% of gross revenue (with a $20,000-for-first-5,000-sq-ft + $10,000-per-additional-5,000-sq-ft formulation in the same table — the extract garbles which figure belongs to which category), Cannabis Production Facility (outside city) at 1% of gross revenue or $1,500, whichever is greater, not to exceed 3%, and a $100 processing fee. Because the extract is garbled, no City of Las Vegas fee is asserted here as a clean current figure — [VERIFY] against the full 5 Aug 2026 category list before publishing any Las Vegas fee. City checklist for production/cultivation establishments outside city limits (secondary mirror of a City PDF): initial processing fee $100 (non-refundable); initial licence fee production $1,500, cultivation $5,000 (prorated); semiannual renewal production 1% of gross revenue/sales or $1,500 whichever is greater (not exceeding 3%), cultivation 1% or $5,000 whichever is greater (not exceeding 3%); waiver $100 per person; temporary request $500 — [VERIFY] against the live City PDF. Historical (2014 Council action, secondary report): dispensary annual fee $75,000; laboratory $10,000/year; edibles manufacturer $25,000; cultivator $20,000 for first 5,000 sq ft + $10,000 per additional 5,000 sq ft; gross-revenue tax then raised from 4% to 5% (cultivators) and 6% to 7% (edibles/dispensaries) — these pre-date the 3% state cap structure and must not be quoted as current. Cannabis Support Business (Retail/Medical) — City instruction sheet: $150 at application ($100 licence fee + $50 processing fee); licence fees based on gross revenue, due semiannually in advance; $50 one-time Home Occupation Permit if home-based in city limits. Applies where ≥50% of annual revenue comes from licensed medical establishments.

Henderson, North Las Vegas, Reno, Washoe County, Carson City, Nye/Elko, Mineral, Storey: see the jurisdiction table in §3.3 for each figure and its date/status. In brief: Henderson CUP $11,022 + notification (2025 schedule) and historical 3% semiannual fees + $60,000–$100,000 origination; North Las Vegas historical $60,000 origination / 3% (2% Apex cultivation); Reno FY26/27 medical 3.0% per quarter (min. $70); Washoe County quarterly 3% licence fee with a dedicated Marijuana Establishment Fund (2018); Carson City 3% of gross revenues (2017 ordinance) replacing earlier flat fees; Nye/Elko steep initial fees $20,000–$37,000+ plus 3% (CAC report, Sept 2026); Mineral County historical flat fees + 2% monthly fee; Storey County fee reduced from 3% to 0% (Ord. 26-336, Mar 2026).

Local tax revenue — what is officially reported and what is not. Official statewide local share: $5,000,000 per fiscal year in total to all local governments from wholesale excise tax (NRS 372A.290(6)). FY2025: the joint release confirms the $5m local distribution as part of the wholesale accounting. Per-jurisdiction local cannabis revenue (licence taxes, fees): [UNAVAILABLE in Run 1]. No official table breaking down local licence-tax or fee revenue by city/county was located. The CAC Taxation Subcommittee report (Sept 2026) explicitly finds county-by-county inconsistency — “For the counties that permit cannabis commerce, the fee structures differ greatly” — and gives the Clark tiered, Nye/Elko steep-fee, and Washoe/Reno flat-fee patterns summarised above, but it does not publish a per-jurisdiction revenue table. Do not estimate local revenue by applying 3% to county taxable sales — licence-tax gross revenue is not the same base as Dept. “taxable sales,” and several jurisdictions use tiered/flat structures. City/county general-fund cannabis revenue beyond the 3% licence tax: cannabis sales also generate ordinary sales-tax distributions to the jurisdiction of sale under NRS chs. 372/377, but a cannabis-attributable sales-tax figure by jurisdiction was not located — [UNAVAILABLE].

3.5 Business concentration — only what official sources say

No independent ranking or clustering analysis is offered here, per instructions. The following are official-source statements only: (1) County taxable-sales concentration (official, CCB + Dept. of Taxation joint release, FY2025): Clark County $567,626,861; Washoe County $105,764,452; all other counties combined $84,323,598; statewide $757,714,911. In other words, the official release itself shows Clark County accounting for roughly three-quarters of statewide taxable sales in FY2025 (the secondary calculation $567.6m ÷ $757.7m ≈ 75% is arithmetic on official figures, not a new estimate). (2) FY2027 year-to-date county pattern (official, Dept. PDF, Jul–Aug 2026): Clark $78,202,568 of $104,482,238 statewide taxable sales; Washoe $11,227,361; all other $15,052,310. (3) CCB licensee list organisation (official): the CCB’s List of Licensees groups retail stores as “Southern Nevada Retail Stores” and (by implication in the full list) northern stores, and lists lounge licensees by address. The list was not tallied by the researcher in Run 1, so no store count by city/county is asserted from it. (4) Secondary (not official) concentration figures — flagged, not adopted: MJBizDaily (10 Feb 2026) reporting state data: “356 operational licenses statewide [AUDIT: removed as an official count — see above], including one medical cannabis dispensary and 107 retail cannabis stores and two cannabis consumption lounges in Las Vegas” — treat as secondary; verify against the CCB’s current licensee list / industry statistics before using the 107-store or 2-lounge figures. (5) No official “cluster” or “tourist-corridor” concentration ranking was located. The Dept. and CCB publish county-level sales, not neighbourhood-level or Strip-vs-off-Strip splits. Any statement such as “most stores are on/near the Strip” would be researcher inference from addresses on the CCB list and is not made here.

Part 4 — Licence Types

4.0 Framework, terminology, and the SB 277 dual-licence change

Regulator: the CCB assumed oversight on 1 July 2020, from the Dept. of Taxation. Source: CCB FAQ, accessed 2026-10-31. Statute: NRS Title 56, Chapters 678A–678D; licensing is principally NRS Chapter 678B. Official chapter text (Rev. 4/15/2026 — 2025): NRS 678B, accessed 2026-10-31.

How many types? — reconcile the counts, do not merge them silently. CCB Industry page lists five core headings plus Consumption Lounge, and describes cultivation, distributor, product manufacturing, testing, retail and consumption lounge. Source: CCB Industry page (updated 2026-10-26), accessed 2026-10-31. CCB FAQ states: “There are six cannabis establishment license types in Nevada” — (1) Cannabis Sales Facility, (2) Cannabis Cultivation Facility, (3) Cannabis Production Facility, (4) Cannabis Distributor, (5) Cannabis Consumption Lounge, (6) Independent Testing Laboratory. In practice there are 7 licence categories, because the consumption lounge splits into two: retail (attached) and independent (standalone). Both lounge types appear separately in NRS 678B.390 fees and in NRS 678B.322–678B.328.

Medical vs adult-use — SB 277 (2023) dual licensing. SB 277 deems each adult-use cannabis establishment to be a dual licensee, authorised to engage in medical-use activity to the same extent as if it held a medical licence of the same type. Source: SB 277 enrolled text, SB277_EN, accessed 2026-10-31; CCB Biennial Report 2025, Biennial Report 2025, accessed 2026-10-31. NRS 678B.215 prohibits issuing or renewing a medical cannabis establishment licence on or after 1 Jan 2024 except for an establishment in a “covered jurisdiction” — i.e. a local jurisdiction that prohibits adult-use establishments. CCB guidance (secondary summary of a CCB notice, 14 Feb 2025) states the medical-to-adult-use conversion was completed 31 Dec 2024 and all dual licences are now Adult-Use Cannabis Establishment Licences with authority to sell both medical and adult-use cannabis. That CCB notice itself was not opened directly in Run 1 — treat the completion date as [VERIFY] against the CCB original, but the statutory mechanism (NRS 678B.215) is official. Consequence: fees and scopes below are given for the adult-use licence (which now carries dual authority) and, where NRS 678B.390 still lists a separate medical fee, for the residual medical licence in a covered jurisdiction. Do not present the old parallel medical + adult-use fee tables as current. Older secondary tables showing cultivation $30,000 initial / $10,000 renewal and retail $6,600 renewal are the pre-SB 277 maxima; the current CCB FAQ and current NRS 678B.390 (as amended 2023) show $3,000 / $1,000 for cultivation and $5,000 retail renewal — see §4.9.

Licence is a revocable privilege, not a vested right: NRS 678B.010. Delivery is NOT a separate establishment licence: only licensed dispensaries/retail stores may request vehicle inspections for customer delivery, and only they may request Board approval for third-party delivery (contract + vehicle inspection required). Source: CCB FAQ. Agent cards are NOT establishment licences, but are required to work: cards exist for production, cultivation, dispensary/retail store, distribution, laboratory, and contractor (verifiable contract company); $150 per card plus background check (Dept. of Public Safety fees). Applications are online only via Accela: Accela NVCCB portal. No “cannabis sales” licence distinct from retail: in Nevada terminology the retail licence is the “cannabis sales facility” / “adult-use cannabis retail store” / “dispensary” (medical-era name). There is no separate state “sales” licence beyond retail.

4.1 Cannabis Cultivation Facility

What it allows (CCB FAQ, verbatim substance): acquires, possesses, cultivates, delivers, transfers, transports, supplies, or sells cannabis and related supplies to a cannabis sales facility, cannabis production facilities, or other cannabis cultivation facilities. Not to consumers. CCB Industry wording: licensed to cultivate (grow), harvest, and package cannabis; to have cannabis tested by a testing facility; and to sell to retail stores, product manufacturing facilities, and other cultivation facilities, but not to consumers. 2025 expansion — seeds and usable cannabis: after SB 168 (2025), licensed cultivation facilities may sell cannabis seeds to any person within or outside Nevada so long as the sale complies with Nevada law, federal law, and the buyer jurisdiction’s law; a new Metrc transfer type “External – Seed Sale” was implemented, and the facility must verify the receiving state permits the sale. Source: CCB FAQ. [VERIFY] the SB 168 enrolled text on NELIS before quoting it in the final dossier — the FAQ statement is the source used here. Caps — state: no statewide numeric cap on cultivation licences in NRS 678B.260 (that cap is retail-only). [VERIFY]: no cultivation cap was located in NRS 678B.220/260 in Run 1; absence of a cap in those sections is not proof no other cap exists in regulation. Caps — local: local approval is required before final approval; local jurisdictions may limit numbers by ordinance (CCB FAQ). Fees — current (adult-use, dual): application $5,000 one-time non-refundable (NRS 678B.390(3)(a)); initial licence maximum $3,000; renewal $1,000. Fees — residual medical: initial $3,000; renewal $1,000 (same figures). Official info pages: CCB Industry and CCB FAQ. No standalone CCB “cultivation licence” page with a distinct URL was located in Run 1 [VERIFY] — the Industry/FAQ pages are the CCB’s published licence information.

4.2 Cannabis Production Facility (Product Manufacturing Facility)

What it allows (CCB FAQ): acquires, possesses, manufactures, delivers, transfers, transports, supplies, or sells cannabis products to cannabis sales facilities, and acquires, possesses, packages, supplies or sells usable cannabis to adult-use cannabis sales facilities. CCB Industry wording: acquire, possess, manufacture, deliver, transfer, supply or sell adult-use cannabis products to adult-use retail stores and/or to other production manufacturing facilities, but not to consumers; products include edibles, ointments, tinctures. 2025 expansion: after SB 168 (2025), the definition of licensed cannabis manufacturing facility was updated to include the ability for production facilities to transfer, hold, produce, and sell usable cannabis to a cannabis sales facility. Source: CCB FAQ. [VERIFY] SB 168 text on NELIS. Caps — state: no retail-style numeric cap located in NRS 678B.260 [VERIFY] as in §4.1. Fees — current (adult-use, dual): application $5,000; initial maximum $3,000; renewal $1,000. Fees — residual medical: initial $3,000; renewal $1,000. Official info pages as in §4.1.

4.3 Cannabis Sales Facility / Retail Store / Medical Dispensary

What it allows (CCB FAQ): acquires, possesses, delivers, transfers, supplies, sells, or dispenses cannabis or related supplies to a consumer/patient or to another cannabis sales facility. CCB Industry wording: purchase cannabis from cultivation facilities, cannabis and cannabis products from product manufacturing facilities, and cannabis from other retail stores; sell to consumers. Retail is the only consumer-facing licence: “Consumers may only legally buy cannabis from a state-licensed retail store.” Medical dispensary — residual status: no new medical-only dispensary licences from 1 Jan 2024 except in a covered jurisdiction (NRS 678B.215, see §4.0). The one standalone medical licence in the Dec 2024 snapshot was in the City of Fallon (CCB Biennial Report 2025). The current CCB List of Licensees (updated 2026-10-26) still shows one “Medical Only” retail entry — Green Cross Farmacy, 510 W Williams Ave (Fallon area, northern list) — consistent with that snapshot. Source: CCB List of Licensees, accessed 2026-10-31.

Caps — adult-use retail, NRS 678B.260 (per county): county population 700,000 or more: 80 licences; 100,000 to <700,000: 20 licences; 55,000 to <100,000: 4 licences; <55,000: 2 licences. Reallocation: if no applicants qualify in a county, the Board must within 2 months after the application period reallocate that county’s licences to other counties in the same proportions. Source: NRS 678B.260 (mirror of NRS text; official chapter at NRS 678B), accessed 2026-10-31. Caps — medical dispensary, NRS 678B.220 (parallel, now largely historic): 40 in counties ≥700,000; 10 in counties 100,000–<700,000; and lower tiers for smaller counties — the full tier table was not re-opened line-by-line in Run 1, so the small-county medical tiers are [VERIFY] against NRS 678B.220. A Carson City staff report (2022) confirms the 55,000–<100,000 tier as 2 medical dispensaries and 4 adult-use retail stores. Caps — local: in counties ≥100,000, the Board must ensure not more than 25% of the county’s medical dispensaries (NRS 678B.230, historic) are in any one local jurisdiction, subject to county-commission request to increase — adult-use analogue provisions are in NRS 678B.260/270 (secondary mirror; verify against official NRS chapter). Fees — current (adult-use, dual): application $5,000; initial maximum $20,000; renewal $5,000. Fees — residual medical dispensary: initial $30,000; renewal $5,000. Official info pages: CCB Industry; CCB List of Licensees (retail storefront list).

4.4 Cannabis Distributor (Distribution Facility)

What it allows (CCB FAQ): transport cannabis and cannabis products from cannabis establishments to another cannabis establishment. CCB Industry wording: transport cannabis from a cannabis establishment to another cannabis establishment, e.g. cultivation facility to retail store. When a distributor is mandatory: a licensed distributor is required to transport cannabis to a retail cannabis store; distributor vehicles must carry an approved Inspection Card. A cultivation facility may transport to a production facility without a distributor, but vehicles must still be inspected and carry an approved Inspection Card. Delivery to a dual-licensed retail establishment without a distributor can only be sold as medical cannabis to patient cardholders. Source: CCB FAQ. Caps: no numeric distributor cap located in NRS 678B.260 [VERIFY]. Fees — current (adult-use only in NRS 678B.390; no separate medical distributor fee is listed in that section): application $5,000; initial maximum $15,000; renewal $5,000. Official info pages as in §4.1.

4.5 Independent Testing Laboratory

What it allows (CCB FAQ): test cannabis and all cannabis products. CCB Industry wording: test cannabis and cannabis products, including for potency and contaminants. Independence: secondary sources state labs cannot hold any other establishment licence type. That independence rule is in NRS 678B.290 (Independent testing laboratories; regulations) — the operative text was not re-opened in Run 1, so independence is [VERIFY] against NRS 678B.290. Caps: no numeric lab cap located [VERIFY]. Fees — current: application $5,000; initial maximum $5,000; renewal $3,000 (both adult-use and residual medical tables agree). Official info pages as in §4.1. A standalone CCB list of licensed labs was not located as a separate page in Run 1; labs appear in the License Search (Part 5) [VERIFY].

4.6 Retail Cannabis Consumption Lounge

Definition: must be “attached or immediately adjacent to an adult-use cannabis-retail store” (NRS 678A.237, as cited in CCB FAQ). What it allows (CCB FAQ): sell single-use cannabis and/or ready-to-consume cannabis products to a consumer/patient that are then consumed in the lounge. Eligibility: only the owner of a licensed operational cannabis sales facility may apply. Cap: no initial numeric cap stated for retail lounges (the 20-licence cap is independent lounges only — see §4.7). Local jurisdictions may set limits/moratoriums. Fees: application $100,000 one-time non-refundable; initial maximum $10,000; renewal $10,000. No social-equity reduction is stated for retail lounges (the 75% reduction is for independent-lounge social-equity applicants and, by regulation, independent-lounge issuance/renewal — NRS 678B.390(2)). Other lounge rules (CCB FAQ): no alcohol sold/consumed in a lounge; consumers may not take home unfinished cannabis/products.

4.7 Independent Cannabis Consumption Lounge

Definition: not attached or immediately adjacent to an adult-use cannabis sales facility (NRS 678A.157, as cited in CCB FAQ). What it allows: same lounge activity as §4.6 (sell single-use / ready-to-consume products for on-site consumption), but standalone. It must enter into a contract with one or more adult-use cannabis retail stores to sell to the independent lounge (i.e. it does not itself hold a retail store licence for that supply). Eligibility / holding bar: any person may apply, but one person may not hold both an adult-use retail-store licence and an independent consumption-lounge licence (NRS 678B.325). Cap — initial: “Initially, the CCB shall not issue more than 20 licenses for an independent cannabis consumption lounge” (NRS 678B.326). Whether the 20-cap still binds after the initial tranche, and the exception in NRS 678B.326, are [VERIFY] against the full NRS 678B.326 text. One-person / transfer bar: NRS 678B.328 prohibits issuance of multiple [independent lounge] licences to one person and governs transfer. Fees: application $10,000 one-time non-refundable; initial maximum $10,000; renewal $10,000. Social equity — set-aside: at least 50% of initial independent licences issued (10 of the first 20) must go to social-equity applicants (NRS 678B.326; CCB Industry page cites AB 341 §12.4(3) for the same 10-of-20 rule). Social equity — who qualifies (NCCR 5.055, as summarised in CCB FAQ): (1) at least 51% ownership in the business; (2) resided for ≥5 years in a census tract nationwide meeting both: (a) Area Deprivation Index score of 7 (state-only decile) or higher, and (b) incarceration rate in the 90th percentile (roughly ≥3.3% of those who grew up in the tract were in jail/incarcerated at the 2010 Decennial Census); (3) convicted of a non-violent felony and/or misdemeanour cannabis offence, or an immediate family member (parent, sibling, child) convicted of a felony cannabis offence who lived in / lives in a Board-designated census tract. Persons convicted of an excluded felony offence (NRS 678B.050) are not eligible. Social equity — fee relief: administrative processing fee reduced by 75% — i.e. $2,500 instead of $10,000 for a qualifying independent-lounge application. NRS 678B.390(2) caps any such reduction at 75% and also permits reduced initial/renewal fees for independent lounges by regulation (the reduced renewal amount, if any, is [VERIFY] — CCB FAQ gives only the $2,500 application figure). Lounge licensing process (two-stage): applicant must meet minimum scoring guidelines in the initial application and be selected by random number selector for a prospective licence; applications are scored for timely submission and completeness. For a conditional licence, the holder must be found suitable by the Board after a suitability investigation by Board Agents. Lounge application window mechanics (CCB FAQ, historic wording — do not present as a currently open window): CCB will give formal public notice 30 days ahead of accepting lounge applications; the initial application period was 10 business days; applicants must first file for a Nevada state business licence via the Secretary of State and submit a lounge agent-registration-card application.

4.8 Any other licence types?

No other establishment licence type was found in CCB’s six-type list or in NRS 678B.390’s fee schedule. The following are not separate establishment licences: delivery (retail-store activity, see §4.0), hemp grower/handler/producer registrations (NDA, NRS Chapter 557 — covered in Run 2, Part 7, and noted in Part 5 below), tribal licences under SB 375 compacts (tribal-authority licences, not CCB licences — see Part 5, tribal row), and agent registration cards (individual, see §4.0). Research / university licences: none identified as a distinct CCB licence type in Run 1 [VERIFY] — to be covered in Run 2, Part 11.9. Cannabis support businesses (local category): Clark County’s business-licence category includes “Cannabis Support Businesses” alongside establishments, but that is a local business-licence category, not a CCB establishment licence. Source: Clark County business detail page, accessed 2026-10-31.

Residual medical (covered jurisdiction only): dispensary $30,000 / $5,000; cultivation $3,000 / $1,000; production $3,000 / $1,000; laboratory $5,000 / $3,000; no medical distributor fee listed in NRS 678B.390. Plus actual background-check costs (NRS 678B.390(3)(d), (4)) and Board investigation costs for transfers, initial issuance, management agreements and waivers (NRS 678B.390(5)–(6)), capped at a reasonable hourly rate + travel/per diem. The Board may not charge any fee not expressly authorised (NRS 678B.390(8)). Sources: NRS 678B.390; CCB FAQ — both accessed 2026-10-31. Warning on secondary fee tables: any table still showing cultivation $30,000/$10,000, production $10,000/$3,300, retail renewal $6,600, or lab $15,000/$5,000 is pre-SB 277 and must not be used. Those figures conflict with the current official sources above and are superseded.

4.10 General requirements, ownership, transfer, renewal, relocation

General adult-use application contents (NRS 678B.250, summarised from official chapter + mirror): legal name; physical address of the proposed establishment and of co-owned/associated establishments; location bars — not on airport property, not within 1,000 feet of a public/private school (preschool–grade 12) existing on the application date, not within 300 feet of a community facility existing on the application date, and, in a county ≥100,000, not within 1,500 feet of an establishment holding a nonrestricted gaming licence (NRS 463.0177(1)–(2)); evidence of liquid assets in an amount the Board judges sufficient for opening and compliance; fingerprints/background; operating procedures. Licence expires 1 year after issuance; renewal on submission of Board-required information + renewal fee. [VERIFY] the 1,500-foot gaming buffer and airport bar line-by-line against the official NRS 678B.250 text before final publication. Qualification / disqualification: Board determines qualification under NRS 678B.200; excluded felony offences (NRS 678B.050) disqualify, subject to the SB 277 petition-for-exemption process (NRS 678B.633). No Nevada residency or US-citizenship requirement for a licence or agent card (CCB FAQ: “No”). Business-entity and state business licence: lounge applicants must file for a Nevada state business licence through the Secretary of State (SilverFlume — see Part 5). The same state-business-licence prerequisite applies generally to operating a business in Nevada (NRS Chapter 76, cited in Clark County guidance).

Local approval is a condition, not an afterthought: “Licenses are conditional until local approval has been obtained. Conditional license holders are granted 12 months to obtain required approvals and a final inspection by the Board. Extensions are permitted in certain circumstances.” All establishments are expected to become operational within 12 months after the conditional licence is issued; if a timely renewal is filed and significant progress continues, the Board may take no action by the deadline; if there is no effort, unresponsiveness, or misleading renewal information, the Board may investigate and potentially revoke the conditional licence. Source: CCB FAQ. Statutory basis: NRS 678B.320 (licences deemed conditional pending local compliance + Board inspection).

Renewal mechanics: all licence types, regardless of status, must submit an annual renewal application through Accela and pay the renewal fee; renewal opens 60 days before expiration; final licences expire annually on the issuance anniversary; lounge conditional licensees need not file an annual renewal until their initial active year nears expiration. Renewal forms may not be faxed/emailed — Accela only. Source: CCB FAQ.

Ownership / monopoly cap (adult-use, counties ≥100,000 — currently Clark and Washoe): to prevent monopolistic practices, the Board must ensure it does not issue to any one person, group or entity the greater of (a) one adult-use licence, or (b) more than 10% of the adult-use licences otherwise allocable in the county. Exception: the cap does not apply to retail or independent consumption-lounge licences (NRS 678B.270(2)). Source: NRS 678B.270, accessed 2026-10-31; CCB monopoly guidance (14 Feb 2025, secondary copy) confirms Clark County 331 adult-use licences and Washoe County 79 adult-use licences as of 1 Jan 2025 for this calculation — [VERIFY] against CCB original; the 331/79 figures are a 1 Jan 2025 snapshot, not a current count.

Ownership registration: owners with ≥5% ownership must register with the Board (NRS 678B.350); agents must register (NRS 678B.340); receivers must register (NRS 678B.355). Transfer: “Transfers of interest are permitted, however, they must be approved by the Board prior to the transfer. Transfers of Interest require extensive review and there is no timeline for completing a transfer of interest.” CCB publishes a Notice of Transfer of Interest Packet (linked from FAQ; packet URL was not separately verified in Run 1). Statute: NRS 678B.380 — licence and registration card nontransferable; Board must adopt regulations governing transferability of licences, cards and ownership interests. Relocation: only within the same local jurisdiction as the original location; local approval first, then a Change of Location Request through Accela with documentation of local approval. Statute: NRS 678B.500(2) (move allowed regardless of distance if within the same local government and approved at a public hearing with ≥7 working days’ written notice). What is public: “Only ownership names and final disciplinary action is public information. Contact information is confidential.” Source: CCB FAQ. This limits every Part 5 source below. Amendments: holders’ licences are issued on operational criteria (location, ownership, equipment); changes (facility modifications, R&D requests, retest requests, menu changes, change of location) require a Licence Amendment Request via Accela.

4.11 How licences were awarded — including the 2018 retail competition and litigation

Medical-era awards (pre-2017): not detailed in sources opened in Run 1 — [UNAVAILABLE in this research pass] — the parent should commission the medical-allocation history separately if needed for Part 9.

2018 adult-use retail competition. On 7 Sep 2018 the Dept. of Taxation began soliciting applications for 64 recreational retail stores statewide, 7 allocated to Washoe County; applications were ranked on factors including owners’ prior business experience, educational achievements, experience operating a medical establishment, and grow plan. Source: US District Court order summarising the complaint, accessed 2026-10-31. 462 applications were scored and 61 licences awarded to 16 companies (contemporaneous reporting); plaintiffs in at least seven lawsuits challenged transparency, use of temporary workers to screen applicants, and alleged bias, and sought to freeze issuance (secondary — contemporaneous news, not a court record). On 23 Aug 2019 Clark County District Judge Elizabeth Gonzalez issued a temporary injunction stopping issuance, finding the Dept. of Taxation acted beyond its legal authority, arbitrarily replaced mandatory requirements, removed a necessary background check for some applicants, changed location/building-code requirements, improperly trained temporary scorers, and allowed personal relationships to affect scoring (secondary — verify against the injunction itself). Consolidated state litigation: In re DOT Litigation, Case No. A-19-787004-B (consolidated with A-785818, A-786357, A-786962, A-787035, A-787540, A-787726, A-801416), Eighth Judicial District Court, Dept. XI. TGIG plaintiffs objected in August 2020 to CCB administrative approval of a partial settlement, alleging (among other things) more than one conditional licence per jurisdiction to favoured applicants, undisclosed information advantages, and licences to applicants known to have sold to minors (a filed objection, i.e. allegations, not findings — CCB public comments, 7 Aug 2020). Outcome as reported by a winning licensee’s counsel: after a six-week trial, the court ruled in favour of the State and the licence winners, finding the 2018 application and grading process valid, but found one regulation on background checks unlawful — [VERIFY] (counsel’s summary, not the judgment). The federal parallel case (High Sierra Holistics v. Dept. of Taxation, No. 3:19-cv-00270) was stayed 30 June 2020 pending the state consolidated case. Bottom line: the 2018 round was a scored competitive process run by the Dept. of Taxation (not the CCB, which did not exist until 2020), it was litigated for roughly two years, and the licences ultimately stood. The exact final judgment citation and settlement terms are [VERIFY] — they were not opened in Run 1.

Lounge awards: two-stage scored + random-selector process, prospective → conditional (suitability) → final (see §4.7). No scored competition for cultivation/production/distribution/testing comparable to 2018 retail was located; those categories have historically been applied for in Board-noticed windows [VERIFY] — see §4.12. White Pine County round (CCB’s second-ever licensing round): the Biennial Report states the CCB “made history by completing the second ever licensing round in agency history for White Pine County. One cultivation and one production license were awarded.” Date within the Dec 2024 report period; the application window dates and scoring method are [VERIFY].

4.12 Are applications open now?

General position — no general open window was found on CCB’s site in Run 1. CCB maintains a public mailing list to notify interested parties of open application periods and meetings (subscribe via the CCB website header). Source: CCB FAQ. Retail: secondary sources state the last retail application window opened in 2018 and that the state is not currently accepting retail-store applications, with a 45-day notice promised before any new window (that 45-day formulation is from older Dept. of Taxation guidance, not current CCB FAQ). Treat “not currently open for retail” as [VERIFY] against a current CCB notice — it is consistent with the absence of any open-window notice found, but no current CCB “applications closed” page was located. Lounges: no currently open lounge window was located; the FAQ’s 30-day-notice / 10-business-day mechanics are written as standing procedure, not as an announcement of an open window. Lounge conditional licensees continue to appear in Board minutes with deadline extensions to 1 Dec 2026 / 1 Mar 2027 / 30 Apr 2027, indicating a pipeline of already-selected applicants still working toward final inspection — not a new intake. Examples: CCB Minutes 16 Apr 2026; Minutes 19 Mar 2026 — both accessed 2026-10-31. Hemp (for contrast, NDA — not CCB): applications are due on or before 1 July each year and registrations expire 31 Dec each year (NRS 557.200). Full hemp licence detail is Run 2, Part 7.

No official CCB startup-cost figure exists. The following secondary ranges conflict sharply and must not be averaged or presented as official:

Why the spread matters: Nevada is a limited-licence state; the binding cost for a new entrant is usually acquiring an existing licence/business, not the state fee. One secondary marketplace listing in Run 1 offered a non-operational, “portable” Clark County cultivation licence at $99,000 asking price (listing states the buyer must find a new Clark County location). That is a single asking price, not a market valuation, and the listing itself disclaims independent verification — secondary, unverified. Official costs that ARE certain (use these instead of estimates where possible): state application + initial + renewal fees (§4.9); agent cards $150/category; Clark County cannabis establishment application fee $1,500 and distribution $250 (see Part 5, Clark County row); Nevada state business licence fees via SilverFlume (see Part 5). Local fees beyond Clark County are [VERIFY] jurisdiction by jurisdiction.

Part 5 — Licence Data Sources

How to read this Part: for each source: URL, holder type, format, access/download, stated update frequency, latest data date found, fields, limitations. “Interactive only” means no bulk download was found on that page. Where a direct file link was only cited by a third party and not re-opened, it is marked [LINK NOT RE-OPENED — VERIFY BEFORE PUBLISHING]. This Part does not list every licence holder, as instructed.

5.1 CCB License Search (the master search)

  • URL: https://ccb.nv.gov/license-search/ — accessed 2026-10-31. Paginated form seen in search results, e.g. license-search/?listpage=185&instance=2.
  • Holder type: cannabis establishments and their owners, officers, board members and agents — the search is a business/owner/member/board-member database, not a pure establishment list. CCB description: “Nevada Cannabis Compliance Board business, owner, member and board member license database.” Published 2020-05-08, updated 2020-11-04 (page metadata — stale metadata; the underlying records are plainly newer, see record totals below).
  • Format: interactive web search / paginated HTML list, 20 records per page. No CSV/XLSX/PDF bulk download and no export button were found on this page in Run 1.
  • Access: free, no login stated; search/filter by name/entity and licence type (cultivation, production, dispensary, distributor, consumption lounge examples seen: C155, C152, P020, P094, D053, T101, CLA016, CLA014, RD547, RD596).
  • Update frequency as stated: none stated on the page [UNAVAILABLE].
  • Latest data date: no “as of” date on the search page itself. Search-engine crawls in Run 1 show total records fluctuating 4,617 → 4,727 → 4,932 across crawls (most recent crawl 16 days before 2026-10-31 for one page) — that fluctuation reflects owners/officers as well as licences and must not be quoted as a licence count.
  • Fields available (as seen in results): first name / last name or entity name; licensed entity; licence type; Cannabis Establishment ID (e.g. C155, P094, D053, T101, CLA016); mailing address (P.O. Box 1948, Nevada 89701 appears as a CCB mailing address in results, not a facility address — do not use it as a premises address).
  • Limitations: no jurisdiction/county filter was verified; no status filter (operational vs conditional vs prospective) was verified; contact information is confidential per CCB FAQ (see §4.10), so the search does not publish phone/email; the page metadata date (2020) is misleading; bulk reuse requires scraping paginated HTML, which CCB does not offer as a download.

5.2 CCB List of Licensees (retail storefronts + operating lounges)

  • URL: https://ccb.nv.gov/list-of-licensees/ — accessed 2026-10-31. Updated: 2026-10-26T18:11:20+00:00 (page footer). Published 2020-06-02.
  • Holder type: licensed retail stores (Southern Nevada / Northern Nevada sections) and cannabis consumption lounges.
  • Format: interactive HTML table on a web page. No download file and no export button found.
  • Access: free, open web page; each store name links to the operator’s own website (not to a CCB record).
  • Update frequency as stated: none stated, but the footer update stamp (2026-10-26) shows it is actively maintained.
  • Latest data date: 26 October 2026 (footer).
  • Fields: business/trade name; street address; Adult Use vs Medical Only designation; 20-digit licence number; Delivery Option Y/N. Lounge section gives the same fields for the 3 operating lounges.
  • Limitations: retail + lounges only — no cultivation, production, distribution or testing facilities; no licence-status field beyond inclusion on an “active” list; no county/jurisdiction column (region is by section heading only, and several addresses are in unincorporated Clark County / Paradise / Spring Valley, not the City of Las Vegas — the page does not distinguish them, so do not re-label them as “Las Vegas” city licences); no owner names.

5.3 CCB disciplinary actions and settlements — no standalone database; use Board minutes/agendas

No single searchable disciplinary database or downloadable disciplinary CSV was located on ccb.nv.gov in Run 1. [UNAVAILABLE as a standalone source.] Actual source — CCB public meeting minutes and agendas (PDFs, individually downloadable): where to find them — CCB Public Meetings pages (ccb.nv.gov/publicmeetings/ — link as cited in CCB workshop notices; the meetings index URL itself was not re-opened, so cite as [VERIFY]) and individual PDFs linked from search results. Format: PDF per meeting (agenda + minutes). No consolidated download, no search index by licensee. Access: free PDFs. Examples opened/cited in Run 1 (accessed 2026-10-31): Minutes 19 Feb 2026 — settlements for Livfree Wellness LLC dba The Dispensary (Eastern), Case No. 2025-024 (RD547); Clear River LLC dba Nevada Made Marijuana, Case No. 2025-022 (RD230); Lone Mountain Partners LLC dba Zenleaf of North Las Vegas, Case No. 2025-023 (RD592) — all arising from spring 2025 underage compliance checks (passport ID, person under 21). File: 02.19.26-Meeting-Minutes-approved.pdf. Minutes 20 Nov 2025 — settlement for Blossum [sic] Group LLC, Case No. 2025-006 (C059): repeat violations including failure to properly tag cannabis, waste disposal, seed-to-sale tracking, quarterly reports. File: 11.20.25-Meeting-Minutes-approved.pdf. Minutes 19 Mar 2026 — complaint authorisation procedure (Respondent A redacted) under NRS 678A.510/520; complaint posted on CCB website once served. File: 03.19.26-Meeting-Minutes-Watermarked_rev.pdf. Minutes 16 Apr 2026 — consent agenda including final-inspection deadline extensions to 1 Dec 2026 and lounge extension to 30 Apr 2027. File: 04.16.26-Meeting-Minutes-approved.pdf. Minutes 21 Aug 2025 — lounge conditional-licence consideration; complaint procedure. File: 08.21.25-Meeting-Minutes-approved-rev.pdf. Fields (in minutes): licensee legal + DBA name, establishment ID (C/P/D/RD/CLI codes), case number, violation summary, settlement terms as summarised by the Senior Deputy Attorney General, Board vote. Full settlement documents themselves are not consistently attached to minutes [VERIFY per case]. Limitations: chronological only — to build a disciplinary history for one licensee you must search minutes case-by-case; pre-service complaints are redacted (“Respondent A”); CCB FAQ confirms only “final disciplinary action” is public. Biennial context: 9 disciplinary complaints authorised by Board members in 2024 (CCB Biennial Report 2025, FY24 inspection data: 157 Statements of No Deficiencies, 198 Statements of Deficiencies, 1 Jul 2023–30 Jun 2024).

5.4 CCB product recalls / health advisories — Public Health and Safety Bulletins (PDFs, no index)

No consolidated recall database or searchable recall list was located on ccb.nv.gov in Run 1. [UNAVAILABLE as a database.] Actual source — individual Public Health and Safety Bulletins (PDFs): Bulletin 2025-02 (15 Dec 2025, amended 19 Dec 2025 to remove 8 unaffected packages): cannabis/cannabis products treated with unapproved pesticide Eagle 20, not applied per label, at Greenway Medical, LLC (Licence #45452459135682728564; cultivation C018), harvested Aug–Nov 2025; affected products include flower, shake/trim, uninfused pre-rolls, infused edibles, infused flower, infused pre-rolls, concentrates; sold Sep–Dec 2025 at listed sales facilities (e.g. The Spot, 4310 W Flamingo Rd, Las Vegas, NV 89103). File: Public-Health-Bulletin-2025-02.pdf — accessed 2026-10-31 (via search result; amended version also at a secondary host — use the CCB file as primary). Bulletin 2026-01 (28 Oct 2026, per secondary reporting 30 Oct 2026): flower and uninfused pre-rolls sold 1 Jan–13 Aug 2026; one lot confirmed failed on secondary testing for Enterobacteriaceae and total coliforms (Orange Juice Mac 3.5 g, batch F1H8OJM, package 1A4040300007081000009370); other listed lots could not be retested; sales facilities instructed to display the bulletin for 30 days. The CCB PDF URL for 2026-01 was not verified in Run 1 — the bulletin is reported at a secondary site, accessed 2026-10-31 [secondary; CCB file link = VERIFY before citing in the dossier]. Format: PDF bulletin per incident. No export, no index page located. Fields: product names, batch/package (Metrc) identifiers, harvest/sale date ranges, affected sales facilities, consumer instruction (avoid / take caution). Limitations: bulletins are advisories, not by themselves a statewide recall order or a final enforcement outcome; there is no CCB page that lists all bulletins in one place that was located in Run 1 — bulletins must be found via CCB news/media posts or search.
