5.6 Medical patient statistics (counts only — no patient identities)

  • Holder/operator: Nevada Division of Public and Behavioral Health (DPBH), Medical Marijuana Patient Cardholder Registry (MMR) — not CCB. The registry stayed with DPBH when CCB took over establishment licensing.
  • Index page: DPBH monthly reports index — accessed 2026-10-31 (via search result; lists 2026 monthly PDFs Jan–Sep 2026).
  • Format: PDF per month. No CSV, no dashboard, no export button.
  • Direct download links + file names (verbatim from search results, accessed 2026-10-31):
    • Sep 2026: mmr-monthly-stats-eom-september-2026.pdf
    • Aug 2026: mmr-monthly-stats-eom-august-2026.pdf
    • Jul 2026: mmr-monthly-stats-eom-july-2026.pdf
    • Mar 2026: mmr-monthly-stats-eom-march-2026.pdf
    • Feb 2026: mmr-monthly-statistics-eom-2-2026.pdf (note different naming)
    • Jan 2026: mmr-monthly-statistics-eom-1-2026.pdf
    • Apr/May/Jun 2026 PDFs are listed on the index page but their exact URLs were not returned in Run 1 [VERIFY on the index page].
  • Update frequency: monthly (end-of-month statistics).
  • Latest data date: September 2026: 8,718 active patient cardholders; 414 active caregiver cardholders; 1,180 physicians recommending. August 2026: 8,699 / 419 / 1,181. July 2026: 8,736 / 426 / 1,180.
  • Fields: active patients, caregivers, recommending physicians; 1-year / 2-year applications received; qualifying conditions (patients may have >1 — e.g. Sep 2026: severe/chronic pain 6,412; anxiety disorder 3,798; neuropathic condition 1,990; muscle spasms 1,781); patients by county (Sep 2026: Clark 5,370; Washoe figure truncated in excerpt — do not quote Washoe Sep 2026 from Run 1); patients by age group.
  • Limitations: counts only; no names, no card numbers, no dispensary-level patient data; a secondary summary claiming ~28,308 patients in 2026 (CannBus) conflicts with DPBH’s ~8,700 and must not be used — DPBH is authoritative. Statutory mandate cited on each PDF: Nevada Constitution, Article 4, Section 38.

5.7 Lounge licence lists

Operating lounges: CCB List of Licensees, lounge section (3 lounges, updated 2026-10-26) — see §5.2. Interactive HTML table only; no download. Prospective / conditional lounge applicants: no standalone lounge-licence list was located. The pipeline must be reconstructed from Board minutes (deadline extensions for named applicants, e.g. ACON-A22-00029 Meds N Sin prof. LLC — extension to 30 Apr 2027; ACON-A22-00071 Greenwood Investment Group, LLC and ACON-A22-00102 Firehouse 420 Inc — extensions to 1 Mar 2027; ACON-A22-00085 Las Vegas Cannabis Industry Leaders LLC — extension to 1 Jul 2026; CLI002 KV Group, CLI010 The Limo Joint LLC — final-inspection extensions). Sources: CCB Minutes 16 Apr 2026, 19 Mar 2026, 21 Aug 2025 (URLs in §5.3). Secondary lounge pipeline figure [VERIFY, do not use as official]: CannBus Q3 2026 states “23 more lounge licenses were conditionally approved and waiting on inspections” in spring 2026 (labelled Modelled-Est. by CannBus itself). Licence Search lounge records: consumption lounge records appear in the License Search with IDs CLA014, CLA016 etc. (see §5.1). That is the only CCB search that returns lounge licence IDs, but it does not distinguish operating vs conditional vs prospective in the excerpts seen [VERIFY].

5.8 Department of Agriculture hemp licence lists — no public list located

Status: [UNAVAILABLE as a published list in Run 1.] No NDA page listing current hemp growers, handlers or producers by name, and no downloadable NDA hemp registry file, was located via search or via the NDA site in Run 1. The historic NDA hemp overview URL cited in older sources — http://agri.nv.gov/Plant/Seed_Certification/Industrial_Hemp/Industrial_Hemp_Overview/ — was not opened successfully in Run 1 and may be dead after NDA site migration; do not cite it as live without re-checking. What is official and verified: hemp registration law is NRS Chapter 557 (grower / handler / producer; application by 1 July; expiry 31 Dec; fingerprints/background check; site registration). Source: NRS 557, accessed 2026-10-31. Hemp fees in regulation (secondary summaries, to be verified against NAC 557): grower $900 + $5/outdoor acre or $0.33/1,000 sq ft indoor [AUDIT: NAC 557.150; $500 was the superseded 2018 draft figure]; seed producer $100 + acreage/sq-ft fees (and a $725 nursery-stock variant reported in one secondary source — conflicting, [VERIFY]); handler $1,000 per facility. Secondary hemp counts [NOT official, undated in source]: Cannabiz Media tally — 175 licences: 109 growers, 47 handlers, 19 seed producers; 114 holders with one licence, 28 with two or three. Date of that tally is not stated on the page excerpt — do not use as a current count. A separate secondary source states “as of August 1, 2025, there were 16 registered hemp processing facilities” — also unverified and inconsistent with the 47-handler tally; both are flagged as conflicting secondary figures for Part 6/7 follow-up. Follow-up: contact NDA Plant Industry Division / request the registry under Nevada public-records law; check whether NDA publishes hemp certificates only on request. This is the single largest Part 5 gap for hemp.

5.9 Secretary of State business entity search (SilverFlume — with a 2026 migration warning)

Current position — migration in progress [VERIFY before citing a URL in the dossier]: secondary reporting (Oct 2026) states the Secretary of State’s Project ORION replaced SilverFlume and esos.nv.gov in 2026 (phase 1 Dec 2025 for UCC; phase 2 summer 2026 fully replacing SilverFlume), with old links redirecting to the ORION public portal [secondary; the Secretary of State’s own ORION announcement was not opened in Run 1]. Historic / still-cited official entry points (as cited by CCB FAQ and secondary guides): SilverFlume home: https://www.nvsilverflume.gov/home (as printed in CCB FAQ, accessed 2026-10-31); entity search (historic): esos.nv.gov/EntitySearch/OnlineEntitySearch (as cited in secondary guides; not re-opened in Run 1). Holder type: all Nevada business entities (corporations, LLCs, partnerships, nonprofits, foreign registrations) — not cannabis-specific. A cannabis licensee’s entity record appears here only as a business entity; the CCB licence itself does not appear here. Format: interactive web search. No bulk download. Certified copies / certificates of good standing are ordered separately (fee-bearing). Access: basic search free, no account required (per secondary guides); search by entity name, entity number, NV Business ID (NVBID), officer/member name, or registered agent name; modes include Starts With / Contains / Exact Match / All Words (per secondary guides). Fields: entity legal name, status (Active / Default / Revoked etc.), entity type, formation date, NVBID, entity number, registered agent, managers/managing members/officers (from Initial/Annual Lists — Nevada publishes these, unlike most states), state business licence status, filing history, name changes, mergers/conversions. Limitations: covers state-registered entities only — county fictitious firm names (DBAs) are filed with the county clerk, not the state; search is informational only, not certified proof; if ORION has replaced SilverFlume, every SilverFlume URL in older CCB guidance (including the CCB FAQ link) may now redirect — the dossier must give the ORION entry point once verified on nvsos.gov. Why it matters for Part 4: CCB lounge applicants (and, in practice, all applicants) must first hold a Nevada state business licence via this system. Source: CCB FAQ.

5.10 Tribal compact and licence records (if public)

Public compact located — Las Vegas Paiute Tribe. File (fully executed compact, PDF): LV-Tribe-of-Paiute-Indians-Fully-Executed.pdf — accessed 2026-10-31 (via search result). Entered into under SB 375 (2017), Chapter 305, Laws of 2017; parties: Las Vegas Tribe of Paiute Indians of the Las Vegas Indian Colony and the State of Nevada; tribal regulator named in the compact: Las Vegas Paiute Cannabis Authority (LVPCA). Governor’s office (General Counsel, 101 N. Carson Street, Carson City, NV 89701) is the state notice address; Dept. of Taxation was the state cannabis authority at signing. Format: PDF compact text. No download index of all compacts was located on CCB or the Governor’s site in Run 1. Other compacts — [VERIFY / UNAVAILABLE as public texts in Run 1]: secondary sources report compacts signed summer 2017 with Las Vegas Paiute, Yerington Paiute, and Ely Shoshone, and later references to 4–5 compacts total; the Yerington Paiute compact (reported as the second signed, July 2017) and any Ely Shoshone, Moapa, Reno-Sparks or other compact texts were not located as public PDFs in Run 1. Tribal licence lists: no public tribal licence search or tribal licensee list was located. Tribal operations (e.g. NuWu Cannabis Marketplace, Las Vegas Paiute; a Moapa Band of Paiutes / Mint Cannabis dispensary reported opened 20 June 2026) do not appear as CCB licences — they are tribal-authority licences, and whether they appear in CCB’s List of Licensees is [VERIFY] — the 3-lounge list and retail list in §5.2 do not label any entry as tribal. Follow-up for Run 2, Part 10: request compact texts from the Governor’s office / CCB; check each tribe’s own government site for its cannabis authority and licence list. Do not assume a tribe without a located compact has no operation, or vice versa.

5.11 USDA hemp records for Nevada

Nevada has its own USDA-approved state plan — so there is NO USDA-issued producer licence list for Nevada to inventory. USDA AMS “List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans” (updated 17 Feb 2026) lists: Nevada — NV Approved Plan (pdf) * (* = approved under the final rule effective 22 March 2021). Source: USDA AMS state-and-tribal-plan-review, accessed 2026-10-31. Nevada State Hemp Plan (revised 25 May 2021; USDA approval 28 May 2021): NevadaHempStatePlan.pdf — accessed 2026-10-31 (via search result). USDA rule: if a state has an approved plan, producers must apply under that state’s programme; USDA Hemp Producer Licences (via the HeMP — Hemp eManagement Platform) are only for states/tribes without an approved plan. Source: USDA information for hemp growers, accessed 2026-10-31. Consequence: do not search HeMP for “Nevada USDA licences” — Nevada producers are NDA registrants (see §5.8, where no public list was found). USDA’s public HeMP data, if it lists Nevada at all, would be [VERIFY] and is not the operative registry. Related federal record: NDA-certified growers must report acreage to USDA Farm Service Agency (FSA) annually under the state plan — that FSA acreage data is a separate federal dataset, not a licence list, and was not located in Run 1 [VERIFY].

5.12 Local (county / city / Carson City) business licence records

General rule: local business licensing is separate from the CCB licence and is a precondition to final CCB approval (see §4.10). No statewide index of local cannabis business licences exists [UNAVAILABLE as a single source].

  • Clark County (unincorporated — including the Strip, Paradise, Spring Valley, Enterprise, Winchester): category page: Clark County business detail t39-r91 — accessed 2026-10-31. Cannabis establishments = Cannabis Stores, Cultivation and Production Facilities, Cannabis Testing Facilities, Cannabis Distributors; applicants must hold a valid CCB licence + zoning approval; Cannabis Support Businesses also in the category; appointments via (702) 455-4125 / CCRMMI@ClarkCountyNV.gov (public contact as published on the page). Application packet (PDF, direct download): cannabis-establishment-checklist-and-application-packet.pdf — accessed 2026-10-31 (via search result). Contents: instructions; application fee $1,500 for a cannabis establishment, $250 for cannabis distribution; requirement for a Special Use Permit (zoning) verification, public-safety inspections, and a Code Conference; “No business may be conducted until a Clark County Business License has been approved and issued.” Format: PDF packet + (presumably) in-person/mailed application; no searchable online Clark County cannabis-licensee list was located in Run 1. Clark County’s general business-licence search, if it exists, was not verified — do not cite one.
  • City of Las Vegas: Business Licensing Division instructions exist by category, including a “Hemp Handler” instruction sheet (a person/business holding a state NDA licence who receives industrial hemp for processing). URL as returned: City Hemp Handler instructions — accessed 2026-10-31 (via earlier search result; not re-opened in this pass — [VERIFY live status]). No searchable City of Las Vegas cannabis-licensee list was located in Run 1. Note the trap: most “Las Vegas” dispensaries on the CCB list are in unincorporated Clark County, not the City of Las Vegas — the CCB list does not distinguish, so a city-only list would be much shorter.
  • North Las Vegas, Henderson, Boulder City, Mesquite, Washoe County, Reno, Sparks, Carson City, Douglas, Lyon, Nye (incl. Pahrump), Elko and other rural counties: no published searchable cannabis business-licence list was located for any of these jurisdictions in Run 1. Each jurisdiction’s business-licence records, if published at all, are in that jurisdiction’s own (usually non-cannabis-specific) business-licence system or available only by public-records request. Do not invent URLs for them. Part 3 should check each jurisdiction’s business-licence / planning portal individually and record “no online cannabis list found” where that is the result, rather than omitting the jurisdiction. One useful local datum found: a Carson City staff report (18 Aug 2022) confirms Carson City then limited medical dispensaries to 2 and retail stores (co-located with dispensaries) to 2 by ordinance.

5.13 Other CCB data sources relevant to licence research

  • CCB Industry / Forms / Media pages: CCB Industry page (ccb.nv.gov/industry, updated 2026-10-26) is the hub for licence-type descriptions, agent cards, social equity, and forms; the CCB Media page hosts demographic studies and other industry statistics (cited in the demographics press release as ccb.nv.gov/media/#item-3 — not re-opened). Format: HTML + linked PDFs. No bulk data download.
  • CCB Biennial Report 2025: the only official source found that gives a by-type operational breakdown + jurisdiction breakdown (Clark 260 / Washoe 62) in one document. File: CCB-Biennial-Report-2025-FINAL.pdf — opened 2026-10-31.
  • CCB Annual Demographics Study 2025 (for release, 13 May 2026): workforce demographics + licence totals (353 active, 107 dispensaries, ~12,541 agent cards, Dec 2025). File: 2025-CCB-Annual-Demographics-Study-for-release.pdf — accessed 2026-10-31 (via search result; not fully opened — verify by-type detail inside before quoting beyond the press-release figures).
  • Metrc: Nevada’s seed-to-sale system (CCB FAQ: “Nevada has a seed-to-sale inventory tracking system called Metrc”). Metrc data is not public licence-holder data and has no public search; it is included here only to prevent confusion — do not list Metrc as a Part 5 public source.
  • Accela Cannabis Customer Portal: aca-prod.accela.com/NVCCB — the system of record for applications, renewals, amendments and transfers. It is a licensee/applicant portal, not a public search; public users cannot browse other licensees’ records there.

Part 6 — Market Data

6.1 Medical and adult-use sales — fiscal year and monthly

What the official series actually measures. The Dept. of Taxation series is “Taxable Sales Reported by Cannabis Dispensaries and Consumption Lounges.” Its own footnote states it: “Includes sales of adult-use cannabis, medical cannabis, tangible personal property transferred for value, and all other amounts subject to Sales or Use Tax, as reported by licensed cannabis establishments.” It therefore does not publish a separate medical-vs-adult-use sales split in the monthly DoT tables opened for Run 1. Any medical/adult-use split must be marked [UNAVAILABLE] from DoT monthly tables. Sources: Dept. of Taxation Cannabis Tax Revenue FY26 PDF, last updated 17 Aug 2026, NV-Cannabis-Revenue-FY26.pdf; FY27 PDF, last updated 16 Oct 2026, NV-Cannabis-Revenue-FY27.pdf — both accessed 2026-10-31.

Fiscal year (1 July–30 June)Taxable sales — officialChangeSource
FY24$829,225,193—CCB/DoT joint release, 5 Feb 2026
FY25$757,714,911−8.6% vs FY24CCB/DoT joint release, 5 Feb 2026
FY26$695,995,234−8.2% vs FY25 (calculated: 695,995,234 ÷ 757,714,911 − 1)DoT FY26 table, last updated 17 Aug 2026

Detail for FY25: “During FY 25 (July 1, 2024 – June 30, 2025) licensed adult-use cannabis retail stores and medical dispensaries generated $757,714,911 in taxable sales. This is a decrease of roughly 8.6% as compared to $829,225,193 in taxable sales from FY 24.” Source: CCB/DoT, “CCB/Tax Release Annual Cannabis Taxable Sales Data,” 5 Feb 2026, CCB-Tax-Release PDF, accessed 2026-10-31. County split FY25: Clark County $567,626,861; Washoe County $105,764,452; all other counties $84,323,598 — same source. Clark share = 74.9% of FY25 taxable sales (calculated, not a tourist share — see §6.2). Detail for FY26, by county (DoT FY26 table): total $695,995,234; Clark County $506,357,507; Washoe County $101,178,243; all other counties $88,459,484. Clark share FY26 = 72.8% (calculated). This is a county share, not a tourist share.

Conflict / reconciliation note — calendar-year figures are a different series, do not mix with fiscal-year figures. Secondary compilations citing DoT give calendar-year totals of $723,845,835 for 2025 and $812,005,882 for 2024, and a company filing citing DoT gives “over $723 million for the 2025 calendar year” (Vencanna Ventures MD&A, effective 27 Aug 2026) — both secondary, accessed 2026-10-31; direct DoT calendar-year table not opened in Run 1, so treat as VERIFY before republication. The apparent gap between FY25 $757.7m and calendar-2025 $723.8m is explained by the different 12-month windows (July–June vs January–December), not by an error in either series. Do not present either as the other. FY25 DoT PDF access failure: an attempt to open the FY25 PDF on 2026-10-31 returned only a bot-check page (“One moment, please…”). FY25 monthly detail is therefore not reproduced from that PDF directly; FY25 totals above rest on the CCB/DoT joint release PDF, which did open. FY25 monthly breakdown = [VERIFY] from the DoT FY25 PDF itself.

Medical-vs-adult-use split

DoT monthly tables: no split published — [UNAVAILABLE] from the tables opened. One secondary summary of a CCB-commissioned study states 2022 medical sales of $1.6 million against $861 million adult-use — secondary, dated study, not a current split; do not use as a 2025–2026 split. The same CCB-commissioned study is cited in secondary summaries for: flower = 34% of adult-use sales in September 2023; average retail flower price near $3,000 per pound in September 2023, down from over $4,000 per pound in 2020–2021 — secondary reporting of a CCB-commissioned study; the study itself was not opened in Run 1 — VERIFY before quoting as official. Because Nevada retail stores are now dual licensees (see Part 4), and DoT reports combined taxable sales, a current medical share cannot be derived from the official monthly tables without a separate CCB/DPBH release. That release was not found in Run 1 = [UNAVAILABLE].

The release states: “NRS 372A.290 directs all revenue from the 10-percent State Retail Cannabis Excise Tax into the State Education Fund (SEF), formerly the Distributive School Account (DSA)” and that $5 million of wholesale excise revenue is distributed to local governments (see Part 2 for the full distribution rule). Sales-tax (standard state/local sales tax) revenue on cannabis is not separated in the excise tables opened = [UNAVAILABLE] as a cannabis-only sales-tax total from these files.

6.2 Share of sales to tourists — officially [UNAVAILABLE]

No official CCB, DoT, or LVCVA figure for the share of Nevada cannabis sales made to tourists/non-residents was found in the sources opened for Run 1. Do not estimate it. Do not use Clark County’s share of sales (§6.1) as a proxy for tourist share — Clark County includes a large resident population. What is officially/contextually established, without converting it into a tourist share:

  • Tourism volume (context only): Las Vegas visitation was 38.5 million in 2025, down 7.5% year-over-year from 41.6 million in 2024; hotel occupancy averaged 80.3%, down 3.3 points; average daily room rate $183.52, down 5.0% — as reported from LVCVA data. Source (secondary reporting of LVCVA, 29 Jan 2026), accessed 2026-10-31 [secondary reporting of LVCVA; LVCVA’s own 2025 annual release was not opened — VERIFY against LVCVA directly before quoting as LVCVA-direct].
  • Secondary attribution of the sales decline to tourism: a company filing citing DoT and LVCVA Research Centre attributes the calendar-2025 sales decline partly to “declining tourism (down 7% in 2025 compared to 2024…)” and price compression, with “38 million tourists in 2025” — secondary, company filing; tourism percentage differs slightly (7% vs LVCVA-reporting 7.5%) — flag as conflicting secondary rounding, do not resolve by averaging.
  • Secondary lounge-tourist claim — NOT official, do not use as a market share: a lounge guide claims “~60% tourists, ~40% locals” for lounge clientele and “an estimated 60% of lounge visitors are tourists” — secondary, no methodology or source given — UNVERIFIED.
  • UNLV gaming–cannabis separation study (secondary reporting, not a tourist-sales share): the UNLV Cannabis Policy Institute / International Gaming Institute report “The 1,500 Foot Wall” (discussed May 2026) is reported to estimate ~$540 million in lost 2024 legal retail revenue ($210 million from the ban on delivery to hotels/gaming properties), ~$80 million in lost annual tax revenue, and that 69% of Las Vegas visitors stay on the Strip — secondary reporting, accessed 2026-10-31 [secondary reporting of a university study; the study itself was not opened — VERIFY the $540m/$80m figures against the UNLV report before use in Part 6 or Part 10].
  • Illicit-market context (not tourist share): see §6.7.

Recommended follow-up: ask CCB/DoT directly, or search CCB market-study releases, for any residency/visitor breakdown in point-of-sale or Metrc data. None was found in Run 1’s official sources.

6.6 Official price data

No official CCB or DoT published price series (price per gram / per item, by month) was found in the sources opened for Run 1. State that plainly; the figures below are all secondary and, where they conflict, the conflict is preserved.

  1. Headset (point-of-sale data), September 2026 snapshot, data snapshot 3 Oct 2026: Nevada total sales (Headset-tracked, past 12 months) $498.3 million; average item price $20.73; −9.0% year-over-year sales; −6.5% units sold. Category prices: flower (gram) $10.63; flower (eighth) $21.80; edible (100 mg) $13.72; concentrate (gram) $20.99. Source: Headset markets page, accessed via search 2026-10-31 [secondary — Headset’s tracked-store universe is smaller than DoT’s statewide taxable sales ($498.3m vs DoT FY26 $696.0m); do not present Headset totals as statewide totals]. Corroborating Headset national page: Nevada average eighth $21.80 (Sep 2026), national average item $15.54 — secondary.
  2. Conflicting secondary flower-per-gram figure — flag, do not resolve: Marijuana Herald, 14 Oct 2026, reporting September pricing data from state regulators + Headset: Nevada average flower price $6.23 per gram, concentrate $20.99 per gram — secondary, accessed 2026-10-31. This $6.23/g conflicts with Headset’s $10.63/g “flower (gram)” above. The difference is plausibly a package-size / equivalisation definition (single-gram package vs all packaged flower averaged per gram), but neither source as fetched defines it sufficiently to reconcile — [VERIFY definition before quoting either as “the” Nevada price per gram]. An earlier Herald compilation (23 Jun 2025, updated 29 Sep 2025) gave Nevada $9.25/g — secondary, superseded snapshot.
  3. Nevada Interim Legislative Committee on Revenue / Taxation subcommittee — reported in trade press, Oct 2026: “The retail price of cannabis flowers in Nevada dropped from $9.02 per gram in 2019 to $4.46 in 2025, a 50% decline, while infused edibles fell 40% over the same period.” “Excise tax revenue peaked in 2021 and fell 40% by 2025” (per the subcommittee report as reported). A proposal studied (not adopted): eliminate the wholesale tax, raise retail excise to 12–15%; “the report stopped short of recommending any specific changes” and “the revenue committee took no action.” Source: MJBizDaily article, accessed 2026-10-31 [secondary reporting of an official subcommittee report — VERIFY the $9.02→$4.46 series against the subcommittee report itself; note it also conflicts in level with Headset/Herald, reflecting a different price concept (likely equivalised flower price), so do not chain these series together]. The same article reports the subcommittee estimated Nevada’s intoxicating hemp market at ~$45 million annually — see §6.9.
  4. CCB-commissioned market study (older, official-commissioned but secondarily reported): flower ~$3,000/lb Sep 2023, down from >$4,000/lb in 2020–2021; flower 34% of adult-use sales Sep 2023 — see §6.1 [secondary reporting — VERIFY].

No official wholesale (fair-market-value) price series was found in the DoT tables opened; wholesale excise is reported only as tax collected, from which an implied wholesale base could be calculated (wholesale tax ÷ 15%) but that derivation is not presented as an official price, and the FY25/FY26 wholesale-tax figures include delinquencies/adjustments noted in DoT footnotes.

6.7 Market concentration among operators

Official position: the CCB publishes concentration by geography, not by operator/company. No official operator market-share ranking was found. Do not rank operators yourself. Official geographic concentration (see §6.3): 260 of 387 operational licences (67.2%) in the Clark County area; 62 (16.0%) in the Washoe County area; CCB: “The highest concentration of operational licenses is in Southern Nevada.” Source: CCB Agency Overview, accessed via search 2026-10-31. List observation only (not a market-share finding): the CCB retail list fetched 2026-10-31 shows multiple locations under the same brand names (e.g. Curaleaf, RISE, Thrive Cannabis Marketplace, The Source, The Dispensary, Green., Deep Roots Harvest, Zen Leaf, Cultivate, Beyond/Hello, The Grove, Silver State Relief) — CCB List of Licensees, accessed 2026-10-31. Location counts are not sales shares; a multi-store operator may have small stores and a single-store operator may have large sales. No sales-by-operator data was found = [UNAVAILABLE].

Illicit-market size (context for concentration — licensed share is understated if illicit sales are included): CCB-contracted market study as presented to the Legislature, Feb 2025: illicit market $242 million, about 21% of the total cannabis market in Nevada. Sources: Senate Committee on Judiciary minutes, 6 Feb 2025; Senate Commerce & Labor minutes, 7 Feb 2025 — both accessed via search 2026-10-31. CCB “Buy Legal” release, 16 Apr 2025: “A third-party study estimates 30% of cannabis purchased in Nevada is illicit.” CCB Executive Director in Feb 2026 press reporting: “up to a quarter of marijuana sales in Nevada are illegal” — secondary reporting of a CCB statement, FOX5/KVVU, published 6 Feb 2026. AB 76 Taxation Subcommittee report (Sep 2026) cites the 2024 CCB market study / New Frontier Data forecast of the illicit market “steadily decreasing to 20 percent of the cannabis market in 2025, which would put an estimate for Nevada’s illicit market size at around $145 million to the licensed cannabis market size of $725 million estimated from the 2025 Cannabis Statistics” — official subcommittee report, accessed via search 2026-10-31 [note its $725m licensed-market estimate differs from both FY25 $757.7m and calendar-2025 $723.8m; the report’s estimation basis is not fully stated in the snippet — VERIFY before quoting $145m as a point estimate]. Conclusion for Part 6: report the illicit share as a range across official/commissioned sources: ~20–30%, with the point estimates and dates above, not as a single figure.

6.8.3 Most recent official count — Cannabis Advisory Commission Hemp Subcommittee Report, 10 Sep 2026 (AB 76 §54.3)

“According to the NDA, the number of registered hemp growers declined from 213 in 2019 to five as of July 1, 2026; the Department also reported nine registered handlers and no registered agricultural hemp seed producers.”

Source: CAC Hemp Subcommittee Report, 10 Sep 2026, accessed via search 2026-10-31 [official CAC subcommittee report citing NDA — this is the latest official registration count found]. Conflict to flag: the Aug 2025 secondary summary (11 growers, 16 processing facilities for the 2025 season) vs the July 2026 official count (5 growers, 9 handlers, 0 seed producers) reflects both a different date (2025 season vs 1 July 2026) and possibly different counting (season planters vs year-end registrants; “processing facilities” vs “handlers”). Do not present 11/16 and 5/9 as contradictory without that date/definition note; the direction (continued collapse from a tiny base) is consistent. The same report states: “The size of the hemp-derived cannabinoid product marketplace is not explained by Nevada agricultural production” — i.e. Nevada’s intoxicating-hemp retail market is supplied overwhelmingly from out of state, not from Nevada-grown hemp. Same source.

6.8.4 USDA NASS — national context; Nevada state value [UNAVAILABLE] in sources checked

USDA NASS National Hemp Report methodology (released 4 Jun 2026): the Hemp Production and Disposition Inquiry covers all growers licensed in 2025; the 2025 publication was released 16 Apr 2026; sample 7,777 operations, “a census of all known hemp growers”. National 2025 results (secondary reporting of the 16 Apr 2026 NASS release): US value of hemp production (open + under protection) $739 million, up 64% from 2024; outdoor value $646 million; outdoor floral $574 million (89% of outdoor value); planted open acreage 49,267 acres — secondary reporting of USDA NASS — national figures only. Nevada-specific NASS acreage/value for 2025 was not found in the sources opened. NASS withholds small-state data to protect individual producers, and Nevada’s 2025 planted area (10.5 acres per the NDA summary) is small enough that suppression is plausible. Nevada NASS state table = [UNAVAILABLE — check the NASS National Hemp Report state tables directly (ESMIS, Apr 2026 release) before asserting a Nevada NASS figure]. USDA AMS state-plan records for NV were not separately opened in the Part 6 pass = [VERIFY via AMS Domestic Hemp Production Program listings in Part 7] — see Part 5.11 for the AMS list check that does confirm Nevada’s approved-plan status.

6.9 Hemp product market figures

Official Nevada hemp-product sales series: none found. The only quantified Nevada figure found is an estimate, reported secondarily. Intoxicating hemp market estimate: ~$45 million annually (Nevada, as estimated by the Interim Legislative Committee on Revenue’s Taxation Subcommittee; the subcommittee “recommended the state pursue tax parity between hemp and cannabis products”). Source (secondary reporting), accessed 2026-10-31 [secondary reporting of an official subcommittee estimate — VERIFY against the subcommittee report itself; methodology, year, and whether this is retail sales or taxable base are not stated in the article snippet]. Qualitative official finding (CAC Hemp Subcommittee, 10 Sep 2026): Nevada’s hemp registration system “is principally designed to regulate cultivation and the initial handling of hemp, not to identify every business manufacturing hemp-derived cannabinoid products sold in the state”. This explains why no official retail hemp-product sales total exists: the regulator that tracks cultivation (NDA) does not track retail hemp-product sales, and the CCB tracks licensed cannabis, not unlicensed hemp retail. National hemp context: US hemp production value $739 million in 2025 (NASS, via secondary reporting — §6.8.4). No Nevada share of that national value is published in the sources opened = [UNAVAILABLE]. No official Nevada CBD/hemp-product retail, beverage, or delta-8 sales series was found = [UNAVAILABLE]. Do not import national CBD market reports as Nevada figures.

6.10 Cannabis-friendly banks and credit unions in Nevada

Framing required: there is no federal cannabis banking law (given baseline). What exists in Nevada is (a) a state-chartered credit-union programme operating under FinCEN guidance and NCUA/FID examination, and (b) fintech platforms that route deposits to partner banks/credit unions. No official Nevada state list of cannabis-serving financial institutions was found (the Financial Institutions Division does not publish a cannabis-bank list in the sources opened). Every institution below is therefore a primary/marketing claim, labelled as such, not an official endorsement.

Confirmed by the institution’s own / local-press reporting (primary, not official regulator list)

  1. Greater Nevada Credit Union (GNCU), headquartered Carson City, NV. Began a cannabis banking pilot in 2020; expanded since; serves licensed dispensaries, cultivators, production companies, non-licensed ancillary firms (packaging, trade organisations), and has “a special program in place to support cannabis consumption lounges.” Services described: commercial deposit accounts, on-site cash management via integrated smart safes, electronic payments, treasury services; examined by NCUA and the Nevada Financial Institutions Division (FID); follows 2014 FinCEN guidance. Sources: NNBW, 25 Sep 2024; GNCU programme expansion release (4th anniversary, i.e. 2024) — both accessed 2026-10-31 [local press, with GNCU interviews; company release — marketing claim]; GNCU’s own cannabis page is cited in secondary listings as gncu.org/business/cannabis-business-banking/ [not directly opened in Run 1 — VERIFY]. Note: a GNCU-related secondary item states “There are 756 CRBs in the state, according to the Cannabis Compliance Board” — do not use 756 as a licence count — it counts cannabis-related businesses broadly (including ancillary firms), not CCB establishment licences (387 operational, §6.3). Flagged to prevent a Part 4/5 error.
  2. Safe Harbor Financial (SHF Holdings, Nasdaq: SHFS) — fintech, NOT a bank. Safe Harbor’s own FAQ: “Safe Harbor is a financial technology company, not a bank. We provide licensed cannabis operators with access to… banking accounts… Deposits are held by federally insured partner banks and credit unions and are FDIC or NCUA protected up to $250,000 per depositor, per ownership category.” Serves Nevada among 41 US states/territories listed; serves cultivators, processors, dispensaries, transporters, testing labs, hemp/CBD businesses. Marketing claim (company page): “more than $36 billion in funds processed since launching the nation’s first compliant program in 2015” and “the established leader in cannabis banking… in Nevada.” Sources: company FAQ and locations pages, accessed via search 2026-10-31 [company claims — marketing, not verified deposits or Nevada client counts]. Company news Oct 2026 (non-binding term sheet with Opifex Media; Nasdaq compliance period to 19 Apr 2027) — secondary company news; included only to show the provider’s current corporate status is in flux — VERIFY before recommending.

Aggregator list — UNVERIFIED, do not present as official. An aggregator page lists, for NV, among others: Amalgamated Bank, Bison State Bank, BlueSky Bank, Chase (CBD only), Greater Nevada Credit Union, Green Check (fintech), Herring Bank, KeyPoint Credit Union, Lighthouse Biz Solutions, Safe Harbor Financial, Salal Credit Union — accessed 2026-10-31 [aggregator, self-submission list (“Request to be listed”) — every name except GNCU/Safe Harbor above is [VERIFY] individually; the same page’s claim of “over 6,000 active cannabis-related licenses, according to the CCB” is wrong on its face against the CCB’s 387 operational licences and must not be repeated]. Banking access conclusion: Nevada licensees have at least one in-state credit-union programme (GNCU, since 2020) plus national fintech routing (Safe Harbor). No official count of how many Nevada licensees are banked, or what share of sales are cash vs banked, was found = [UNAVAILABLE].

6.12 Tourism context, lounge rollout, and federal-baseline cross-check

Tourism context (for Part 6 framing, not a sales share). Las Vegas visitation 38.5m in 2025 (−7.5% vs 2024’s 41.6m); 12 consecutive months of year-over-year visitor declines in 2025; December 2025 3.1m (−9.2% YoY) — LVCVA via secondary reporting, accessed 2026-10-31. The sales decline (FY25 −8.6%, FY26 −8.2% on DoT taxable sales) coincides with the visitor decline, price compression (§6.6), more states legalising, and a 20–30% illicit market (§6.7) — but no official source in Run 1 apportions the decline among those causes. Do not attribute a percentage of the decline to tourism. Gaming–cannabis separation (1,500-foot rule, no delivery to hotels/gaming properties, Gaming Control Board licensee restrictions since 2014) is presented by UNLV researchers as a structural drag on tourist-area sales (§6.2) — secondary reporting, VERIFY against the UNLV report and Gaming Control Board policy in Parts 9–10.

Lounge rollout in one line (detail §6.4). AB 341 (2021) → first state-licensed opening Feb 2024 → 2 corroborated operating state lounges + 1 listed-but-reported-closed + 1 tribal lounge in late 2026, against 40 prospective licences issued in 2022 and ~23 conditional licences still in pipeline (secondary). Lounge sales are included in DoT taxable sales (the DoT series is “Dispensaries and Consumption Lounges”) but are not broken out separately in the tables opened = lounge-only sales [UNAVAILABLE].

Federal-baseline cross-check — no contradiction found in official Nevada sources opened; two Nevada-specific qualifications confirmed. The given baseline is: (i) state-licensed medical + FDA-approved products → Schedule III effective 28 Apr 2026 (91 FR 22714); adult-use remains Schedule I; broader 2024 proposal not final; (ii) §280E generally lifted for Schedule III medical, adult-use fully subject; (iii) hemp PL 119-37 §781 effective 12 Nov 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 Dec 2026, full total-THC definition + 0.4 mg/container cap after 11 Dec 2026; (iv) USDA DEA-lab enforcement delayed to 31 Dec 2026; (v) medical appropriations rider through 11 Dec 2026; (vi) no federal banking law. Official Nevada confirmation (CCB Licensee Update, 20 May 2026, fetched directly): “On April 28, 2026, the United States Department of Justice published several federal actions…” — “The first action was a final order… that moved certain marijuana from Schedule I to Schedule III… The order applies to marijuana contained in FDA-approved drug products and marijuana subject to a qualifying ‘state medical marijuana license.’ Recreational/Adult-use marijuana remain Schedule I.” “Because Schedule III substances are not subject to Section 280E, qualifying medical cannabis activity potentially may become eligible for different federal tax treatment” — but “substantial uncertainty remains regarding… mixed-use operations, allocation of expenses between medical and adult-use activity, retroactive application… and implementation by the IRS and Treasury.” Nevada dual-licence qualification is unresolved (the key Nevada caveat): under NRS 678D.490 each adult-use establishment “is deemed a dual licensee” for medical activity. The CCB states this “presents a substantial and legitimate legal basis” for qualification, but “the Board cannot presently represent that the DEA has adopted, or will adopt, that interpretation” and the DEA “could interpret the federal definition more narrowly to require a separately designated medical license instrument.” Establishments holding active medical licences under NRS 678B.215 (primarily in jurisdictions prohibiting adult-use) “appear to present the clearest case.” Practical CCB guidance: maintain accurate separation of medical/adult-use activity in Metrc and point-of-sale (already required by Nevada law: every transaction designated medical or adult-use at point of sale, patient registry verified for medical transactions); consult tax counsel before amending returns; DEA registration pathway exists with a 60-day early-applicant period for interim operating protections, but “The Board cannot determine whether any Nevada licensee qualifies.” “The federal order does not itself legalize adult-use marijuana federally, nor does it resolve ongoing banking access issues” — confirming baseline point (vi) for Nevada. Source: CCB Federal Rescheduling Licensee Update, 20 May 2026, accessed 2026-10-31 (fetched directly, full text). Further official confirmation / risk flag (CCB minutes, 18 Jun 2026): CCB Executive Director update: DOJ April 2026 final order “placed FDA approval and state licensed medical marijuana into Schedule III, while recreational cannabis remains Schedule I and subject to 280E restrictions.” “The final order is under active challenge in the US Court of Appeals for the DC Circuit and petitioners have filed a motion asking the court to stay the order. If a stay is granted, the federal landscape could shift again, including a potential return of medical marijuana to Schedule I and the restoration of Section 280E…” Source: CCB minutes, 18 Jun 2026, accessed via search 2026-10-31 [official minutes — this is a litigation risk to the baseline, not a contradiction of it as of 31 Oct 2026; no stay outcome was found in Run 1 — VERIFY current DC Circuit status in Part 1]. A secondary note that the CCB + DoT said on 23 Apr 2026 that “Nevada’s state tax rules do not change” — secondary — VERIFY against the joint statement itself; consistent with Nevada having no state income tax to decouple from, so §280E is a federal-only issue for Nevada operators. Hemp / rider / DEA-lab points: no Nevada official source opened in the Part 6 pass contradicts the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 hemp staging, the 31 Dec 2026 DEA-lab delay, or the rider through 11 Dec 2026. Two imprecisions to not copy: (a) a CHAMPS report refers loosely to “a federal ban on hemp-derived THC products set to take effect in December” — secondary imprecision — the baseline’s staged 12 Nov / 11 Dec dates govern; (b) the CAC Hemp Subcommittee report (Sep 2026) predates the federal hemp dates and does not address them — do not cite it as a Nevada response to PL 119-37/119-103. Nevada’s formal response to the new federal hemp definition = [VERIFY — to be covered in Part 1 §6, not established by Part 6 sources]. Tribal note (for Part 10, flagged here because the baseline asks for it): the CCB rescheduling update does not address whether tribal medical activity under a state compact counts as “state-licensed” — that question remains [VERIFY] and must not be answered from Part 6 sources. NuWu Sky High is tribal and not CCB-licensed (§6.4), so its sales are not in CCB/DoT taxable sales as far as the sources show — but that exclusion is inferred from its non-CCB status, not stated in a DoT footnote opened here — [VERIFY].

Part 7 — Hemp licences / registrations

7.1 Governing authority and definitions (official statute text, opened directly)

The regulator is the State Department of Agriculture (NDA). NRS 557.130: “‘Department’ means the State Department of Agriculture.” NRS 557.140: “‘Grower’ means a person who is registered by the Department and produces hemp.” NRS 557.150: “‘Handler’ means a person who is registered by the Department pursuant to this chapter and handles hemp for processing into commodities, products or agricultural hemp seed.” NRS 557.170: “‘Producer’ means a person who is registered by the Department pursuant to this chapter and produces agricultural hemp seed.” NRS 557.110: “‘Agricultural hemp seed’ means the seed of any plant of the genus Cannabis that (1) is sold to or intended to be sold to a grower for planting; or (2) remains in an unprocessed or partially processed condition that is capable of germination.” Source: NRS Chapter 557 (leg.state.nv.us), accessed 2026-10-31.

NRS 557.160 (verbatim, operative definition, matching Run 1 §1.6 exactly): “‘Hemp’ means any plant of the genus Cannabis sativa L. and any part of such a plant, including, without limitation, the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a THC concentration that does not exceed the maximum THC concentration established by the Department for hemp. ‘Hemp’ does not include any commodity or product made using hemp.” Source: same NRS Chapter 557 page, accessed 2026-10-31. Nevada’s hemp definition is therefore Department-maximum-based, not a fixed 0.3% figure in the statute itself. NRS 557.180: “‘THC’ has the meaning ascribed to it in NRS 453.139.” Per Run 1 §1.6, NRS 453.139 includes delta-9 plus structural/optical/geometric isomers including delta-8, delta-7 and delta-10, so Nevada measures hemp THC on a total-isomer basis, not delta-9-only. [VERIFY] the current NRS 453.139 text against leg.state.nv.us before filing use, as Run 1 flagged.

NRS 557.190 exemptions: Chapter 557 does not apply to (1) a person who purchases, for resale, hemp or a commodity/product made using hemp which was not grown or processed by the person; (2) a person who transports such hemp/commodity/product not grown or processed by the person; or (3) a person described in NRS 580.520 purchasing/handling such material for NRS 580.520 activities — in each case if the person reasonably believes the material was grown/processed in compliance with Chapter 557. Source: same NRS Chapter 557 page, accessed 2026-10-31.

7.2 The three registration types — what each allows

There are exactly three NDA hemp registration types under NRS Chapter 557. No separate NDA hemp retail, distribution, transport or laboratory registration under Chapter 557 was found in either run.

RegistrationWhat it allowsPrerequisite / limitInfo link
GrowerProduces (grows) hemp — biomass, flower or fibre production (secondary description, Connor PLLC 2026 guide)None beyond the common requirements in §7.3; each site must be certified/registered (NRS 557.205)NRS 557.200, accessed 2026-10-31; NDA hemp hub: agri.nv.gov/Industrial_Hemp, accessed 2026-10-31 (hub contents not fully opened — see §7.6)
HandlerHandles hemp for processing into commodities, products or agricultural hemp seed (NRS 557.150). Agricultural processing methods only, including grinding, pressing, extraction and seed cleaning (2021/2024 handler forms, via search extract)Each facility requires a separate application. Hemp handled must have been grown by a Department-certified grower or seed producer, with documentation maintained by the handler. Handler must not handle Cannabis plants/parts known or with reason to know to be of a variety producing THC above acceptable limits as defined in NAC 557 on a dry-weight basis, unless otherwise approved by the CCB (handler-form condition — a form condition, not a general permission)Same NRS link; forms hub via USDA contact list: NDA hemp forms, accessed 2026-10-31
Producer (agricultural hemp seed producer)Produces agricultural hemp seed for planting/distributionMay not register as a producer unless also registered as a grower or handler (NRS 557.200(3)). Exceptions: a registered grower who retains seed solely under NRS 557.250(3), and a registered grower/handler who processes non-germinable Cannabis seed into commodities/products, need not register as a producer. Producer must comply with NRS 587.015–587.123 and regulations thereunder, plus Department seed packaging/labelling and certification rules (NRS 557.260, via search extract)Same NRS link, accessed 2026-10-31

What NDA registration does NOT authorise: (a) retail sale of intoxicating hemp-derived products — no Nevada retail pathway for intoxicating hemp-derived THC beverages was found in Run 1, and none was found in Run 2; (b) processed products for human consumption — that regime is NRS 439.532 / Department of Health and Human Services testing and labelling (Run 1 anchor, preserved); NDA itself states it does not regulate processed products for human consumption (secondary summary of the NDA programme, accessed 2026-10-31); (c) a CCB cannabis licence — CCB licensing under NRS 678B is a separate system; hemp registration is not a CCB licence.

Other Chapter 557 processes that are NOT licence types: NRS 557.225 (petition to review criminal history to obtain a licence; fee; report) is a pre-application review process, not a fourth registration type. Transfer of registration is governed by NRS 557.200(7) and NAC 557.112 (transfer approved by the Department on request, including on business-name or ownership change; transfer request must be accompanied by the fee for making a revision to an issued registration as set forth in NAC 557.150 — Cornell LII extract of NAC 557.112, accessed 2026-10-31).

7.3 Common requirements (all three types)

  • Application deadline — 1 July. NRS 557.200(4), verbatim in substance: a person who wishes to register “must, on or before July 1 of any year, submit to the Department the fee established pursuant to subsection 8 and an application, on a form prescribed by the Department,” including (a) applicant name/address, (b) business name/address if different, (c) land and crop management practices information, and (d) other information required by regulation. Source: NRS Chapter 557 page, accessed 2026-10-31. The 2024 grower form repeats “ALL APPLICATIONS ARE TO BE SUBMITTED ON OR BEFORE JULY 1st” (via search extract, accessed 2026-10-31). [VERIFY] whether the current 2026 NDA forms retain the same 1 July wording — the Connor PLLC 2026 guide says applications are “generally due by July 1 for the following year” (secondary, accessed 2026-10-31).
  • Expiry — 31 December, annual renewal. NRS 557.200(5): “Registration as a grower, handler or producer expires on December 31 of each year and may be renewed upon submission of an application for renewal” containing (a) proof of compliance with testing provisions, (b) proof land/crop management practices are adequate, consistent with prior submissions and do not negatively affect natural resources, and (c) other information required by regulation. Source: same NRS page, accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Surrender / non-renewal notice. NRS 557.200(6): a registrant intending to surrender or not renew must notify the Department not less than 30 days before surrender/expiry and submit a plan for effective disposal or eradication of existing live plants, viable seed or harvested crop. Source: same NRS page, accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Site registration — separate from personal registration. NRS 557.205: “Each site used for growing, handling or producing hemp in this State must be certified by and registered with the Department before growing, handling or producing hemp.” Source: same NRS page, accessed 2026-10-31. Grower forms add: a separate application for each noncontiguous field; a notarised landowner statement if the applicant is not the legal landowner (including date range, authorisation, and NDA staff inspection access); local-jurisdiction approval / zoning verification required, with failure to obtain appropriate jurisdictional approval at submission resulting in denial or revocation; a grower certificate is required prior to possession of any propagative hemp (2024 grower form, via search extract, accessed 2026-10-31). Handler forms likewise require site local-jurisdiction approval for zoning, water use, city/county ordinances and building codes.
  • Fingerprints / background check. NRS 557.200(9)–(10): for compliance with 7 CFR §990.6, each applicant to register or transfer a registration must submit a complete set of fingerprints and written permission authorising the Department to forward them to the Central Repository for Nevada Records of Criminal History and the FBI; in lieu, the applicant may submit documents/information required by Department regulation for a background check verifying eligibility under 7 CFR §990.6. Source: same NRS page, accessed 2026-10-31. NAC 557.102 requires a criminal history report for the applicant or, for a business entity, a key participant, and NAC 557.108 sets a period of ineligibility for certain felony convictions (with exceptions, including pre-20 December 2018 lawful pilot-programme growing) — NAC Chapter 557 table of contents and extracts via search, accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] the exact current criminal-history timing: the 2023/2024 grower forms require a report completed within 120 days immediately preceding submission (for the applicant or one key participant for a business entity), while the State Hemp Plan (revised 25 May 2021) describes a 10-year criminal history report under LCB T001-21 §10. These are different vintages and must not be merged; the current form controls, and it was not opened in full in this run.
  • Child-support statement. NRS 557.210: an applicant for registration or renewal must include their Social Security number and the Division of Social Services statement under NRS 425.520; registration may not be issued/renewed if the statement is missing or shows non-compliance with a child-support order/plan. Source: same NRS page, accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Records, inspection, sampling and testing. NRS 557.240 (retention of records by grower/handler; inspection of records; inspection and analysis of the growing crop; plan for disposal or remediation; additional inspection, sampling and testing; land and crop management practices) and NRS 557.270 (submission of hemp for testing; testing regulations; results; failing testing; retesting) — NRS Chapter 557 table of contents, same NRS page, accessed 2026-10-31. NAC 557.121 covers Department inspection, sampling and analysis of a registrant’s plants; NAC 557.136 covers testing procedures and the requirement of a report of results before a crop enters the stream of commerce; NAC 557.141 covers a disposal/remediation plan and destruction/remediation orders if a sample exceeds the acceptable hemp THC level; NAC 557.042 sets the maximum THC concentration / acceptable hemp THC level — NAC Chapter 557 table of contents via search, NAC Chapter 557, accessed 2026-10-31. Per Run 1 §1.6 (preserved): crop testing under NRS 557.270 + NAC 557.136 (R188-24) converts THCA to THC by post-decarboxylation or a similarly reliable method per 7 CFR §990.3. Harvest-report practice from NDA forms: approximately 45 days’ notice before intended harvest via a Hemp Harvest Report in the 2024 grower form; the harvest/inspection form states crops should be sampled/inspected a minimum of 15 days before harvest, 30 days’ notice is ideal, and harvested crops must not move from the indicated storage area until a Report of Analysis showing Total THC compliance is issued (form extracts via search, accessed 2026-10-31). [VERIFY] the current notice window (45 vs 15/30 days) against the current harvest-report form.
  • USDA reporting by growers. Under the State Hemp Plan, all certified Nevada growers must annually submit a production report to USDA Farm Service Agency (FSA); the State reports monthly to USDA via AMS-23 (producer report) and AMS-24 (disposal report) on the first of each month; grower reporting includes crop acreage (planted, harvested, disposed), grower certificate number, street address and geospatial location of each lot/greenhouse — State of Nevada Hemp Plan, revised 25 May 2021 (USDA AMS-hosted PDF, via search extract, accessed 2026-10-31). Whether FSA acreage data is publicly retrievable remains [VERIFY] (Run 1 gap, still open).
  • Penalties for unregistered activity. NRS 557.290: a person who grows/handles hemp or produces agricultural hemp seed without Department registration is subject to an administrative fine under NRS 557.280; NRS 557.280 authorises Department action (refusal, suspension, revocation) for violations of law, regulations or orders — NRS Chapter 557 table of contents, same NRS page, accessed 2026-10-31.

7.4 Fees — conflicting secondary figures, current regulation text NOT fully opened

The NAC Chapter 557 table of contents confirms NAC 557.150 “Fees” exists (NAC Chapter 557, accessed 2026-10-31), but the full NAC 557.150 text was not opened in this run because of the final-stage browser rate limit. NRS 557.200(8) requires only that fees be set by regulation “in an amount necessary to cover the costs of carrying out this chapter” — it states no dollar figure.

7.5 USDA-approved state plan vs USDA programme — ANSWER: Nevada runs its own USDA-approved state plan

Directly opened and confirmed: USDA AMS “List of USDA-approved Hemp Plans” (updated 17 February 2026) lists: “Nevada | NV Approved Plan (pdf) *” — where “Approved *” means “Plan approved under the final rule which took effect March 22, 2021.” The page states an approved plan “remains in effect unless withdrawn by the State or Tribal government or revoked by USDA pursuant to the procedures described in the Final Rule,” and that substantive revisions must be re-submitted for USDA approval. Source: USDA AMS approved hemp plans, opened directly, accessed 2026-10-31.

Therefore Nevada does NOT use USDA direct licensing (HeMP) for Nevada producers. USDA Hemp Producer Licences via HeMP are only for states/tribes without an approved plan (Run 1 §5.11, preserved). Do not search HeMP for Nevada USDA licences; the operative registry is NDA.

Plan approval date 28 May 2021 — verified against the NDA release, corroborated by the USDA list status: NDA press release 23 June 2021, as reproduced by Cannabis Law Report and Farm Progress: “The Nevada Department of Agriculture (NDA) State of Nevada Hemp Plan was approved by the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA) on May 28, 2021.” The plan document itself is titled “State of Nevada Hemp Plan,” “Revised May 25, 2021,” Hemp Program, Plant Industry Division, NDA, submitted to operate as a USDA-approved state under the Agriculture Improvement Act of 2018 (2018 Farm Bill). Sources: Cannabis Law Report, NDA hemp plan approval (via search, accessed 2026-10-31); plan PDF via USDA AMS (via search extract, accessed 2026-10-31). The USDA list page itself confirms approved status but does not print the 28 May 2021 date, so the date rests on the NDA release.

Plan contents (from the plan document extract): authority NRS 557, SB 347, SB 209, NAC Temporary Regulations 557 (LCB T001-21); elements include grower registration, crop sampling and chemical analysis, and remediation/disposal of non-compliant crops; grower reporting to USDA AMS or FSA per LCB T001-21 §9; monthly State reporting via AMS-23/AMS-24 (see §7.3).

R188-24 alignment work: NDA hearing notice for LCB File R188-24 (hearing 14 April 2026, 10:00 AM, 405 South 21st Street, Sparks, NV 89431; authority NRS 561.105 and NRS 233B.0608): “The NDA is required to maintain a USDA approved Hemp State Plan to enable hemp production and sale in Nevada pursuant to the 2018 Farm Bill. These changes are necessary to align state and federal definitions to maintain an approved Hemp State Plan. Although this regulation is current with federal regulation, the only entity enforcing the regulation is the State of Nevada through administration of the Nevada State Hemp Plan.” — via search extract, accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] R188-24’s final adoption and effective date: one legislative-review document in search results describes R188-24 as “adopted amendments to Nevada Administrative Code (NAC) Chapter 555,” while the January 2026 small-business statement describes it as proposed amendments to NAC Chapter 557. That chapter-number discrepancy is unresolved — do not cite R188-24 as adopted NAC 557 text until the adopted regulation is opened. The NAC Chapter 557 page header in search results still reads “[NAC-557 Revised Date: 7-22],” which does not by itself prove or disprove a 2026 amendment.

Tribal distinction on the same USDA page: the USDA list separately shows “Walker River Paiute Tribe | USDA Hemp Producer License” (tribal row), distinct from the State of Nevada approved plan. Source: USDA AMS approved hemp plans page, opened directly, accessed 2026-10-31. This is a tribal-programme entry, not evidence that Nevada state producers use HeMP.

History (secondary, background only — not law): SB 305 (2015, Governor approval 4 June 2015) authorised hemp growing; SB 396 (2017, Governor approval 1 June 2017) added handling; NAC Chapter 557 codified 10 March 2016 (R085-15); SB 347 and SB 209 (2019) revised statutes to align with the 2018 Farm Bill. Sources: hempcbdbusinessplans.com; NDA 2019 season presentation via Readkong; R085-15 (all via search, accessed 2026-10-31). Note: one secondary source phrases this as “codified in 2016 as Chapter 557 in the Nevada Administrative Code” — that phrasing must not be repeated as if NRS Chapter 557 (the statute, 2017) did not exist; NRS 557 is the statute, NAC 557 is the regulation.

7.6 Registry re-check (Run 1 §5.8 follow-up) — CONFIRMED UNAVAILABLE

Re-check performed this run: searches for an NDA registered-grower/handler list, the NDA hemp hub, and the USDA grower-contact list. No public NDA registry of hemp growers, handlers or producers was found.

NDA hemp hub located (not a registry): agri.nv.gov/Industrial_Hemp — search extract shows sections “Applications and Harvest Reports,” “Hemp Applications and Harvest Reports,” “Program Data and Resources,” “National Hemp Association,” “Nevada Hemp Regulations,” and “Industrial Hemp Staff Listing.” No registrant list appears among those sections in the extract. — via search, accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] the contents of the “Program Data and Resources” section by opening the hub directly (not opened in this run due to the final-stage rate limit); if it contains a registrant list, this finding must be corrected.

Forms hub (not a registry): NDA hemp forms — listed by USDA AMS as Nevada’s hemp programme contact page (USDA Hemp Program Contact Information, current as of March 2025, via search, accessed 2026-10-31). Personal contact details from that page are not reproduced here, per the commission’s no-personal-contact rule.

Old overview URL: NDA hemp overview (old) — cited by MJBizDaily 2019 and Atlas Seed as the NDA hemp page. It was not opened successfully in Run 1 and was not re-opened successfully in this run; do not cite it as live without re-checking. The current hub appears to be agri.nv.gov/Industrial_Hemp (above).

Not a registry — do not present as one: (a) the 2020 seed-producer form’s question asking whether farm name/county/city/email “may be listed on the NDA website for potential buyers/industry stakeholders” is an opt-in listing question on a form, not evidence of a current public registry; (b) Cannabiz Media’s undated tally of “175 licenses: 109 growers, 47 handlers, 19 seed producers” (Cannabiz Media, Nevada industrial hemp overview, via search, accessed 2026-10-31) is a private database count that conflicts sharply with the official 1 July 2026 count below and must not be used as a registry or a current count; (c) the secondary “16 registered hemp processing facilities as of 1 August 2025” figure (Run 1) uses different wording/date from “handlers” and is not a registry.

Official count anchor (unchanged, Run 1): 5 registered growers, 9 handlers, 0 seed producers at 1 July 2026 (NDA via Cannabis Advisory Commission Hemp Subcommittee Report, 10 September 2026, as reported in Run 1). No newer official count was found in this run.

Follow-up (unchanged from Run 1): contact NDA Plant Industry Division / Nevada public-records request; ask whether NDA publishes hemp certificates only on request.

7.7 Federal PL 119-37 / PL 119-103 staging — effect on Nevada hemp licensing

Baseline (fixed commission context, not re-researched): PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026, but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026. After 11 December 2026 the full total-THC hemp definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply. USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026. The federal medical marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026.

Nevada position as of the 2026-10-31 access date: No Nevada statute, regulation, NDA guidance or CCB guidance implementing the 12 November / 11 December 2026 dates, adopting a finished-product total-THC rule, or adopting/enforcing the 0.4 mg-per-container cap was found in opened official sources in Run 1, and none was found in this run. The CAC Hemp Subcommittee Report (10 September 2026) confirms the staging, quotes §2019, and recommends maintaining alignment with PL 119-37 (total THC + final-product restrictions) — a recommendation is not law.

What Nevada licensing therefore continues on, as of the access date: the NRS 557.160 Department-established-maximum framework (§7.1), the NAC 557.042 acceptable hemp THC level, and post-decarboxylation crop testing under NRS 557.270 / NAC 557.136 (§7.3). The Connor PLLC 2026 guide’s statement that NAC 557 revisions “emphasise pre-harvest testing protocols, total THC compliance, and record-keeping to align with USDA standards” (secondary, accessed 2026-10-31) does not cite a Nevada rule adopting the federal 0.4 mg/container cap or a finished-product total-THC rule, and the R188-24 hearing (April 2026) predates the federal staging dates.

Do not advise either way: this report does not state that Nevada hemp products automatically become federally unlawful on 12 November or 11 December 2026, nor the reverse. Whether/when Nevada amends NRS 557.160 / NAC 557.042 to adopt total THC (including THCA) for finished products, or adopts the 0.4 mg/container cap, remains [VERIFY] / [UNAVAILABLE].

Boundary preserved from Run 1: NDA registration covers growing and agricultural handling (grinding, pressing, extraction, seed cleaning) of raw hemp. It does not by itself authorise retail sale of intoxicating hemp-derived products, and it does not regulate processed products for human consumption (NRS 439.532 / DHHS regime).

Part 8 — Cannabis-zoned properties

8.1 Method, jurisdiction rules, and the headline finding

Sites searched: 420Property (Las Vegas and North Las Vegas business pages, national real-estate and business indexes), LoopNet / LoopNet UK, Crexi, CannaMLS, BizBuySell, BizTrader, plus general searches for Nevada hemp farm land and for Henderson, Carson City, Mesquite, Boulder City, Ely, Reno/Sparks and Pahrump. Official zoning maps/codes (Clark County Title 30, City of Las Vegas Title 19, Washoe County Development Code, parcel records) were not successfully consulted for any candidate in this run — zoning below comes from the listing/flyer/offering memorandum itself unless stated otherwise.

Jurisdiction rule applied throughout: The Las Vegas Strip and Paradise, Spring Valley, Enterprise and Winchester are unincorporated towns governed by Clark County, not the City of Las Vegas. Carson City is a consolidated municipality with no separate county. A mailing address saying “Las Vegas, NV” does not prove City of Las Vegas jurisdiction.

Same-name exclusions applied: No listing from Las Vegas NM, Henderson KY/TN, Carson City MI, Boulder CO, Mesquite TX, or Ely MN/UK is included. A Truth or Consequences, New Mexico listing on a 420Property index page was excluded as out of state.

Headline finding: No verified, street-address, fee-simple property was obtained with BOTH (a) confirmed active/recent status on 31 October 2026 from a live listing open and (b) official zoning-district verification. The closest candidate is 4333 W Tropicana Ave (Q2 2026 offering memorandum, Clark County jurisdiction, Industrial Light zoning stated in the memorandum), but its live-listing active status on 31 October 2026 was not confirmed because the final-stage page opens failed. Everything below is therefore labelled by evidence strength, and licence/business sales are kept strictly separate from fee-simple property.

Part 9 — Regulators and history

Method note for Parts 9–10: a browser.open attempt in this track was rate-limited (429, upstream unavailable) and was not repeated. This part therefore rests on search-result excerpts and previously opened material, not on fresh direct page opens in this session. Where a fact rests only on a search excerpt or a secondary source, it is flagged [VERIFY] and must not be presented as directly opened official text until re-opened. No live website visit is claimed for any page only searched.

9.1 Every state agency regulating cannabis/hemp

Cannabis Compliance Board (CCB) — the cannabis regulator

  • Created by AB 533 (2019), 80th Session; formally began operations 1 July 2020, taking over from the Department of Taxation’s Marijuana Enforcement Division, which no longer exists under Taxation. Modelled on the Nevada Gaming Control Board. Source: CCB Biennial Report 2025, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31 — CCB Biennial Report 2025; CCB FAQ, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31 — ccb.nv.gov/faq.
  • Function: licensing and enforcement for adult-use and medical cannabis establishments; investigations, audits, inspections, seed-to-sale tracking (Metrc), laboratory-test review, agent cards, vetting owners/officers/board members, transfers of interest, packaging approval. Source: CCB FAQ, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Board size: five board members appointed by the Governor, with expertise including finance/accounting, law enforcement, medicine, regulatory/legal compliance, and cannabis. Source: CCB “Meet the CCB” page, search result in prior work, accessed 2026-10-31 — Meet the CCB [VERIFY — direct page open failed in prior work; re-open before publishing as current.]
  • Board roster, mid-2026 (not a 31 Oct 2026 roster): Chair Maj. Gen. Ondra Berry (Ret.); Vice Chair Riana Durrett; Members Jerrie Merritt, Dr. Vicki Mazzorana, L. Kristopher Rath. Executive Director Michael Miles (announced as officially Executive Director at the 21 May 2026 meeting; Steve Gilbert became Deputy Director 18 May 2026, succeeding James Humm as Executive Director). The 18 June 2026 minutes list Berry, Merritt, Mazzorana and Rath present; the 6 Aug 2026 minutes list the same four present, Durrett not present. Sources: CCB minutes 21 May 2026, 18 June 2026, 6 Aug 2026, search excerpts, accessed 2026-10-31. Current roster as of 31 Oct 2026 [VERIFY]; do not publish the June/August roster as the October roster without a fresh CCB check.
  • Public offices (official, from CCB documents): Carson City Office, 3850 Arrowhead Drive, Suite 100, Carson City, NV 89706, main line (775) 687-6299; Las Vegas Office, 700 East Warm Springs Road, Suite 100, Las Vegas, NV 89119. Website: ccb.nv.gov. Source: CCB regulation notice R104-26, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31. Currency as of 31 Oct 2026 [VERIFY] before publishing as current contact details.
  • Regulation transition note, June 2026: CCB stated the Nevada Cannabis Compliance Regulations (NCCRs) have switched to Nevada Administrative Code (NAC) and NAC will be referenced on CCB’s website. Source: CCB minutes 18 June 2026, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31.

Cannabis Advisory Commission (CAC) — advisory only

  • Created by AB 533 (2019); 12-person commission that studies cannabis issues and makes recommendations to the CCB; recommendations do not bind the CCB. Composition under NRS 678A.300: CCB Executive Director as Chair; Director of the Department of Public Safety; Attorney General; Executive Director of the Department of Taxation; plus eight members appointed by the Governor for 2-year terms, eligible for reappointment, vacancy to be filled by the Governor not later than 90 days after vacancy. Gubernatorial categories: cultivation; retailing; laboratory sciences and toxicology; physician licensed under NRS Ch. 630 or 633 with medical-cannabis clinical/research experience OR public-health/food-safety experience; representative of an organisation advocating for medical-use patients; criminal-justice reform dealing with disproportionate impact of drug prosecutions on communities of colour; attorney licensed in Nevada experienced in cannabis-establishment or patient legal services. Source: NRS 678A.300 via search result, prior work, accessed 2026-10-31 — NRS 678A.300 (mirror) [VERIFY against current official NRS text before quoting verbatim.]
  • First meeting 13 July 2021; first members announced March 2021 (Benjamin Chew, DB Labs; James Hammer, Wallflower Cannabis House; A’Esha Goins, Cannabis Equity and Inclusion Community; Jillian Nelson, Evergreen Organix; Michael Nikhman, Nevada Group Wellness; Kema Ogden, Top Notch The Health Center; Dr. Jennifer Pearson, University of Nevada, Reno; Daniel Stewart, Hutchison & Steffen; plus ex officio CCB Executive Director Tyler Klimas (Chair), Attorney General Aaron D. Ford, DPS Director George Togliatti, Taxation Executive Director Melanie Young). Sources: CCB CAC releases, prior work, accessed 2026-10-31. Do NOT present this 2021 roster as current. Current CAC roster [VERIFY].
  • Subcommittees at first meeting: Social Equity, Diversity, and Inclusion; Public Health; Public Safety; Cannabis Industry and Market Stability. Later CAC work already in Run 1: Hemp Subcommittee Report 10 Sep 2026 (created by AB 76 §54.3) and Taxation of Cannabis Subcommittee Report 4 Sep 2026.
  • Current CAC roster, meeting schedule, and contact [VERIFY — not opened in this session].

Nevada Department of Taxation — cannabis tax, not licensing

  • Role: administers wholesale and retail cannabis excise tax and sales/use tax; formerly the pre-2020 cannabis regulator (Marijuana Enforcement Division) before CCB takeover 1 July 2020. Source: CCB FAQ, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31.
  • SB 41 (2025) Cannabis Tax Permit: every person who sells cannabis or cannabis products must obtain a Cannabis Tax Permit from the Department, separate from the CCB licence and separate from the Sales Tax Permit under NRS 360.597. Existing businesses were to submit a paper application no later than 1 Sep 2026; wholesale and retail cannabis tax accounts go live in My Nevada Tax 7 Dec 2026; the Department has 15 days from 1 Jan 2027 to issue the permit; permit is not assignable and is valid only for the person and location issued; if noncompliance is not cured by 1 Jan 2027, the Department must notify the CCB, which will automatically suspend the cannabis licence until compliance is achieved. Sources: Department of Taxation Cannabis Tax Permit FAQs, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31 — Taxation Cannabis Tax Permit FAQs; Tax Notes July 2025, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31. Confirm final SB 41 chapter/effective-date text on NELIS before quoting; the 1 July 2027 full-effect date in one secondary report conflicts with the 1 Jan 2027 permit date and is [VERIFY].
  • Public contacts (official): Website tax.nv.gov; Carson City 3850 Arrowhead Dr., Carson City, NV 89706, (775) 684-2000; Reno 9850 Double R Blvd., Reno, NV 89521, (775) 687-9999; Las Vegas 700 E. Warm Springs Road, 2nd Floor, Las Vegas, NV 89119, (702) 486-2300; Call Center (866) 962-3707, Mon–Fri 7:30 a.m.–5:00 p.m. Source: Tax Notes July 2025, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31.

Nevada Department of Agriculture (NDA) — hemp

  • Role: registers hemp growers, handlers and seed producers under NRS Chapter 557 and the USDA-approved Nevada State Hemp Plan; pre-harvest sampling/inspection by NDA a minimum of 30 days prior to harvest; crops may be harvested only after NDA sampling. Source: NDA hemp forms, search excerpts, accessed 2026-10-31 — NDA hemp forms (via USDA AMS contact list, accessed 2026-10-31).
  • Hemp plan dates (Run 1 anchor, re-stated not re-verified here): Nevada State Hemp Plan revised 25 May 2021, USDA approval 28 May 2021; AMS list updated 17 Feb 2026. Run 2 Part 7 re-verified the approved-plan status directly against the USDA list (§7.5).
  • 2026 NAC 557 rulemaking: NDA proposed amendments to NAC Chapter 557 (LCB File No. R188-24) to align state and federal definitions to maintain a federally compliant hemp programme so Nevada hemp can enter the national marketplace; workshop 3 Mar 2026; hearing 14 Apr 2026. Source: NDA workshop notice, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31. Final adopted text and effective date [VERIFY].
  • Public contacts (official, offices only — individual staff emails/phones found in search results are deliberately omitted per the task): Headquarters 405 South 21st Street, Sparks, NV 89431-5557, (775) 353-3601; Las Vegas Office 2300 East St. Louis Ave., Las Vegas, NV 89104, (702) 668-4590; Elko Office 4780 East Idaho Street, Elko, NV 89801, (775) 738-8076; website agri.nv.gov.
