Nevada Gaming Control Board and Nevada Gaming Commission — casino policy only

  • NGCB Industry Notice 2014-39, “Medical Marijuana Establishments,” 6 May 2014: “unless the federal law is changed, the Board does not believe investment or any other involvement in a medical marijuana facility or establishment by a person who has received a gaming approval or has applied for a gaming approval is consistent with the effective regulation of gaming. Further, the Board believes that any such investment or involvement by gaming licensees or applicants would tend to reflect discredit upon gaming in the State of Nevada.” Source: UNLV “1,500 Foot Wall” report, Dec 2025, search excerpt citing the notice, accessed 2026-10-31 — UNLV, The 1,500 Foot Wall [VERIFY against the notice PDF itself before quoting; the UNLV report’s footnote gives the Gaming URL, but the PDF was not directly opened in this session.]
  • Nevada Gaming Policy Committee Resolution (2018): “RESOLVED, the Committee reaffirms and recommends, based on provisions of the Nevada Gaming Control Act, Nevada Gaming Commission Regulations and federal law and regulations, that Nevada gaming licensees shall not participate in the marijuana industry.” Source: UNLV report, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Nature of the barrier: the investment/leasing bar is regulatory guidance enforced by NGCB, not statute, per UNLV: owners/investors in gaming businesses cannot own or invest in Nevada cannabis businesses, and gaming-licensed businesses cannot lease space to cannabis licensees. Sources: UNLV report, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31; Nevada Lawyer, Feb 2026, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Statutory separation (do not contradict Run 1): 1,500-foot setback from gaming operations in counties ≥100,000, imposed by the Legislature in 2019 via AB 533 (Nevada Lawyer, Feb 2026); reconcile with Run 1’s citation to NRS 678B.210/250 buffers, which are the current statutory text. Clark County delivery ban: Clark County Code §8.65.140 — “The delivery of cannabis and cannabis products to consumers is prohibited within the Las Vegas Boulevard gaming corridor or an H1 Zone as defined in Title 30 of this Code, and on the premises of any restricted or non-restricted gaming licensee” (quoted in UNLV report, Dec 2025). Sources: UNLV report and Nevada Lawyer, search excerpts, accessed 2026-10-31.
  • UNLV proposals (not law): revise CCB regulations and Clark County ordinances to allow delivery to hotels, short-term rentals and gaming-licensed properties; abolish the 1,500-foot setback in densely populated counties; update NGCB guidance to let gaming operators lease to cannabis retailers/lounges. Secondary reporting of UNLV figures conflicts: ~$540 million lost 2024 legal retail revenue ($210 million from the hotel/gaming delivery ban; ~$80 million lost annual tax revenue) vs a later secondary panel report citing $750 million annual retail+wholesale revenue loss. Flag the $540m vs $750m discrepancy; verify both against the UNLV report itself before publishing either as the report’s figure.
  • Gaming agency public contacts [VERIFY — no official Gaming contact page was opened in this session; do not invent a phone/email.]

Nevada Department of Public Safety (DPS) — background checks / agent cards

  • Role: DPS Director is an ex officio CAC member. CCB partners with DPS and the FBI to conduct background checks and reviews background reports before approval of an Agent Card application. Source: CCB Biennial Report 2025, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Agent cards: to work in the Nevada cannabis industry a person needs a CCB Agent Card for each category/role (production, cultivation, dispensary/retail store, distribution, laboratory, contractor); each card requires a $150 fee and a background check; background-check fees are managed by DPS; fingerprints are submitted through a CCB-approved process — Nevada residents at a DPS-approved Livescan facility, out-of-state residents by mailing fingerprints directly to DPS; CCB cannot provide a fingerprint form prior to application submission. Source: CCB FAQ, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31. [Secondary detail on $150/two-year validity is consistent across secondary guides but the statute/regulation text (NRS 678B.340; CCB Regulation 5) was not directly opened here — [VERIFY] before quoting fee/validity as current law.]
  • DPS cannabis-specific public contact page [VERIFY — not opened in this session.]

Nevada Attorney General — opinions and consumer protection

  • Role: Attorney General is an ex officio CAC member. CCB’s May 2026 rescheduling update states the Board was actively seeking clarification from DEA, the Nevada Attorney General’s Office, and other federal/state authorities (see §9.5). Under AB 504 (2025, Ch. 484, eff. 1 Oct 2025), Bureau of Consumer Protection enforcement under NRS 598 applies to unlicensed hemp sellers (Run 1 anchor). Source: CCB Federal Rescheduling Licensee Update, 20 May 2026, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31.
  • No specific Attorney General cannabis opinion was opened in this session. [VERIFY — search AG opinions index before listing any opinion number; do not invent one.]

9.5 Federal rescheduling — CCB’s own May 2026 position (relevant to Part 9 history and Part 10 tribal question)

  • CCB Licensee Update, 20 May 2026: on 28 Apr 2026 DOJ published three Federal Register actions; the final order signed by Acting Attorney General Todd Blanche moved certain marijuana from Schedule I to Schedule III — marijuana in FDA-approved drug products and marijuana subject to a qualifying “state medical marijuana license”; recreational/adult-use marijuana remains Schedule I. The order establishes a DEA registration pathway and a 60-day early-applicant registration period with interim operating protections while DEA review is pending. CCB states: “The Board cannot determine whether any Nevada licensee qualifies for DEA registration” and “many critical legal and operational questions remain unresolved”; CCB was seeking clarification from DEA, the Nevada Attorney General’s Office, and other authorities; several lawsuits challenging the actions had already been filed. Source: CCB Licensee Update, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31 — CCB Federal Rescheduling Licensee Update, 20 May 2026.
  • CCB board briefing, 18 June 2026: the final order was under active challenge in the U.S. Court of Appeals for the D.C. Circuit with a motion to stay (if granted, medical marijuana could return to Schedule I and 280E liability could be restored); DEA expedited hearing on broader (including adult-use) rescheduling began 29 June 2026, to conclude no later than 15 July 2026; no final determination on broader rescheduling had been made. Source: CCB minutes 18 June 2026, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31. Outcome of that hearing and of the D.C. Circuit stay motion as of 31 Oct 2026 [VERIFY — not found in this session; do not assume a decision.]
  • Correction to secondary sources: several secondary guides still describe marijuana as simply “Schedule I in 2026” or credit AB 422 / SB 235 with creating the CCB. Those do not supersede the commission baseline (medical Schedule III effective 28 Apr 2026; adult-use Schedule I) or Run 1 (AB 533 created the CCB). Do not use those secondary attributions in the dossier.

Part 10 — Tribal and interstate

10.1 Legal framework — SB 375 (2017)

  • SB 375, Chapter 305, Laws of 2017, enacted 23 May 2017, signed by Governor Brian Sandoval 2 June 2017, effective 2 June 2017, authorises the Governor to enter agreements with any federally recognised Indian tribe located within Nevada regarding marijuana. Source: Las Vegas Paiute compact recitals, directly opened in prior work, accessed 2026-10-31 — Las Vegas Paiute compact (CCB PDF).
  • Compact recitals state the federal Controlled Substances Act continued to designate marijuana as Schedule I at signing; Cole Memorandum 29 Aug 2013 (eight federal enforcement priorities, including preventing distribution to minors, preventing diversion to other states, preventing possession/use on federal property); Wilkinson Memorandum 28 Oct 2014 (Cole priorities guide enforcement in Indian Country; tribal governments treated the same as state governments in the decision to legalise). These memoranda are historic policy guidance, not current law in 2026, and must be labelled as such.
  • Compact expressly acknowledges: “pursuant to federal law, 21 U.S.C. §812, marijuana is a Schedule I controlled substance and that this Compact does not protect the sales or regulation of marijuana in Indian Country from federal law.” That 2017 statement must not be silently updated to the 2026 medical Schedule III baseline without a new official determination.

10.2 Las Vegas Paiute Tribe — compact partly read; remainder NOT re-read in this session

Document: Marijuana Compact Between Las Vegas Tribe of Paiute Indians of the Las Vegas Indian Colony, Nevada and the State of Nevada, hosted at CCB, Las Vegas Paiute compact PDF — directly opened in prior work, lines 0–422 of 4,121 read. Lines 422–4,121 were NOT re-read in this session because the re-open attempt was rate-limited and not to be repeated. Tax, testing, tracking, trade, term/termination, and dispute provisions beyond line 422 are therefore [UNAVAILABLE in this return] and must be extracted from the compact PDF before the dossier is finalised. Do not fill them from secondary summaries.

Parties/definitions already read (lines 0–422):

  • Tribe: Las Vegas Tribe of Paiute Indians of the Las Vegas Indian Colony, federally recognised, possessed of full sovereign powers of a government; located on the Las Vegas Indian Colony in Nevada.
  • State authority at signing: Nevada Department of Taxation, under NRS Chapters 453A and 453D (pre-Title 56 numbering; now 678A–678D — note the renumbering, do not present 453A/453D as current chapter numbers).
  • “Indian Country” = lands of the Las Vegas Paiute Tribe, including the Las Vegas Indian Colony Reservation and all lands held in trust or restricted fee status by the United States for the Tribe or its Tribal Members.
  • “LVPCA” = Las Vegas Paiute Cannabis Authority, an agency of the Tribe — the tribal regulator named in the compact.
  • “LVPTC” = Las Vegas Paiute Tribal Code; tribal marijuana provisions are LVPTC Title 19 and 19A (as existing on the compact date, attached as Exhibit A; current copies to be made available to the State on request; Tribe to notify the State within 10 days of any LVPTC change affecting Marijuana Products).
  • “Marijuana,” “edible marijuana products,” “marijuana concentrates,” “marijuana-infused products,” “useable marijuana” have the same meanings as in NRS Chapters 453, 453A, and 453D.
  • “Marijuana Cultivation Facility,” “Marijuana Product Manufacturing Facility,” “Retail Marijuana Store” defined by reference to LVPTC §19A-1-30(g), (o), and (p) respectively.
  • “State Licensee” = any marijuana product manufacturing facility, cultivation facility, distributor, testing facility, or retail marijuana store licensed by the State. “State Tax” = any marijuana excise tax or sales and use tax imposed by the State. “Tribal Tax” = a tax imposed by the Tribe. “Tribal Enterprise” = LVPCA or other business/agency owned in whole or in part by the Tribe and authorised to sell marijuana products under the LVPTC.

Applicability / operative framework already read (verbatim substance): Compact applies to cultivation, processing, and sale of Marijuana Products in Indian Country where the Tribe or Tribal Enterprise (i) delivers, causes delivery to be made to, or receives delivery of Marijuana Products from a State Licensee, or (ii) physically transfers possession from seller to buyer within Indian Country. Except as otherwise provided, cultivation, processing, sale, and possession in Indian Country pursuant to the LVPTC and in accordance with the Compact are not subject to NRS 453A, NRS 453D, NAC 453A, or other regulations under those chapters, and will not be a criminal or civil offense under Nevada state law.

Tribal law standard already read: parties acknowledge tribal laws regarding Marijuana Products are, in all material ways relating to testing, labeling, tracking and packaging, at least as restrictive as NRS Chapters 453A and 453D and regulations thereunder, and the tribal government is enforcing or will enforce those laws.

Retail sales mechanics already read: Tribe/Tribal Enterprises may sell Marijuana Products in Indian Country pursuant to the LVPTC and Compact. Tribe must notify the Department at least 30 days prior to opening any Retail Marijuana Store, including operator identity, location, and certification that the store is located in Indian Country. Retail sales must be conducted in accordance with LVPTC Title 19 and 19A and internal policies/controls of the Tribe/Tribal Enterprise. All Marijuana Products purchased by a Retail Marijuana Store from a State Licensee will be verified upon delivery in Indian Country, confirmation of receipt by executing the delivery invoice, and input into the State’s tracking system within 24 hours of delivery. Products purchased from / sold to another federally recognised tribe with a reservation in Nevada will be recorded in either the Tribe’s or the State’s tracking system within 24 hours of receipt/delivery (line 422 cut off mid-sentence — resume there).

LVPTC Title 19/19A operative text: not publicly opened in this session. [VERIFY — obtain from the Tribe / compact Exhibit A; do not quote tribal ordinance text from secondary sources.]

Operations (secondary only, flagged): NuWu Cannabis Marketplace, Las Vegas Paiute Tribe, opened October 2017 (one secondary account says a 31-acre marketplace opened 16 Oct 2017); size reported as both 15,800 sq ft and 15,500 sq ft — flag the discrepancy, do not pick one without a tribal source. Second dispensary NuWu North near Snow Mountain / northwest Las Vegas Valley, opened early 2018 / January 2018 in secondary accounts. Drive-thru added December 2017, reported as Nevada’s only dispensary drive-thru because state law prevents non-tribal dispensaries from having drive-thrus — secondary claim, [VERIFY]. First cannabis tasting room in Nevada opened 5 Oct 2019 (reported 9 Oct 2019) inside NuWu Cannabis Marketplace, two blocks north of downtown Las Vegas — secondary, [VERIFY]. NuWu Sky High Lounge is the tribal consumption lounge, operating, not CCB-licensed, not counted in the CCB lounge total (Run 1 anchor). Former tribal chairman Benny Tso and chairman Chris Spotted Eagle working with Governor Sandoval’s office on SB 375 — secondary, not a legal source.

Current NuWu operating status, locations, and hours as of 31 Oct 2026 [VERIFY — no tribal/official source opened in this session].

Tribal medical-card reciprocity — secondary only, [VERIFY]

An Inter-Tribal Cannabis Commission (ITCC) FAQ (secondary) states: purchases at a tribal dispensary are within the allowable amount even if the buyer also bought at a State dispensary; marijuana purchased at a tribal dispensary may be taken anywhere within Nevada allowed by state law; medical marijuana may be bought at most tribal dispensaries; testing standards are the same throughout Nevada; anyone over 21 may buy at a tribal dispensary (no tribal membership required), with driver’s licence or state/federal ID (medical and ID cards from other Tribes also accepted); state and Tribal taxes are similar if not the same, but Tribal taxes go to Tribal community improvement programmes and fund the Tribal Marijuana Enforcement Division, described as a nonprofit third-party regulator ensuring compliance between Tribal and State programmes; State and Tribal laws are similar but not identical; allowable personal possession is the same. The same FAQ lists Tribal Medical Marijuana Cards recognised by Nevada as: Ely Shoshone Tribe, Yerington Paiute Tribe, Winnemucca Indian Colony, Walker River Paiute Tribe, Lovelock Paiute Tribe, Timbisha Shoshone Tribe. Source (secondary), prior work, accessed 2026-10-31 — ITCC FAQ — All secondary; for legal points, only official tribal/government sources and compact texts may be used. The state-law medical reciprocity rule itself is NRS 678C.470 (see §10.6), which is distinct from tribal-card recognition.

Ely secondary reporting also states tribal marijuana cards issued under tribal regulatory codes “are also accepted for reciprocity with the state of Nevada” (Tribal Cannabis Consulting, 2017). Secondary, [VERIFY] against DPBH/CCB guidance.

10.4 Federally recognised tribes and colonies — count discrepancy, do not smooth over

  • Nevada Department of Native American Affairs (DNAA) Executive Director, Senate Committee on Natural Resources minutes, 20 Mar 2025: “28 federally recognized tribal nations, bands, colonies and communities” and “28 sovereign governments,” but in the same testimony: “Based on the federal government’s description of what a federally recognized tribe is, Nevada has 20 federally recognized tribal nations,” with Washoe Tribe of Nevada and California and Te-Moak Tribe of Western Shoshone having different structures (Washoe community band councils at Carson, Dresslerville, Stewart, Woodfords/Gardnerville; Te-Moak constituent bands Battle Mountain, Elko, South Fork, Wells). Same testimony notes about 62,000 self-identified Urban Indians living in Nevada. Source (official, via search), prior work, accessed 2026-10-31 — Senate Natural Resources minutes, 20 Mar 2025.
  • Secondary Nevada-tribes article: 21 federally recognised tribes, spanning 28 separate reservations, bands, colonies, and community councils; five larger peoples: Numa (Northern Paiute), Newe (Western Shoshone), Nuwuvi (Southern Paiute), Wá∙šiw (Washoe), Mojave People (including Ft. Mojave and Pipa Aha Macav); more than 1.6 million acres of tribal land in Nevada, 97% rural. Secondary; conflicts with DNAA’s 20/28 figures.
  • Bureau of Indian Affairs, Western Region, Tribes Served page (official, via search) lists under Eastern Nevada Agency, Western Nevada Agency, and Southern Paiute Agency, among others: Te-Moak Tribe of Western Shoshone (Battle Mountain, Elko, South Fork, Wells Bands); Ely Shoshone Tribe; Confederated Tribes of the Goshute Indian Reservation; Shoshone-Paiute Tribes of the Duck Valley Indian Reservation; Duckwater Shoshone Tribe; Washoe Tribe of Nevada and California (Carson, Reno-Sparks, Stewart, Woodfords, Dresslerville Community Councils); Fallon Paiute Shoshone Tribe; Fort McDermitt Paiute and Shoshone Tribe; Lovelock Paiute Tribe; Pyramid Lake Paiute Tribe; Walker River Paiute Tribe; Winnemucca Paiute and Shoshone Tribe; Summit Lake Paiute Tribe; Yerington Paiute Tribe; Yomba Shoshone Tribe; Las Vegas Paiute Tribe; Moapa Band of Paiutes. Source (official, via search), prior work, accessed 2026-10-31 — BIA Western Region, Tribes Served.
  • Final-report rule: present the BIA/DNAA list, explain the 20 nations vs 28 nations/bands/colonies/communities distinction explicitly, and do not publish a single unqualified “Nevada has X tribes” figure. A 2017 Governor’s-office statement that SB 375 was “supported by all of Nevada’s 27 tribes” is a third, historic figure — do not reconcile it by picking one number.

10.5 Tribal Schedule III question — remains [VERIFY]

  • No official determination was found in this session on whether tribal medical activity under a state compact counts as “state-licensed” under the federal Schedule III rule effective 28 Apr 2026. Keep as [VERIFY]; do not answer from inference.
  • Facts that do not answer it: (1) CCB October 2026 split-payment guidance says tribal establishments are not licensed by the CCB and tribal products manufactured on tribal land under tribal authorisation are “treated the same as state licensed products” for split-payment purposes (Run 1) — that is a CCB payment-treatment statement, not a federal DEA/DOJ determination. (2) The Las Vegas Paiute compact says tribal activity under the LVPTC/Compact is not subject to NRS 453A/453D and is not a state-law offense, and tribal law is at least as restrictive as state law on testing/labelling/tracking/packaging — again, not a federal “state medical marijuana license” determination. (3) CCB’s 20 May 2026 update expressly states the Board “cannot determine whether any Nevada licensee qualifies for DEA registration” and was seeking clarification from DEA and the Attorney General’s Office (see §9.5). If CCB cannot determine qualification for its own licensees, tribal qualification is a fortiori unresolved on this record.
  • Dual-licensee consequence (commission baseline, not new research): Schedule III and 280E relief apply to medical activity only; dual licensees must split medical vs adult-use activity; all adult-use activity, including consumption lounges, remains Schedule I and fully subject to 280E. Tribal lounges (NuWu Sky High) are adult-use/tribal operations, not CCB medical licensees, and no 280E relief for them is established on this record.

10.6 Interstate issues

Who may buy in Nevada; tourist / out-of-state buyers

  • Adult-use: any adult 21+ with valid government-issued photo ID may buy in Nevada, regardless of state of residence; purchase must be in-state, in Nevada. Possession limit (Run 1 anchor): 2.5 oz flower / 1/4 oz concentrate. Retail sale requires government-issued valid identification (per CCB Regulation 7.020, cited in a dispensary FAQ, secondary — [VERIFY] against current NAC text). Source (secondary visitor guide), accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Medical reciprocity — NRS 678C.470 (verbatim substance from a statute mirror; [VERIFY] against current official NRS text before quoting): “(1) A person who is not a resident of this State, but who is authorized to engage in the medical use of cannabis under the laws of his or her state or jurisdiction of residence, is deemed to hold a valid registry identification card for the purpose of the exemption from state prosecution described in subsection 1 of NRS 678C.200 if the person abides by the legal limits on the possession, delivery and production of cannabis for medical purposes in this State, as set forth in NRS 678C.200. (2) A medical cannabis dispensary may dispense cannabis to a person described in subsection 1 if the person presents to the medical cannabis dispensary any document which is valid to prove the authorization of the person to engage in the medical use of cannabis under the laws of his or her state or jurisdiction of residence.” Added 2019, ch. 3827. Source: Justia mirror of NRS 678C.470 (2025), search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31 — NRS 678C.470 (Justia mirror) — Mirror, not the official Legislature site; re-check leg.state.nv.us.
  • Nevada itself issues medical cards to residents only; a visitor cannot apply for a Nevada card, but may be served on home-state authorisation (secondary guide consistent with NRS 678C.470, accessed 2026-10-31). No reciprocal agreement is needed for Nevada to serve a visitor; conversely, whether another state accepts a Nevada card depends on that state’s law — do not assert Nevada-card acceptance elsewhere without checking that state.
  • No official tourist share of Nevada sales was established in Run 1, and none is established here. Do not estimate one.

Neighbouring-state contrast — secondary summary only; each neighbour’s current law [VERIFY] from that state’s official source if contrasted

  • California: adult-use legal (21+, any-state ID in the visitor guide list). Arizona: adult-use legal (21+, any-state ID in the visitor guide list). Utah: medical only, no recreational use permitted under state law; visiting-patient programme (21-day card, $15, per secondary guide). Idaho: no store sells to visitors; listed among states with no legal medical/adult-use programme in secondary guides. Sources (secondary visitor guide, 25 Oct 2026; Utah contrast, secondary), accessed 2026-10-31.
  • The federal rule overrides the neighbour contrast: a purchase legal in Nevada is not legal to carry home — see §10.7. Do not advise otherwise.

10.7 What can / cannot be taken across state lines or onto federal land — state the conflict plainly

  • Interstate transport: adult-use cannabis remains Schedule I federally; carrying it across a state line is a federal crime even between two states that allow it, and even if bought legally in Nevada. Medical status does not create interstate immunity: a state medical card is valid only within the issuing state except where another state accepts it, and it does not protect the holder at federal checkpoints. Sources (secondary guides consistent on the principle), accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Penalty figures [VERIFY — do NOT publish]: secondary sources conflict (up to 5 years / $250,000 for less than 50 kg trafficking vs fines up to $5,000 and 6 months for simple federal possession contexts). No penalty figure was verified against 21 U.S.C. §§841, 844 in this session. Do not publish either figure.
  • Airports / Harry Reid International Airport (LAS), Las Vegas, NV (formerly McCarran):
    • TSA’s stated position (via secondary summaries of TSA’s Medical Marijuana page): TSA officers do not search for marijuana or other illegal drugs, but if an illegal substance is discovered during screening, TSA will refer the matter to a law enforcement officer; products with no more than 0.3% THC on a dry-weight basis or FDA-approved products fall within TSA’s stated exception. TSA page itself not directly opened here — [VERIFY] against tsa.gov before quoting. Note the secondary guides’ “Schedule I” framing predates or ignores the 28 Apr 2026 medical Schedule III baseline; the baseline distinction (medical Schedule III vs adult-use Schedule I) governs, and TSA guidance may not yet reflect it — flag that tension, do not resolve it by assertion.
    • Clark County airport ban (official ordinance, via search): Clark County Code Title 20, §20.04.090 (Ordinance, proposed 6 June 2017, passed 5 Sep 2017) makes it unlawful to possess, consume, use, display, transfer, distribute, sell, transport or grow marijuana in or upon any airport or Department of Aviation property (buildings, land, terminals, parking, ground transportation, roadways, air operations areas, hangars, warehouses, offices, commercial properties) or to advertise a marijuana business there (vehicle-permit exception for advertising). Violation: misdemeanor or civil fines, or both. Source: Clark County ordinance PDF via Harry Reid Airport site, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31.
    • Amnesty boxes (secondary, with an official-ordinance context): green “Disposal for Prescription and Recreational Drugs” boxes at Harry Reid International — secondary reports give ~10 boxes in terminals plus 3 at an airport-owned car-rental facility (one 2018 report: 20 boxes at McCarran plus 2 other Clark County airports; counts conflict). Boxes are described as bolted, locked, one-way, emptied by authorised personnel, for voluntary disposal before security; amnesty is a local policy decision, not a change in federal law and not federal immunity. Box existence, current locations, and operating authority at Harry Reid as of 31 Oct 2026 [VERIFY] against an official Clark County Department of Aviation source; secondary counts conflict and must not be published as a current count. Sources (secondary), accessed 2026-10-31.
    • Denver International is contrasted in secondary sources as banning cannabis on airport property with no amnesty boxes; Chicago O’Hare/Midway as having boxes. Secondary contrast only.
  • Hoover Dam (Bureau of Reclamation, federal property, ~30 miles southeast of Las Vegas, on the Nevada–Arizona border): “Marijuana is prohibited at Hoover Dam per 43 CFR 423.44.” Open daily 5:00 a.m.–9:00 p.m. (PST); vehicles inspected at the Nevada Security Checkpoint; refusal means no entry. Source: Bureau of Reclamation Hoover Dam visitor page, search excerpt, accessed 2026-10-31 — USBR Hoover Dam visitor page — This is an official-agency page via search excerpt, not a direct open in this session; re-open before quoting 43 CFR 423.44 verbatim, and check whether the regulation text itself, rather than the visitor page, should be cited.
  • Lake Mead National Recreation Area (National Park Service, federal land/waters): NPS states the Lake Mead Superintendent’s Compendium applies to all lands and waters administered by NPS within Lake Mead NRA boundaries, under 36 CFR Parts 1–7, and that the Compendium does not repeat 36 CFR/U.S.C. provisions enforced without further elaboration at park level. No Lake Mead-specific marijuana clause was extracted from the Compendium in this session. The general NPS position (illustrated by Crater Lake’s published policy, not a Lake Mead policy) is that state marijuana law “has no bearing on federal laws and regulations” in a national park and possession/use of any amount, even medical with a valid state card, is prohibited, punishable by up to $5,000 for an individual (18 U.S.C. §§3559, 3571). Do NOT transfer Crater Lake’s penalty statement to Lake Mead as a Lake Mead rule. Lake Mead-specific marijuana clause [VERIFY — open the Lake Mead Compendium PDF / 36 CFR 1.5, 2.35, 7.48]. Sources, accessed 2026-10-31 — NPS Lake Mead regulations and Superintendent’s Compendium pages; NPS Crater Lake laws/policies page (general NPS principle only).
  • Federal-property principle from the compact era (historic, not a current Lake Mead/Hoover Dam regulation): the Cole priorities included preventing marijuana possession/use on federal property and growing on public lands (2013 guidance). Do not cite Cole as the current Lake Mead/Hoover Dam rule.

10.8 Hemp shipments — [UNAVAILABLE for Nevada-specific rules]

  • No Nevada-specific hemp-shipment statute, NDA shipment/manifest guidance, or interstate hemp agreement was opened in this session. The general federal framework (2018 Farm Bill 0.3% delta-9 definition before 11 Dec 2026; post-11 Dec 2026 total-THC / 0.4 mg per container cap per the commission baseline) must not be presented as a Nevada shipment licence or safe harbour.
  • NDA’s 2026 NAC 557 purpose statement — aligning state/federal definitions “to maintain a federally compliant hemp program… which enables Nevada hemp to enter the national marketplace” (see §9.1) — is the closest Nevada statement found, and it is a rulemaking purpose, not a shipment rule.
  • Required follow-up: search NDA / USDA AMS hemp interstate-transport guidance and any Nevada hemp-shipment/manifest rule, or carry as [UNAVAILABLE] in the dossier.

10.9 Border markets — partial, secondary; no sales or enforcement data

  • Mesquite, NV (Clark County — not Mesquite, TX): Run 1 Part 3 rules apply — IR-1/IR-2 zones only, conditional-use permit, 1 dispensary per 25,000 combined Mesquite + Bunkerville population, no drive-through, no outside storage, lounge opt-out. A Deep Roots Harvest Mesquite store (195 Willis Carrier Canyon, Mesquite, NV 89027) is listed in that operator’s own store list (secondary/operator source), accessed 2026-10-31. CCB licence confirmation for that address [VERIFY] against the CCB List of Licensees.
  • West Wendover, NV (Elko County, Utah border, I-80; Mountain Time by federal DOT order, unlike the rest of Nevada): Deep Roots Harvest West Wendover, 395 Industrial Way, West Wendover, NV 89883, listed as active (operator source, hours Sun–Thu 9 a.m.–8 p.m., Fri–Sat 9 a.m.–12 p.m. in one listing; 10 a.m.–8 p.m. / 10 p.m. in another — hours conflict, [VERIFY]). West Wendover is described in secondary sources as home to five casinos and a cannabis dispensary attracting visitors from neighbouring Utah, where casino gambling and non-medical cannabis are illegal. Sources (operator/secondary), accessed 2026-10-31. CCB licence confirmation and any border-market sales/enforcement data [VERIFY]; do not estimate West Wendover sales or Utah-visitor share.
  • Laughlin (unincorporated Clark County, Colorado River / Arizona border), Primm (unincorporated Clark County, I-15 California border), Jackpot (Elko County, Idaho border): no retail presence, licence count, or county zoning finding was made in this session — [UNAVAILABLE]. Check the CCB List of Licensees (updated 26 Oct 2026 per Run 1) and county zoning before asserting any store exists or does not exist in each town. Do not estimate border-market sales for any of these towns.
  • Ely note (not a border market, but a tribal-market data point): Tsaa Nesunkwa was described in 2018 as “the only marijuana dispensary in Eastern Nevada” and “Elko’s closest marijuana dispensary, 188 miles south on U.S. Highway 93” — historic secondary claim, not a current market finding.

10.10 Interstate laws / agreements — none found

  • No Nevada interstate cannabis commerce law or agreement was found in this session. The Las Vegas Paiute compact’s inter-tribal trade provision (Nevada-tribe to Nevada-tribe recording in the Tribe’s or State’s tracking system within 24 hours) is intra-state tribal trade, not interstate commerce.
  • Any claim of a Nevada–California, Nevada–Arizona, or multi-state cannabis compact must be verified or marked as none found; do not import other states’ interstate-commerce trigger laws as Nevada law.
  • Gaming separation reminder (Run 1, not contradicted): 1,500-foot rule in counties ≥100,000 from a nonrestricted gaming licensee; delivery-to-hotel/gaming restrictions including Clark County Code §8.65.140; Gaming Control Board policy since 2014 that gaming licensees must have no involvement with cannabis while federally illegal. Note the tension, flagged not resolved: the 2026 medical Schedule III baseline does not by itself change Nevada’s gaming-separation rules, and secondary UNLV panel reporting (May 2026) states rescheduling “has not yet triggered a change in Nevada’s rigid regulations” — NGCB/Commission confirmation of any post-April-2026 gaming-guidance change [VERIFY].

Part 11 — Operations rules

Consistency statement: Checked against the Run 1 file. No Run 1 anchor is contradicted. Anchors preserved: adult-use 2.5 oz / ¼ oz, per-serving edible 10 mg, per-package caps (capsule/tincture/other 1,000 mg, edible 100 mg, concentrate 3,500 mg); Metrc seed-to-sale; agent cards $150; NRS 678B.390 fees (retail $5,000/$20,000/$5,000, cultivation/production $5,000/$3,000/$1,000, distributor $5,000/$15,000/$5,000, lab $5,000/$5,000/$3,000, lounges $100,000/$10,000 retail and $10,000/$10,000 independent, $2,500 social-equity application); CCB took over 1 July 2020; lounges 2 corroborated operating state + 1 listed-but-reported-closed + 1 tribal. Where a Part 11/12 source was a search return, statute mirror, or secondary summary rather than a direct official open, that is flagged — do not upgrade those items to confirmed without a direct re-check.

11.1 Testing: required tests, sampling, failed tests and recalls, lab enforcement, lab list

Who tests, and accreditation

  • A cannabis independent testing laboratory must be licensed by the CCB to test cannabis in Nevada. Source: nevadastatecannabis.org, testing lab — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • NRS 678B.290: the Board shall establish standards for and certify one or more cannabis independent testing laboratories to test adult-use and medical cannabis/products sold in the State, able to determine THC and cannabidiol concentration, microbes/molds/fungi, composition, and chemicals including pesticides, heavy metals, herbicides or growth regulators. Source: NRS 678B.290 (LawServer mirror) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • To obtain a lab licence the applicant must agree to become accredited under ISO/IEC 17025 within 1 year after licensure by an impartial organisation operating under ISO/IEC 17011 and a signatory to the International Laboratory Accreditation Cooperation Mutual Recognition Arrangement. Same source — accessed 2026-10-31. CCB Regulation 11, §11.020 states the same 1-year agreement, with scope covering all analytes under NCCR 11.050. Source: CCB Regulation 11 (2021) — accessed 2026-10-31.

Required quality-assurance tests

  • Regulation 11, §§11.050–11.065 is the required-test framework. Source: CCB Regulation 11 (2023) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Categories returned in search, by product type, include: moisture content, potency analysis, terpene analysis, homogeneity of potency for edibles (§11.060), mycotoxin screening, heavy-metal screening, pesticide-residue analysis, herbicide screening, growth-regulator screening, total yeast and mold, total Enterobacteriaceae, Salmonella, pathogenic E. coli, Aspergillus fumigatus, Aspergillus flavus, Aspergillus terreus, Aspergillus niger, plus foreign-matter inspection, water activity and residual solvents where applicable. Sources: nevadastatecannabis.org testing-lab page and CCB Regulation 11 (2021) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Limits excerpted from Regulation 11 in search: moisture <15% for usable cannabis/infused pre-rolls; mycotoxins <20 µg/kg total aflatoxins B1, B2, G1, G2 combined and <20 µg/kg ochratoxin A; heavy metals arsenic <2 ppm, cadmium <0.82 ppm, lead <1.2 ppm, mercury <0.4 ppm; total yeast and mold <10,000 CFU/g. Pesticide/herbicide/growth-regulator limits are stated as “See NAC 555.640” in that excerpt. [VERIFY] — full current NAC 678B table should be re-checked on leg.state.nv.us before republication, because Regulation 11 is being recodified from NCCR to NAC and amended by R104-26 / R152-24.
  • A lab must file an electronic copy of the certificate of analysis (COA) for all tests, regardless of outcome, at the same time it transmits results to the submitting facility — pass to the CCB pass address, fail to the CCB fail address (addresses omitted here; they are in Regulation 11). Source: CCB Regulation 11 (2023) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Proficiency testing: Regulation 11 §11.040 requires Board proficiency-testing participation; failure to achieve an acceptable score for a required test requires written notice to the appropriate Board Agent within 24 hours and repeat proficiency testing within 30 calendar days or as otherwise approved; unsuccessful participation is grounds for limitation, suspension or revocation. Source: CCB Regulation 11 (2023) — accessed 2026-10-31.

Sampling — current law and pending change

  • SB 157 (2025), codified at NRS 678B.649, effective 1 October 2025: “lot” = (1) flowers from one or more plants of the same harvest batch, ≤15 pounds; (2) leaves/other plant matter other than full female flowers, ≤45 pounds; or (3) wet flower/leaves/other plant matter used only for extraction, ≤150 pounds, within 2 hours of harvest. Representative samples of each lot must be tested by a cannabis independent testing laboratory, with collection aligned with the most recent ASTM D8334/D8334M standard. Sources: CCB public comment, 15 Oct 2026 and LCB Register R104-26P — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Pending implementation — [VERIFY] final text: LCB File R104-26, heard 15 October 2026, would revise sampling, testing, documentation, chain-of-custody and laboratory procedures to implement SB 157, conform “lot” to NRS 678B.649, and adopt ASTM D8334/D8334M. Source: CCB Notice of Intent, R104-26 — accessed 2026-10-31. Do not present R104-26 as adopted law until the adopted regulation is confirmed.
  • Older rule still cited in 2026 workshop material: a 20-gram sample for the 5-pound lot under R152-24 RP1 §69(3). Source: CCB public comment, 15 Oct 2026 — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] — this is superseded in principle by the SB 157 15-pound lot, but the operative sample weight in force on 2026-10-31 was not confirmed from a single adopted text.
  • A facility shall not use more than one cannabis independent testing laboratory to test the same lot/production run without approval of the appropriate Board Agent. Source: CCB Regulation 11 (2023) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Random / confirmation sampling: Regulation 11 §11.080 — at the Board’s request, a Board-chosen testing laboratory may collect and test random samples from establishments for comparison with reported results. Source: CCB Regulation 11 (2021) — accessed 2026-10-31.

Failed tests, retesting, remediation, destruction

  • If a sample does not pass NCCR 11.050 testing, the providing establishment shall dispose of the entire lot/production run and document disposal via its inventory control system (NCCR 6.080/6.082), except as provided in NCCR 11.075 (retesting/remediation). If it passes, the lab releases the lot/production run for manufacturing, packaging and labelling for sale. Source: CCB Regulation 11 (2021) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Retest cap: a cultivation or production facility may submit a request for retesting of not more than 50 lots or production runs each calendar year. For any subsequent failure in that year, the facility shall request Board permission for an additional 50 tests, destroy the lot/production run, or request to send it to extraction or remediation. If the additional 50 are approved, it must obtain two retests in the failed category from two different cannabis independent testing laboratories, and both must pass for sale approval. Source: CCB Regulation 11 (2023) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Pesticide-residue failure: a failed quality-assurance test for pesticide residue must be retested by the State Department of Agriculture unless otherwise approved by the Board or appropriate Board Agent. Same source — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • No remediation for unapproved pesticide — [VERIFY] as a general rule: secondary analysis states “THERE IS NO OPTION TO REMEDIATE CANNABIS THAT HAS BEEN TREATED WITH AN UNAPPROVED PESTICIDE UNDER NEVADA LAW OR REGULATIONS.” Source: Connor PLLC, pesticides and cannabis in Nevada — accessed 2026-10-31. This is a law-firm summary, not a CCB text; do not state it as statute without a CCB/NAC citation.
  • Failed samples held by a lab: a lab shall keep any sample which fails testing, or which is collected by the Board for confirmation testing, for 30 days after failure/collection, stored as approved by the appropriate Board Agent, then dispose of it. Source: CCB Regulation 11 (2021) — accessed 2026-10-31.

Lab enforcement actions and bulletins — verified

  • Bulletin 2025-02 — Greenway Medical: CCB Public Health and Safety Bulletin 2025-02, issued 15 December 2025 and amended 19 December 2025 to remove 8 packages determined unaffected: CCB agents discovered cannabis/products treated with unapproved pesticide Eagle 20, not applied in accordance with its label, at Greenway Medical, LLC (Licence #45452459135682728564; cultivation C018). Usable cannabis harvested August–November 2025 was affected; affected products may include flower, shake/trim and uninfused pre-rolls, infused edibles, infused flower, infused pre-rolls, or concentrates. CCB advised consumers to avoid consumption, and stated there was no reason to believe sales, production or testing facilities had any knowledge of the unapproved pesticide use. Affected packages were sold at listed sales facilities September–December 2025. Sources: search return for CCB Bulletin 2025-02 PDF and Cannabis Law Report copy — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] — the direct CCB PDF open failed on a local worker error in the child session; the bulletin text above is from search returns, not a fresh direct open. Re-verify from the CCB site before republication.
  • Related administrative hold: CCB unanimously approved a 30-day extension of an administrative hold on Greenway Medical products, moving expiry from 18 December 2025 to 17 January 2026, under SB 168. Source (secondary): Citizen Portal summary — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] — secondary summary only.
  • Bulletin 2026-01 — microbial failure after sampling: CCB Public Health and Safety Bulletin 2026-01, issued 28 October 2026: packages (uninfused pre-rolls and whole flower) successfully passed initial laboratory testing, but CCB agents subsequently determined the cannabis was altered after the laboratory sampling process. Subsequent testing: one lot failed for microbial contamination, specifically Enterobacteriaceae and Total Coliforms — Orange Juice Mac 3.5g, batch F1H8OJM, package 1A4040300007081000009370 — consumers advised to avoid it. Other listed lots could not undergo secondary testing, so contamination cannot be confirmed; they are listed as a precaution. CCB stated it has no reason to believe the sales facilities which sold the products had any knowledge of the issue. Affected packages were sold 1 January–13 August 2026. Source (directly opened): CCB Public Health Advisory 2026-01 — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • This bulletin is a public health and safety advisory; it does not by itself announce a completed statewide recall or a final enforcement outcome against a producer or lab. [VERIFY] final enforcement outcome — none found in CCB sources searched.
  • No standalone CCB “lab enforcement actions” list was found. Lab discipline falls under the general disciplinary framework in §11.2. Greenway Health Community, LLC settlement (Case 2025-001, production P097) is not a lab case: $18,000 penalty, 7-day suspension and 1 year probation for repeated failure to file quarterly reports and obstruction, per secondary summary. Source: Citizen Portal — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] — do not conflate Greenway Health Community (production) with Greenway Medical (cultivation, Bulletin 2025-02).

List of licensed testing laboratories — no standalone list found

  • No standalone CCB-published list of licensed testing laboratories was found. Do not invent a complete lab list.
  • CCB License Search route (Run 1 Part 5, confirmed again): CCB public List of Licensees — ccb.nv.gov/list-of-licensees — accessed 2026-10-31 — is the CCB’s “businesses not listed here may not be licensed” check; the CCB release of 16 April 2025 directs consumers to that list and notes 384 operational establishments including 106 dispensaries and two consumption lounges at that date. Source: CCB Buy Legal release, 16 Apr 2025 — accessed 2026-10-31. The List of Licensees page returned in search is organised by retail/lounge sections; a lab-only filter was not confirmed. [VERIFY] whether labs appear on that page or only via the CCB Business Entity Search.
  • CCB Business Entity Search returns lab entities by owner search, e.g. NV Cann Labs LLC (L001), DB Labs LLC (L003), LettuceTest LLC (L006), G3 Labs LLC (L007), 374 Labs LLC (L012), DPL NV LLC (L015). Sources: CCB Business Entity Search pages (search by owner surname) — accessed 2026-10-31. These search pages are dated 2020 in their metadata and are not proof of current licensure — [VERIFY] current status in Accela / List of Licensees before naming any lab as currently licensed.
  • Coalition of Nevada Cannabis Independent Testing Laboratories, October 2026 CCB public comment, lists as approving laboratories: 374 Labs, LLC; G3 Labs, LLC; MA and Associates, LLC; ERP, LLC; NV Cann Labs, LLC; RSR Analytical Laboratories; and DPL Nevada LLC dba Digipath Labs. Source: CCB public comment, 15 Oct 2026 — accessed 2026-10-31. This corroborates activity, not licensure, and gives no licence numbers for MA and Associates, ERP or RSR — [VERIFY].

11.2 Licence rules: transfers/sales, ownership changes, renewal, caps, relocation, surrender/revocation, insurance/bonding

Transfers / sales and ownership changes

  • NRS 678B.380: a cannabis establishment agent card, executive card, receiver card, medical licence and adult-use licence are nontransferable except as provided by Board regulations. The Board shall adopt regulations prescribing procedures by which (a) a licence holder may transfer the licence to another party qualified to hold it, and (b) an ownership-interest holder may transfer all or any portion of the interest to a qualified party. Regulations may give priority to transfers where the transferor is in receivership, recapitalisation, or a court proceeding involving financial distress. Source: NRS 678B.380 (LawServer mirror) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • CCB Regulations 5.110 and 5.112 provide the requirements for transferring all or a portion of an ownership interest. Source: Assembly Revenue minutes, 83rd Session — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • No timeline for Board approval of a transfer was found — consistent with Run 1. [UNAVAILABLE] as a stated timeline.
  • Independent lounge transfer lock: NRS 678B.380(4) prohibits transfer of an independent consumption lounge licence until at least 2 years from the date the lounge became operational; a holder wishing to cease operations before 2 years must surrender the licence, which the Board holds in reserve for future issuance. Source: NRS 678B.380 (LawServer mirror) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Lounge multiple-licence rule: NRS 678B.328 — Board shall not issue more than one independent lounge licence, more than one retail lounge licence, or both types, to any one person, except a transfer to a person acquiring 100% ownership in a county where they hold another such licence, if it complies with NRS 678B.380 and does not result in holding more than two lounge licences in combination. Source: NRS 678B.328 (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31.

Renewal — requirements and timeline

  • All licence types, regardless of status, must submit an annual licence renewal application through Accela and pay the appropriate annual renewal fee. Final licences expire annually after the date of issuance. Licensees can submit the annual renewal application and fees through Accela starting 60 days out from the expiration date. Consumption lounge conditional licensees are not required to submit an annual renewal until their initial year active licence is nearing expiration. Source: CCB FAQ — accessed 2026-10-31 (directly opened).
  • Renewal form cites NCCR 5.095; NRS 678B.210, 678B.250, 678B.390, and requires a Department of Taxation Letter of Good Standing dated within 2 months of expiry, plus the completed notarised form and record of payment uploaded in Accela. Source: CCB Licence Renewal Form, Jan 2024 — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Failure to renew by expiry: the licensee must cease operations and has 90 days to renew, or the licence is deemed voluntarily surrendered. Failure to comply with NRS Chapter 372A (cannabis taxes) may result in denial of a licence or renewal under Regulation 5.095. Source: Assembly Revenue minutes, 83rd Session — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • SB 277 dual-licence conversion: medical licences were converted into all-inclusive adult-use licences, completed 31 December 2024; NRS 678B.215 is now the dual-licence provision, with a separate medical licence still possible in a “covered jurisdiction” prohibiting adult-use. Source: CCB monopoly guidance PDF (mirrored copy) — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] — this source is a mirrored CCB PDF; confirm on ccb.nv.gov.

Ownership caps — 10% monopoly cap

  • NRS 678B.270 (adult-use, larger counties): to prevent monopolistic practices, in a county whose population is 100,000 or more, the Board shall ensure it does not issue, to any one person, group of persons or entity, the greater of (1) one adult-use licence, or (2) more than 10% of the adult-use licences otherwise allocable in the county. Source: NRS 678B.270 (public.law) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Exception — retail/lounge: the provisions of NRS 678B.270 do not apply to an adult-use licence for a retail cannabis consumption lounge or an independent cannabis consumption lounge. Source: CCB monopoly guidance (mirrored PDF) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • The two counties over 100,000 are Clark County and Washoe County, per the same CCB monopoly guidance — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • CCB requires licensees to provide monopoly calculations within a “No Monopoly Attestation” for a Transfer of Interest or any other application. Same guidance — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Retail-store county caps (context, not an ownership cap): NRS 678B.260 — 80 retail licences in counties ≥700,000; 20 in counties 100,000–<700,000; 4 in counties 55,000–<100,000; 2 in counties <55,000. Source: NRS 678B.260 (public.law) — accessed 2026-10-31.

Relocation

  • CCB FAQ, directly opened: “Can I move my cannabis establishment to another location? Only if the new location is within the same local jurisdiction as your original location. To request CCB approval for a change of location, you must first obtain approval from the local jurisdiction and submit a Change of Location Request through Accela that includes documentation of the local jurisdiction’s approval.” Source: CCB FAQ — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Regulation 6.065: a facility may move under the same locality regardless of distance if operation at the new location has been approved by the locality, and the locality may approve only in a public hearing with written notice at least 7 working days before the hearing. A written relocation request must include name/current/proposed addresses and licence, documentation of the public meeting, land-use approval, a professional survey showing distance compliance under NRS 678B.250, and a signed attestation. Source: CCB Regulation 6 (2022) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Statutory basis cited in case law: NRS 678B.500(2) — an establishment must comply with local zoning ordinances and may relocate if a local government approves the relocation after a public hearing. Source: FindLaw case law — accessed 2026-10-31.

Surrender / revocation

  • Disciplinary framework: NRS 678A.500–678A.640 (cited as 678A.500–678A.647 in 2025 material). A violation of any Title 56 provision or NCCR is grounds for disciplinary action, including immediate revocation of a licence or agent card. Regulation 4.020/4.030 covers grounds and imposition of civil penalty, revocation/suspension or corrective action. Source: CCB Regulation 4 (2021) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Voluntary surrender during a disciplinary proceeding commenced under NRS 678A.520 is deemed to constitute discipline. Source: SB 69, 82nd Session — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Non-renewal surrender: see above — 90 days after expiry without renewal = deemed voluntarily surrendered. Source: Assembly Revenue minutes, 83rd Session — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • No separate published “surrender form” timeline beyond the renewal/surrender form title was verified. [VERIFY] for a standalone surrender procedure.

Insurance or bonding minimums

  • [UNAVAILABLE] — No statewide CCB insurance minimum and no statewide surety-bond amount for cannabis establishments was found in NRS Chapter 678B or CCB Regulation 5/6 material searched.
  • A commercial surety-bond vendor claims state bonds “typically range from $5,000 to $100,000” and a City of Las Vegas $250,000 bond for cultivation/production/dispensing. Source: Surety Bond Authority, Nevada marijuana surety bond page — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY / DO NOT RELY] — vendor marketing, no statute cited; do not publish as a Nevada requirement. Local-jurisdiction bond/insurance conditions, if any, must be checked jurisdiction by jurisdiction.

11.3 Cultivation rules: canopy, indoor/outdoor/greenhouse, home grow

Licensed cultivation — enclosed, locked; indoor / outdoor / greenhouse

  • NRS 678B.510(3): except as otherwise provided in NRS 678D.400, all cultivation or production by a cultivation facility must take place in an enclosed, locked facility at the physical address provided to the Board during licensing, accessible only by agents lawfully associated with the facility, except supervised construction/repair/labour access. Source: NRS 678B.510 (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • CCB FAQ: “Enclosed, locked facility” means a closet, display case, room, greenhouse or other enclosed area equipped with locks or other security devices allowing access only by a medical cannabis establishment agent and the holder of a valid registry identification card. Greenhouse cultivation is acceptable if the greenhouse is an enclosed, secured area with adequate lighting in all areas. Source: CCB FAQ — accessed 2026-10-31 (directly opened, search return).
  • Outdoor cultivation is expressly contemplated in security rules (see §11.4): outdoor cultivation must cover the entirety of the cultivation area and perimeter/exterior, with 24-hour monitored alarm/video, an exterior barrier (solid block wall or chain-link fence ≥8 feet, plus an additional fence ≥8 feet — full text [VERIFY] on the current NAC version), and a location allowing response by local law enforcement. Source: CCB Security, NRS and NCCR — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Canopy cap: [UNAVAILABLE] — No statewide square-foot canopy cap, plant-count cap for licensed cultivation facilities, or indoor/outdoor/greenhouse quota was found in NRS 678B.510 or Regulation 6/8 material searched. Do not invent one. Board authority to limit production statewide exists under Regulation 6.015, but no numeric limit was verified. Source: CCB Regulation 6 (2022) — accessed 2026-10-31.

Home grow — adult-use (matches Run 1)

  • NRS 678D.200(3)(d)–(e): a person 21+ is exempt only if they do not possess/deliver/produce more than 2.5 oz usable cannabis, 1/4 oz concentrated cannabis, 6 cannabis plants (mature or immature), and a Board-established maximum quantity of products; and cultivate not more than 6 plants within an enclosed area not exposed to public view, equipped with locks/security devices allowing access only by an authorised person, at a residence/grounds where not more than 12 plants are cultivated. Source: NRS 678D.200 (public.law) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • 25-mile rule: NRS 678D.310 prohibits adult-use cultivation within 25 miles of a licensed adult-use retail store, subject to exceptions in that section. Sources: DeCastroverde Law guide and AB 240, 82nd Session — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] the full exception list in current NRS 678D.310 on leg.state.nv.us before listing exceptions beyond the 25-mile rule itself.
  • Property-owner control: NRS 678D.500 — a person who owns/occupies/controls private property may prohibit cultivation on it. Source cited in search: Legal Clarity guide — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] on leg.state.nv.us.

Home grow — medical (matches Run 1: 12 plants collective, exception-gated)

  • NRS 678C.200(3)(b): a registry-card holder and designated primary caregiver, if any, collectively possess at any one time no more than 2.5 oz usable cannabis, 12 cannabis plants (mature or immature), and a Board-established maximum quantity of products, safeguarded in an enclosed, secure location. Source: NRS 678C.200 (public.law) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • NRS 678C.200(6): after a medical dispensary opens in the cardholder’s county of residence, the cardholder (including a caregiver) is not authorised to cultivate, except if: (a) they were cultivating lawfully on or before 1 July 2013; (b) all dispensaries in the county close or cannot supply the quantity/strain necessary for their condition; (c) illness or lack of transportation prevents reasonable travel to a dispensary; or (d) no dispensary was operating within 25 miles of their residence when they first applied. Same source — accessed 2026-10-31.

11.4 Security: cameras, alarms, storage, transport and delivery vehicles

Premises security and video

  • NRS 678B.510(2): an establishment may have more than one entrance so long as each entrance is secure, and it shall implement strict security measures to deter/prevent theft and unauthorised entrance into areas containing cannabis. NRS 678B.510(6): each establishment shall install a video monitoring system that, at minimum, allows transmission/storage (digital or analog) of a video feed displaying interior and exterior, and is capable of being accessed remotely by a law enforcement agency in real time upon request. Source: NRS 678B.510 (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Regulation 6.085 (NCCR security): to prevent unauthorised access, an establishment must have a single secure entrance (older text; NRS now allows multiple secure entrances — [VERIFY] which text controls after NAC recodification), no visible cannabis/products from outside, intrusion-detection devices, exterior lighting to facilitate surveillance, and electronic monitoring including: a call-up monitor ≥19 inches; a printer capable of immediately producing a clear still photo from any camera image for Board/Agent review; video cameras ≥1920×1080 or equivalent, ≥15 frames per second, covering all entrances/exits, any vault room and any point-of-sale location, recording 24 hours/day, remotely accessible by law enforcement in real time, capable of low-light identification; lower-resolution cameras (≥720×480 in secondary summaries) for other limited-access areas; storage of recordings for at least 30 calendar days in a secure on-site or off-site location or via a service/network providing on-demand access; a failure-notification system (audible and visual); and sufficient battery backup (older text: at least 5 minutes of recording in a power outage — [VERIFY] current backup duration). Sources: CCB Security, NRS and NCCR (2021) and Cornell LII, NAC 453A §111 — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Immediate automatic/electronic notification to local law enforcement of an unauthorised breach of security in the interior of each building. Same CCB security source — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Operating documents must include oversight and accurate recordkeeping procedures. NRS 678B.510(1). Source: NRS 678B.510 (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Inspections: establishments are subject to reasonable Board inspection at any time; the licence holder or designee must be available/present. NRS 678B.510(5). Same source — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Vending machines: an establishment shall not dispense/sell from a vending machine or allow one to be installed inside or outside the premises. NRS 678B.510(7). Same source — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Consumption on premises (non-lounge): an establishment that is not a consumption lounge shall not allow consumption on its property/premises. NRS 678B.510(4). Same source — accessed 2026-10-31.

Storage (distributor and general)

  • Distributor storage area: at least one temperature-controlled area, in a commercial food-grade unit kept <41°F (5°C) while storing potentially hazardous cannabis products; the storage area must be a separate, enclosed, locked facility; unrelated products, including alcohol, must not be stored with cannabis; within it, cannabis may only be stored in a secure, locked device/cabinet/room/motor vehicle protected by a lock meeting at least the Underwriters Laboratories security rating for key locks. Source: CCB Security, NRS and NCCR — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Distributor temporary storage: if a distributor uses its storage area for temporary storage due to unusual/extraordinary circumstances beyond its control, it shall submit a notice of temporary storage to the Board. If the final destination exceeds 100 miles and delivery within one trip is unreasonable, product may be stored no more than 48 hours; all stored product must be documented in the seed-to-sale system; a distributor shall not store for more than 48 hours without written consent from the appropriate Board Agent; inventory must be verified after off-load and before on-load. Same CCB security source — accessed 2026-10-31. Conflict flagged: a secondary distributor guide states “may not exceed three days without written consent” — Flourish Software, Nevada guide — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] — the 48-hour CCB text is preferred, but the discrepancy must be resolved on current NAC 678B before publication.

Transport between establishments and delivery to consumers

  • Nevada requires movement between licensed establishments through a separately licensed distributor; a distributor may not cultivate, manufacture or sell to consumers (secondary summary, consistent with the distributor licence type). Source: Flourish Software, Nevada guide — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] on NRS 678B distributor definition.
  • Consumer delivery: CCB FAQ — “Is customer delivery allowed?” and “Is third-party delivery permitted? Yes. Only licensed dispensaries/retail stores may request Board approval for third-party delivery. A contract and vehicle inspection are required. When a dispensary/retail store requests an inspection, the requirements for vehicles are provided to the dispensary/retail store.” Source: CCB FAQ — accessed 2026-10-31 (search return for FAQ lines 764–774).
  • Delivery manifest (older NAC 453A text, now recodified): a retail store delivering to a consumer must secure product at all times, maintain a physical/electronic copy of a delivery manifest generated via the seed-to-sale system, enter delivery information before delivery and not alter it, return undeliverable product to the store, and give each consumer a separate manifest copy containing only that consumer’s information, including date/approximate time, store name/location/address/licence number, consumer name/location/address, item name/quantity by weight and unit, vehicle make/model/plate/identification-card number, and agent name/card number/signature. Source: Cornell LII, NAC 453A §151 — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] current NAC 678B delivery-manifest section — the 453A text is pre-recodification.
  • Delivery-vehicle attributes in secondary summaries (unmarked vehicles, alarm, lockbox, product not visible from outside, no delivery to a gaming-licensed premises, delivery only within Nevada, route documented): Source: IndicaOnline, Nevada market guide — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] — secondary only; the CCB FAQ confirms vehicle inspection but does not publish the vehicle checklist in the text returned.
  • CCB “Buy Legal” warning: licensed establishments may offer delivery to a private residence; delivery to a resort casino is an indicator of illegal cannabis, and no licensed operators are located within 1,500 feet of a casino. Source: CCB Buy Legal release, 16 Apr 2025 — accessed 2026-10-31.

11.5 Waste disposal

  • General rule (older NAC 453D.745, now recodified): an establishment shall store, manage and dispose of all solid/liquid waste and wastewater from processing/production in accordance with all applicable state and local laws, and render waste containing cannabis unusable before it leaves the establishment. Covered waste includes plant waste (roots, stalks, leaves, stems, flower, trim, solid plant material, material used to create extract), solvents used in processing/extraction, plant material/solvents discarded as a result of quality-assurance or other testing by a testing facility, and any other waste as determined by the Department. Source: Cornell LII, NAC 453D.745 — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Rendering method (older text): unless another Board-approved method is used, waste containing cannabis must be rendered unusable by grinding and incorporating it with compostable mixed waste (for organic disposal other than composting) or non-compostable mixed waste (for landfill/other methods), such that the cannabis waste is <50% by volume of the resulting mixture; such waste must not be disposed of by composting under that paragraph. A distributor or retail store may return a product to a cultivation/production facility to be rendered unusable. Notice must be given via the seed-to-sale system before rendering unusable and disposing. Same source — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] current NAC 678B waste section (NCCR 10.080 in older numbering) — the 2024 CCB solicitation minutes note industry requests to amend 10.080(3) (“rendered unusable by grinding,” with no exception) and that inspectors had offered mixing as a Board-approved alternative. Source: CCB Solicitation of Input Minutes, 31 Jan 2024 — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Separate CCB statement in secondary summary: disposed material shall be rendered unusable, protected from possession/ingestion by persons and animals, and not placed within the establishment’s exterior refuse containers. Source: nevadastatecannabis.org, Washoe page — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] — secondary paraphrase of NRS 678B.510-related waste duties.
  • No Nevada-specific list of approved commercial cannabis-waste haulers was verified from a CCB source. Commercial vendor GAIACA advertises Nevada waste services, but that is not a CCB approval list. Source: GAIACA, Nevada cannabis waste management page — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] before naming any hauler as approved.

11.6 Environmental: water, pesticide limits, energy

Pesticides — the only fully sourced environmental control

  • NRS 586.550: a cannabis establishment may use a pesticide in cultivation/production only if it (a) is exempt from registration under 40 C.F.R. §152.25 or allowed on Crop Group 19, hops, or unspecified crops/plants; (b) bears a label allowing use at the intended site; and (c) bears a label allowing use on crops/plants intended for human consumption. The State Department of Agriculture shall establish and publish a list of pesticides allowed on cannabis/cannabis products and accept requests to add pesticides. Source: NRS 586.550 (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Regulation 11 §11.065: an establishment shall only use a pesticide if it appears on the Department of Agriculture list published under NRS 586.550; a lab performing pesticide-residue analysis shall analyse for listed pesticides at Department-specified detection levels and for any other substances. Source: CCB Regulation 11 (2021) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Practical note (secondary): the Department publishes active ingredients rather than a product-by-product list, so cultivators must match products to a “not prohibited” active ingredient and check site/human-consumption labelling. Source: Connor PLLC, pesticides and cannabis in Nevada — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] the current Department list itself — it was not opened in this search.
  • Cultivation facilities must retain at least one certified applicator (NRS 555.2618) authorised for greenhouse/nursery pest control (indoor) or agricultural pest control (outdoor) under NAC 555.640. Source: Cornell LII, NAC 453A §187 — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] current NAC 678B equivalent.

Water — groundwater and Colorado River

  • [UNAVAILABLE for a Nevada cannabis-specific water quota or permit rule] — No CCB or Nevada Division of Water Resources cannabis-specific water-use limit, groundwater permit rule, or energy rule was found in the sources searched.
  • General federal-water principle (secondary, not Nevada-specific): federally managed water resources are off limits for cannabis growing; the Bureau of Reclamation oversees Colorado River diversions for irrigation in parts of Nevada and Southern California, and those diverted waters cannot be used for cannabis operations. Groundwater can be a source but often requires a permit (Oregon example given in that article, not Nevada). Source: Cannabis Law Report, Jenner & Block overview — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] — do not present the Colorado River point as a Nevada statute; it is a federal-water / Bureau of Reclamation principle cited in a general article. A Nevada-specific Division of Water Resources or CCB water guidance check is still required.

Energy

  • [UNAVAILABLE] — No Nevada cannabis-specific energy-use, lighting, or carbon rule was found. The only environmental rulemaking duty found is procedural: SB 277 §1.5 requires the Board, before adopting/amending/repealing any regulation, to consider whether the action is likely to have an adverse effect on the environment and, if so, whether methods exist to reduce/eliminate that effect without imposing an economic burden on adult-use/medical licence holders. Source: SB 277 enrolled text, 82nd Session — accessed 2026-10-31.

11.7 Sampling, giveaways, discounts and promotions

Advertising — “free” ban and other restrictions (matches Run 1)

  • NRS 678B.520(11) advertising restrictions, as summarised in CCB FAQ (directly opened): advertising must not be false/misleading; must not promote overconsumption; must not depict consumption or a child/person <21 consuming; must not use toys, characters, cartoons, mascots, action figures, balloons or other child-appealing depictions; must not model product on a brand primarily consumed/marketed to children; must not advertise in any publication/radio/TV/medium where 30% or more of the audience is reasonably expected to be <21; must not sponsor youth sports/entertainment events including team uniforms; must not place an advertisement within 1,000 feet of a public or private school, playground, public park or library (unless placed before that facility existed); must not place on/inside public-transportation vehicles or shelters (taxis excepted); must not place at a sports/entertainment event to which persons <21 are allowed entry; must not place where prohibited by locality ordinance; and must not advertise or offer any cannabis/cannabis product as “free” or “donated” without a purchase. Source: CCB FAQ — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Statutory text: NRS 678B.520(11)(d) — “Shall not advertise or offer any cannabis or cannabis product as ‘free’ or ‘donated’ without a purchase.” Source: NRS 678B.520 (LawServer mirror) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Advertising warnings: all advertising must contain Board-prescribed warnings including “Keep out of reach of children.” Same NRS source — accessed 2026-10-31. CCB FAQ: pre-approval of advertising is no longer required, but Board staff may review on request, and civil penalties may issue for non-compliant advertising under NRS 678A–D and NCCR Regulations 6 and 12. Source: CCB FAQ — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Local governments may adopt more restrictive advertising ordinances (sign number/location/size, handbills, stationary/moving displays, content). NRS 678B.520(13). Source: NRS 678B.520 (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Advertising audience documentation: if an establishment must determine the <21 percentage reasonably expected to view/hear an advertisement, it shall maintain documentation for not less than 5 years after first broadcast/publication/display showing how it determined the expected age. NRS 678B.520(14). Same source — accessed 2026-10-31.

Sampling — B2B vs consumer

  • B2B free samples exist in the regulatory index: NCCR 8.010 is titled “Required written disclosure with each lot of usable cannabis; provision of free samples to cannabis sales facility; applicability of provisions governing excise tax on cannabis to free samples.” Source: CCB meeting minutes, 20 Nov 2025 — accessed 2026-10-31. Details [VERIFY] — the full text, quantity limits, tax treatment and Metrc treatment of B2B samples were not returned in this search.
  • Consumer “free” giveaways are banned as advertising (see above) — “free” or “donated” without a purchase. That is distinct from a regulated B2B sample to a sales facility and from a lounge’s on-site consumption model; do not conflate them.
  • Lounge sampling note (secondary): “cannabis samples cannot be given away gratis (every product must be tracked via Metrc).” Source: Catalyst BC, Nevada cannabis lounge licence guide — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] — secondary lounge guide, not a CCB text.

Discounts and promotions

  • [UNAVAILABLE as a standalone discount rule] — No Nevada statute or CCB regulation specifically capping discounts, loyalty points, or “buy-one-get-one” promotions was found, beyond (a) the “free/donated without a purchase” advertising ban, (b) the prohibition on promoting overconsumption, and (c) the per-transaction sale limits in NRS 678B.550 / Run 1 possession caps. Do not invent a discount cap. Any discount that in substance offers product “free” without a purchase would fall under the ban above — [VERIFY] with CCB guidance before advising on a specific promotion.

11.8 Drug testing, background checks and agent-card rules for licence holders / staff

General employment drug testing — NRS 613.132 (matches Run 1)

  • NRS 613.132(1): it is unlawful for any employer in Nevada to fail/refuse to hire a prospective employee because a screening test indicates the presence of marijuana. Source: NRS 613.132 (Justia, 2022) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Exceptions, NRS 613.132(2): subsection (1) does not apply if the applicant is applying (a) as a firefighter (NRS 450B.071); (b) as an emergency medical technician (NRS 450B.065); (c) for a position requiring operation of a motor vehicle for which federal or state law requires screening tests; or (d) for a position that, in the employer’s determination, could adversely affect the safety of others. Same source — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Rebuttal test: if an employer requires a screening test within the first 30 days of employment, the employee may submit an additional screening test at their own expense to rebut the initial result; the employer shall accept and give appropriate consideration to it. NRS 613.132(3). Same source — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Non-application: NRS 613.132(4) — the section does not apply to the extent inconsistent with an employment contract/collective bargaining agreement, federal law, or a position funded by a federal grant. “Screening test” means a test of blood, urine, hair or saliva to detect the general presence of a controlled substance or other drug. NRS 613.132(5). Same source — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • No cannabis-establishment-specific drug-testing mandate for staff was found beyond the general NRS 613.132 framework and the agent-card background check below. [VERIFY] if a CCB operating-document requirement imposes staff testing — none was returned.

Agent cards — DPS background check (matches Run 1)

  • CCB FAQ (directly opened): to work in Nevada cannabis, a person needs an Agent Card issued by the CCB, one card/application for each category/role — production, cultivation, dispensary/retail store, distribution, laboratory, and contractor (for a verifiable contract company). Each card requires a $150 fee and a background check. Fees for background checks are managed by the Nevada Department of Public Safety. Applications are online only through the Accela Cannabis Customer Portal. Source: CCB FAQ — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Temporary card: once a complete application is received and payment processed, the applicant receives a Temporary Agent Card allowing work immediately for up to 90 days while the CCB awaits background results, retrieved from Accela attachments. Same FAQ source — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • NRS 678B.340: a person shall not hold an ownership interest of <5%, volunteer or work at, contract to provide labour to, or be employed by an independent contractor to provide labour to, a cannabis establishment as an agent unless registered with the Board. Application must include a signed statement that the applicant has not previously had an agent card revoked, a complete set of fingerprints and written permission authorising the Board to forward them to the Central Repository for Nevada Records of Criminal History for submission to the FBI, and the application fee under NRS 678B.390. The Board shall submit fingerprints to the Central Repository for FBI submission to determine criminal history. Source: NRS 678B.340 (public.law) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Owners/officers/board members who already furnished information at licensing need not resubmit it, but still need the card itself; each owner/officer/board member must possess an agent card corresponding to each licence type sought. Sources: NRS 678B.340 (public.law) and CCB Preparing to Apply Worksheet, May 2024 — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Excluded felony: NRS 678B.050 defines “excluded felony offense” (Category A felony, or two or more felonies) — disqualification subject to the SB 277 exemption-petition process under NRS 678B.633. Sources: Legal Clarity guide and SB 277 enrolled text, 82nd Session — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] the current exemption-petition criteria on leg.state.nv.us.
