  • Card validity: 2 years per category, per Run 1 and confirmed in 2025 legislative minutes: “the cost of an agent card is $150 per category, and each card is valid for two years.” Source: Assembly Revenue minutes, 83rd Session — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Cessation notice: if a cardholder stops working at an establishment, the establishment must notify the CCB within 10 business days (secondary summary of NRS 678B.340). Source: Legal Clarity guide — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] on current NRS text.

11.9 Research and university cannabis / hemp licences

No distinct CCB research licence type found. Plain statement: no distinct “university cannabis licence” or “research licence” type under NRS Chapter 678B was found in the sources searched. University activity below is therefore listed as research/education/hemp-registration activity, not as a CCB licence class. If a CCB research licence exists under a different name, it is [UNAVAILABLE] in this search and must not be invented.

UNR — medical cannabis research programme (statute vs repeal conflict)

  • NRS 678C.700 (2025 text returned): “The University of Nevada, Reno, School of Medicine shall establish a program for the evaluation and research of the medical use of cannabis” in care/treatment of persons diagnosed with a chronic or debilitating condition, but before establishing it the School “shall aggressively seek and must receive approval” by the Federal Government under 21 U.S.C. §823 or other applicable federal law, with Nevada-resident volunteers as participants/subjects and quarterly reports to the Interim Finance Committee on federal-approval progress and, if approved, programme status/activities/information. Source: NRS 678C.700 (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Conflict — [VERIFY] current status: secondary reporting on the 2025 session states lawmakers repealed the 2001 bill (as amended by AB 365) requiring UNR School of Medicine to establish that medical cannabis research programme and report quarterly, noting the school currently offers a Cannabis Healthcare and Medicine Certificate (8-week programme). Source: The Nevadan, 2025 session cannabis bills — accessed 2026-10-31. Do not present NRS 678C.700 as currently operative without checking the 2025 enrolled repeal bill on leg.state.nv.us. The Part 12 pass independently notes the same AB 365 repeal report — see §12.10.
  • UNR campus rule (NSHE): use/possession/cultivation of marijuana, including for medical purposes, on NSHE-owned/leased property or at NSHE-sponsored activities is expressly prohibited under federal funding rules; any NSHE institution may engage in medical marijuana research conducted in accordance with state and federal law only with (a) prior written consent of the institution’s President after consultation with general counsel, and (b) legal authorisation from proper federal authorities. Source: UNR Administrative Manual §7008 — accessed 2026-10-31.

UNR — industrial hemp research (primary source)

  • UNR Sponsored Projects (official): under federal law hemp is Cannabis genus material with ≤0.3% delta-9 THC on a dry-weight basis; the 2018 Farm Bill legalised hemp subject to federal/state oversight; Nevada SB 305 provided the regulatory framework for industrial hemp research conducted by Nevada institutions of higher education. UNR researchers wishing to conduct industrial hemp research must register through the University and follow University procedures/guidelines for growing, processing and manufacturing hemp. Source (directly opened): UNR, industrial hemp research — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • UNR Experiment Station (official): industrial fibre hemp varietal evaluation in western Nevada; pre-harvest sampling by Nevada Department of Agriculture personnel to determine delta-9 THC; varieties Enectarol and Futura 83 top-performing on bast/hurd fibre yield in that trial. Source: UNR Experiment Station publication — accessed 2026-10-31. This is hemp research, not a cannabis licence.
  • Hemp registration generally: NRS 557.200 — a person shall not grow/handle hemp or produce agricultural hemp seed unless registered with the Department (of Agriculture) as grower/handler/producer; registration expires 31 December each year; surrender/non-renewal requires ≥30 days’ notice and a disposal/eradication plan. Source: NRS 557.200 (FindLaw) — accessed 2026-10-31.

UNLV — Cannabis Policy Institute (policy research, not a cultivation licence)

  • UNLV official: the Cannabis Policy Institute (CPI) at UNLV, established in 2023, positions UNLV as a trusted source for cannabis policy and research guidance, education and community engagement; its mission is development/advancement of cannabis research, policy and education, covering policy, law, economics, science, medicine and business. Director Riana Durrett was appointed August 2023. Research listed includes “The 1,500 Foot Wall: Barriers between cannabis and gaming in Nevada” and the “Nevada Cannabis Report.” Source: UNLV Cannabis Policy Institute — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • UNLV official launch release: CPI will generate policy/research guidance and expand education/community engagement, including microcredentials and executive training. Source: UNLV launch release — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • No UNLV cannabis cultivation or CCB establishment licence was verified. UNLV CPI work returned is policy/economics/medicine research and education, including August 2026 Cannabis Career Certificate programmes with Green Flower (secondary press-release summary). Source: CB Insights, Green Flower — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY] — secondary; do not present certificate programmes as a licence.
  • R&D testing (licensed establishments, not universities): Regulation 11 §11.045 allows limited testing for research and development purposes, with reports marked “R&D TESTING ONLY — NOT FOR RESALE,” and R&D batches that fail QA need not be destroyed but may not be sold until they pass all NCCR 6.100 testing. Source: CCB Regulation 11 (2023) — accessed 2026-10-31. This is an establishment R&D provision, not a university licence.

Part 12 — Consumer and social rules

12.1 Paraphernalia and smoke shops

  • Adult-use exemption covers paraphernalia possession/delivery, within the adult-use limits. NRS 678D.200(1)(b) exempts a person 21+ from state prosecution for “the possession or delivery of paraphernalia,” subject to NRS 678D.200(3) limits. Source: NRS Chapter 678D (leg.state.nv.us) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • The exemption is lost for paraphernalia possession discovered through public-place or school-property use. NRS 678D.300(1)(d) denies exemption for possessing cannabis in violation of NRS 453.336 or paraphernalia in violation of NRS 453.560 or 453.566 if discovered because the person engaged in adult use in a public place / place open to the public or exposed to public view, a detention/correctional facility, or if possession occurs on school property. “School property” means grounds of a public school described in NRS 388.020 and a private school as defined in NRS 394.103. Source: same NRS 678D page — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Sale/manufacture for a controlled-substance violation remains a category E felony on the books. NRS 453.560: a person who delivers or sells, possesses with intent to deliver or sell, or manufactures with intent to deliver or sell drug paraphernalia, knowing or where one reasonably should know it will be used to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, prepare, test, analyze, pack, repack, store, contain, conceal, ingest, inhale or otherwise introduce a controlled substance into the body in violation of Chapter 453, is guilty of a category E felony under NRS 193.130. Sources: NRS 453.560 (FindLaw) — accessed 2026-10-31; corroborated by Nevada felony penalties, Category E — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Use / possession with intent to use is a misdemeanour under NRS 453.566 — secondary summary only, penalty stated as up to 6 months and/or up to $1,000. [VERIFY — direct NRS 453.566 text not opened in this run.] Source: 702 Defense, drug paraphernalia laws — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Definition: NRS 453.554 is cited secondarily as defining drug paraphernalia as any object/equipment used or intended to be used to cultivate, manufacture, package, store, test, convert, plant, ingest, inhale or otherwise introduce a controlled substance into the body. [VERIFY — direct text not opened.] Source: Pariente Law, possession of drug paraphernalia — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Delivery to a minor is stated secondarily as elevated to a category C felony where the recipient is under 18 and at least 3 years younger. [VERIFY — do not publish as settled without opening current NRS 453.560/453.566.] Source: 702 Defense — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • No Nevada-specific “smoke shop licence” was found. No statute or CCB rule in the sources searched creates a cannabis-paraphernalia-only retail licence distinct from a licensed cannabis establishment. Smoke/head shops therefore operate, in practice, at the intersection of general business licensing, tobacco-retail rules where tobacco is sold, and NRS 453.560/453.566 — not under a dedicated paraphernalia licence. [UNAVAILABLE / VERIFY — absence is a search result, not a confirmed negative in the full NAC/NRS.]
  • Tobacco-retail age rule, for tobacco products only: Nevada Dept. of Taxation guidance cites NRS 370.521(3), effective 1 Jan 2023: a person shall not sell cigarettes, cigarette paper or other tobacco products to a person under 40 without age verification through scanning/automated controls verifying 21+, with a $100 civil penalty per offence for violating that subsection. Source: Dept. of Taxation, 2023 legislative update, tobacco sales — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Practice note, secondary: Federal Tobacco 21 is presented as making 21 the practical floor for buying bongs/pipes sold as tobacco accessories nationwide. [VERIFY — federal source not opened; do not conflate with Nevada cannabis-paraphernalia law.] Source: Smoke Honest blog — accessed 2026-10-31.

12.2 THC driving limits and impairment testing — correction to pre-2021 figures

Do NOT republish 2 ng/ml blood / 10 ng/ml urine as the current general rule. Those figures survive only in a narrowed felony context, below.

  • General DUI ban: NRS 484C.110(2), as summarised from the statute text in search results, makes it unlawful to drive or be in actual physical control of a vehicle on a highway or premises to which the public has access while (a) under the influence of a controlled substance, (b) under the combined influence of intoxicating liquor and a controlled substance, or (c) using a chemical/poison/organic solvent to a degree rendering the person incapable of safely driving. Entitlement to use the drug under Nevada law is expressly not a defence to that subsection. Source: NRS 484C.110 (LawServer mirror) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • AB 400 change, effective 1 July 2021: AB 400 removed the per se cannabis limits for misdemeanour (first/second) DUI; prosecutors must prove impairment to a degree rendering the driver incapable of safely driving, rather than relying on a blood number alone. The per se limits remain for felony DUI. Sources: Ken Stover Law blog, July 2021; Northern Nevada Business Weekly, 9 July 2021; Paul Padda Law blog — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Felony-only per se blood thresholds, secondary summary: For a third or subsequent DUI within 7 years, per se impairment is established at 2 ng/ml delta-9-THC in blood or 5 ng/ml marijuana metabolite (11-OH-THC) in blood. The same source states urine-based per se limits for marijuana are no longer in effect as of 1 July 2017. Source: Paul Padda Law blog — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Corroborating structure: NRS 484C.110(4) is summarised as applying only to a violation punishable under NRS 484C.400(1)(c), with blood thresholds of 2 ng/ml marijuana (delta-9-THC) and 5 ng/ml for the metabolite. Source: NRS 484C.110 (LawServer mirror) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • [VERIFY — the direct leg.state.nv.us NRS 484C.110 page was not successfully opened in the Part 12 run (local-worker failure, not retried). The felony-only scope is agreed by multiple secondaries, but the verbatim subsection (4) wording and its exact cross-reference to NRS 484C.400 should be re-checked on leg.state.nv.us before republication.]
  • No breathalyser equivalent for cannabis; impairment is proved by observation/testing, not a roadside THC number. Secondary legal summaries state there is no standardised roadside THC test, no widely accepted science linking a specific blood THC level to impairment, and THC can linger days/weeks after effects pass. Source: Gowdey Law, drug DUI marijuana Nevada — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Impairment testing practice, secondary: Officers use field sobriety tests; a Drug Recognition Expert (DRE) may conduct a structured multi-step evaluation (pulse, blood pressure, etc.) and give an opinion on drug category — described as a subjective professional opinion, not a scientific measurement. For suspected drug DUI, breath cannot detect drugs, so blood (and, per NRS 484C.160, urine in some circumstances) is used. Sources: 702 Defense, DUI of marijuana; Christiansen Law, Las Vegas drug DUI — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Implied consent: NRS 484C.160(1) deems a driver / person in actual physical control on a highway or public-access premises to have consented to an evidentiary test of blood, urine, breath or other bodily substance to determine alcohol concentration or whether a controlled/prohibited substance is present, if requested by an officer with reasonable grounds. NRS 484C.160(7) provides that if marijuana in blood is in issue, the officer may request a blood test. Source: NRS 484C.160 (public.law) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Medical card is no defence to DUI. NRS 678C.300(1)(a), secondary/mirror text, lists driving/operating/being in actual physical control of a vehicle or vessel under the influence of cannabis, and other conduct prohibited by NRS 484C.110 etc., as acts for which a registry-card holder is not exempt and may not raise an affirmative defence. Source: NRS 678C.300 (FindLaw) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Vehicle consumption ban (distinct from DUI): NRS 678D.310(3) — except as provided in subsection 10 (lounge) or Board regulations under NRS 678B.645, a person who smokes or otherwise consumes cannabis or a cannabis product in a public place, in an adult-use cannabis retail store, or in a vehicle is guilty of a misdemeanour punished by a fine of not more than $600. Source: NRS Chapter 678D (leg.state.nv.us) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Roadside THC devices in 2025: One secondary claims Nevada deployed new roadside devices detecting THC/opioids/stimulants in 2025. [VERIFY — single secondary, no official source opened; do not publish as an official testing standard.] Source: Joey Gilbert Law blog — accessed 2026-10-31.

12.3 Record sealing for marijuana offences — petition-based, NOT general automatic expungement

Nevada uses sealing, not expungement (records are removed from public access, not destroyed, in secondary descriptions).

  • Decriminalised offences — NRS 179.271 (AB 192, Nevada Second Chance Act, 2019): If an offence is decriminalised, a person convicted before decriminalisation may submit a written request to any court of conviction for sealing of any criminal-history record in its possession and in possession of any criminal-justice agency relating to the conviction. The court shall send written notice to the prosecuting attorney; if no written objection is filed within 10 judicial days after notice, the court shall grant the request; if objection is filed, the court must hold a hearing. No fee may be charged by any court or criminal-justice agency for the request. The section does not apply to a traffic offence. “Decriminalised” means no longer punishable as a crime as a result of a legislative act or Article 19 referendum/initiative. Sources: NRS 179.271 (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31; NRS 179.271 (public.law) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Hearing standard, secondary: at a hearing the prosecutor must show by clear and convincing evidence good cause not to grant. Source: Gersten Law Firm blog, 30 Sep 2019 — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • No automatic statewide sealing mechanism was found. Secondary analysis states AB 192 relief is petition-based and “effectively excludes those who do not file.” Source: Collateral Consequences Resource Center, Nevada restoration profile — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • General sealing after conviction — NRS 179.245: Petition to the court of conviction after an eligibility waiting period varying by seriousness. Secondary summary: misdemeanours 1–2 years (7 years for domestic-violence misdemeanours), felonies 2–10 years depending on category, from release/discharge, whichever is later; sealing unavailable for crimes against a child, sex offences, and certain DUI offences, and to anyone convicted during the waiting period. [VERIFY — detailed waiting-period table not opened on leg.state.nv.us in this run; do not republish the full table without that check.] Source: Collateral Consequences Resource Center — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Small-possession sealing routes, secondary:
    • NRS 453.3365: a conviction for possession of a controlled substance not for sale may be sealed 3 years after conviction/sentence if terms are fulfilled and, after hearing, the court is satisfied the person is rehabilitated. Source: 702 Defense blog — accessed 2026-10-31.
    • NRS 453.336(8) as summarised: records for possession of 1 oz or less of marijuana will be sealed upon completion of terms/conditions imposed at sentencing. Source: National Reentry Resource Center, clean-slate comparison — accessed 2026-10-31.
    • NRS 678D.310(5): for an under-21 false-representation offence, if the person fulfils terms/conditions, the court shall, without a hearing, order sealed all documents, papers, exhibits, minute-book and docket entries and other case documents in custody of agencies/officers named in the order. Source: NRS Chapter 678D (leg.state.nv.us) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Pardon — separate from sealing:
    • State Board of Pardons Commissioners resolution (2020, requested by Gov. Sisolak): pardoned persons convicted of possession of 1 oz or less (secondary accounts vary: “between 2001 and 2017” in one, “1986–2017” in another — [VERIFY date range]); it did not apply to sales or other drug crimes, nor to pre-2001 possession when it was a felony (individual pardons still available); and it did not itself remove convictions from records — sealing is a separate step. Sources: Scribd, Marijuana Pardons FAQ; Cannabis Examiners — accessed 2026-10-31.
    • NRS 179.273: if a court and the Central Repository receive a certified copy of an unconditional pardon from the State Board of Pardons Commissioners, they shall seal all criminal-history records subject to the pardon; a pardoned person may also petition with proof of pardon, with no fee, and the court shall grant unless the charges differ from those in the pardon. Source: NRS 179.273 (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31.
    • Sealing does not by itself restore firearm rights unless a pardon specifically addresses those rights — secondary statement only. [VERIFY.] Source: Gersten Law Firm blog — accessed 2026-10-31.

12.4 Consumption lounges and events

Lounges — law (opened on leg.state.nv.us)

  • Two types: Retail lounge (attached/immediately adjacent to an adult-use retail store) and independent lounge (contracting with one or more adult-use retail stores). NRS 678D.470(1) (retail) and NRS 678D.475(1)–(2) (independent). Source: NRS Chapter 678D (leg.state.nv.us) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Only lounge-bought product may be consumed in the lounge; nothing may be taken out. NRS 678D.470(2), NRS 678D.475(3), and NRS 678D.465(4): a lounge shall not allow a single-use or ready-to-consume product purchased at the lounge to be removed from the premises. Source: same NRS 678D page — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Lounge may consume in the lounge — express exception to the public/vehicle ban: NRS 678D.310(10): “A person may smoke or otherwise consume cannabis or a cannabis product in a cannabis consumption lounge.” Source: same NRS 678D page — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Prohibited in lounges — NRS 678D.465: (1) consumption in any place within view of a public place; (2) entry of any person under 21; (3) consumption of any product that is not a single-use or ready-to-consume product. Source: same NRS 678D page — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Operating duties — NRS 678D.455: ventilation/exhaust capable of expelling odours, reducing volatile organic compounds and meeting Board air-quality standards; employee training on paraphernalia, single-use and ready-to-consume products, potency/absorption/effects, recognition of impairment/overconsumption and safe handling of an impaired customer; security plan; a plan to deter customers from driving under the influence; a plan to ensure cannabis is not sold/distributed other than as authorised; disposal per Board procedures; compliance with local ordinances and Board rules. Source: same NRS 678D page — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Food, non-cannabis items, live entertainment — NRS 678D.460: a lounge may sell food and beverages, sell any other item not containing cannabis and not intended for use with cannabis, and provide live entertainment. Source: same NRS 678D page — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Ready-to-consume standards — NRS 678D.480: Board must adopt regulations prescribing the list of single-use products, content/quality/potency standards including maximum THC concentration for ready-to-consume products, preparation/safe-handling procedures, and sale-notification requirements. Source: same NRS 678D page — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Packaging carve-out: NRS 678D.420(3) — to the extent inconsistent with Board regulations under NRS 678D.480, the ordinary packaging/label limits do not apply to a ready-to-consume product prepared and sold by a lounge. The ordinary adult-use limits in NRS 678D.420(1)–(2) remain: max 10 mg THC per serving; single package max 2.5 oz usable cannabis, 3,500 mg THC for concentrates, 100 mg THC for edibles, etc. Source: same NRS 678D page — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Distance from gaming — secondary, consistent across sources: 1,500 ft from an establishment holding a nonrestricted gaming licence, in counties of 100,000+ population (Clark, Washoe); also 1,000 ft from K–12 schools and 300 ft from community facilities as statewide baselines for cannabis establishments generally. [VERIFY exact NRS 678B.250 wording — direct text not opened in this run.] Sources: 420Property Nevada guide; Springer article — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Licence numbers, secondary: 20 independent lounge licences, at least 10 for social-equity applicants; retail lounge licences unlimited in number (not capped the same way). CCB random selection for independent licences was held 30 Nov 2022. Sources: Cannabis Industry Council; Cannabis Law Report, CCB random selection press release — accessed 2026-10-31.

Lounges — operating status: CONFLICT, do not overwrite Run 1 anchor without CCB list

Run 1 anchor: 2 corroborated operating state-licensed + 1 listed-but-reported-closed + 1 tribal (Dazed!, Society, Smoke and Mirrors listed, NuWu Sky High tribal).

September 2026 secondaries conflict with each other and with that anchor: one Q3 2026 report claims the CCB 1 Sept 2026 active list shows 3 licensed lounges (Dazed!, Society, Smoke and Mirrors) and that Society opened spring 2026 (CannBus, accessed 2026-10-31); another September 2026 guide claims 3 operating in Las Vegas: Dazed! (opened Feb 2024), Society Lounge at The Grove (opened Apr 2026), and Sky High at NuWu — and that Smoke and Mirrors closed in 2025 after less than a year (Euphoria Wellness guide, accessed 2026-10-31); a late-2025 guide claims only 2 operating: Dazed! (state) + NuWu Sky High (tribal), with Smoke and Mirrors closed (THC City Guides, accessed 2026-10-31).

[VERIFY — current operating count as of 31 Oct 2026 against the CCB active-licensee list. Do not publish “3 state-licensed operating” or “Society opened Apr 2026” as settled on secondary evidence alone, and note the Strip/Paradise distinction: the Strip and Paradise/Spring Valley/Enterprise/Winchester are unincorporated Clark County, not the City of Las Vegas.]

Events

  • No standalone “temporary cannabis event licence” section appears in NRS Chapter 678D as opened (sections run 678D.005–678D.510, lounge sections 678D.455–678D.480 only). [VERIFY — chapter text opened, but NAC/NCCR adoption may add a permit route.] Source: NRS Chapter 678D (leg.state.nv.us) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • CCB proposed (Jan 2024) definitions: “Temporary cannabis event coordinator,” “temporary cannabis event permit” (issued to an operational sales facility or lounge holding a final licence, non-transferable, valid only for the event dates, authorising sale/consumption of single-use products in a designated area), and “designated cannabis consumption area.” The draft application required submission 90/60 days prior (draft text shows both figures, struck/added) and a $2,000 one-time non-refundable administrative processing fee. Adoption status of that proposal was not confirmed in this run. [VERIFY.] Source: CCB Proposed Changes to NCCR — Events, 31 Jan 2024 — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Practical consequence: A private event on private property with the owner’s permission is governed by the general private-consumption rule, not by a licensed-event right to consume in public; any public-place consumption at a festival/concert remains a NRS 678D.310(3) misdemeanour (fine up to $600) unless it is inside a licensed lounge or a duly permitted designated area — the latter route is [VERIFY] as above. Do not import California-style temporary-event licences (e.g., Nevada City, CA — a different state/jurisdiction) into Nevada.

12.5 Cannabis tourism and visitor purchase rules

  • Adult-use purchase has no residency requirement in NRS 678D.200: the exemption applies to “a person who is 21 years of age or older” — no Nevada-residency condition is stated in that section. Source: NRS Chapter 678D (leg.state.nv.us) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Visitors may buy at a licensed retail store, but may only consume on private property with the owner’s permission or in a licensed lounge. Public consumption, including on the Strip, sidewalks, parks, bar patios and concert venues, is a misdemeanour up to $600 under NRS 678D.310(3). Source: same NRS 678D page — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Medical reciprocity — NRS 678C.470, secondary summary: a visitor authorised to use medical cannabis under home-state law is treated as holding a Nevada registry card while staying within Nevada limits, and a Nevada medical dispensary may serve them on proof of that authorisation; no time limit is stated in the statute summary. Nevada itself issues registry cards to residents only. Sources: Dr Green Relief guides — accessed 2026-10-31. [VERIFY — direct NRS 678C.470 text not opened in this run.] See also §10.6 for the mirror-text version.
  • Crossing state lines is federally prohibited even between legal states; cannabis is also prohibited on federal land (national parks, national forests, BLM land, Lake Mead National Recreation Area, Red Rock Canyon). Secondary summaries cite federal misdemeanour exposure on federal land (up to 6 months / $5,000 in one summary — [VERIFY penalty]). Sources: Ely News sponsored guide, 14 Aug 2026; Legal Clarity, Las Vegas guide — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Delivery to visitors is restricted: UNLV Cannabis Policy Institute (Dec 2025) summarises NCCR 7.050(3)–(4) and Clark County Code 8.65.140 as prohibiting delivery to gaming-licensed properties, hotels/resorts and, in Clark County, anywhere within the Las Vegas Boulevard gaming corridor or an H1 Zone. Quoted ordinance text: “The delivery of cannabis and cannabis products to consumers is prohibited within the Las Vegas Boulevard gaming corridor or an H1 Zone as defined in Title 30 of this Code, and on the premises of any restricted or non-restricted gaming licensee.” Source: UNLV, The 1,500 Foot Wall — accessed 2026-10-31. Corroborated: Springer article — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • No “cannabis tourism licence” or visitor-consumption permit was found beyond the lounge system above. [UNAVAILABLE / VERIFY.]

12.6 Hotel / casino / short-term rental rules

  • No single hotel-ban statute — Run 1 position confirmed by the opened text. The mechanism is: (a) public-place/vehicle/retail-store consumption ban, NRS 678D.310(3); (b) owner rights, NRS 678D.510(1)(c) — Chapter 678D does not prohibit “a person who occupies, owns or controls a privately owned property from prohibiting or otherwise restricting the smoking, cultivation, processing, manufacture, sale, delivery or transfer of cannabis on that property”; (c) employer/government-building rights in NRS 678D.510(1)(a)–(b); (d) local zoning/land-use power in NRS 678D.510(1)(d). Source: NRS Chapter 678D (leg.state.nv.us) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Practice, secondary — hotels: Most hotels/casino resorts prohibit cannabis on property as a matter of policy; cleaning fees of $250–$750 are claimed secondarily for smoke in a nonsmoking room, and ejection/ban for casino-floor use. [VERIFY — fees and enforcement are property policy, not statute; do not publish a fee range as law.] Source: Legal Clarity, Nevada recreational marijuana laws — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Secondary claim that hotel rooms are “public places” for NRS 678D.310 purposes conflicts with the owner-rights reading (another secondary correctly notes a hotel room is not a “public place” in the statutory sense, but policy breach can still lead to a property fine/ejection without a criminal public-consumption charge). [VERIFY — do not state that smoking in one’s own hotel room is automatically a $600 criminal offence; state the two-track position: criminal exposure turns on public-place/vehicle characterisation, civil/property exposure turns on the property’s ban.] Source: Legal Clarity, Las Vegas age requirements — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Casinos / gaming — Gaming Control Board policy, not a cannabis statute: 6 May 2014 NGCB Notice to Licensees (Medical Marijuana Establishments) advised that, unless federal law changed, investment or other involvement in a medical marijuana facility by a gaming-approval holder/applicant was not consistent with effective gaming regulation and would tend to reflect discredit upon gaming in Nevada. The 2018 Gaming Policy Committee resolution reinforced separation, permitting cannabis conventions/trade shows at gaming establishments but otherwise advising licensees not to contract with, lease to, finance, or maintain business relationships with cannabis businesses. Sources: UNLV, The 1,500 Foot Wall; Springer article — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Gaming licensees must comply with applicable laws; violations can lead to discipline including suspension/revocation under Gaming Commission Regulation 5.011 — secondary summary. [VERIFY — regulation text not opened.] Source: Springer article — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Clean Indoor Air Act does NOT create a cannabis right in casinos: NRS 202.2483(3)(a) excepts areas within casinos where loitering by minors is already prohibited under NRS 463.350 from the indoor-smoking ban — that is a tobacco-smoking exception, and it does not override the cannabis public-place ban, gaming policy, or a property’s cannabis ban. NRS 202.2483(2) prohibits smoking without exception within school buildings and on school property. Source: NRS 202.2483 (FindLaw) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Short-term rentals: No Nevada-wide short-term-rental cannabis statute was found in this run. The governing rule is the same owner-rights rule (NRS 678D.510(1)(c)) plus the host’s/lease terms and any local ordinance; a rental does not automatically confer a right to consume. Delivery to hotels/short-term rentals/gaming properties is prohibited under the NCCR/Clark County rules summarised in §12.5 above. [UNAVAILABLE for a statewide STR-specific cannabis rule — mark Verify before stating any city/county STR cannabis ordinance.]

12.7 Landlord / tenant rules

  • No cannabis-specific landlord/tenant statute was found in NRS Chapter 118A in the sources searched. The operative state-law hook is NRS 678D.510(1)(c) (owner/occupier/controller may prohibit/restrict smoking, cultivation, processing, manufacture, sale, delivery or transfer on privately owned property) plus ordinary lease/nuisance law. Source: NRS Chapter 678D (leg.state.nv.us) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Practice, secondary: A no-smoking lease clause is enforced as a smoking prohibition, not as a marijuana prohibition; a landlord’s claimed enforcement path is a 3-day nuisance/lease-breach notice (cited as NRS 40.2514(4)) then a 5-day Notice to Quit for Unlawful Detainer (cited as NRS 40.254(1)(a)) if smoking continues, with proof of actual smoking (not mere possession or odour) required. [VERIFY — notice periods and citations are secondary only and were not opened on leg.state.nv.us.] Source: IRES Vegas guide — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • No-smoking designation practice, secondary: No law prevents designating areas/apartments smoke-free; smokers are not a protected class; a no-smoking rule must be in the lease to be enforceable mid-term (or added by agreement / at renewal, or with 30 days’ notice for month-to-month). [VERIFY — secondary Q&A, not statute.] Source: Ask A Lawyer On Call — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Federal-housing overlay: Federally assisted housing remains subject to federal drug law; a “drug-free housing” addendum is common in sample leases. No Nevada statute found that requires a private landlord to permit medical cannabis smoking indoors, and no statute found that bars a landlord from banning it. [VERIFY — do not present either as settled; reasonable-accommodation claims for non-smoked medical use are fact-specific and were not verified.]
  • Cultivation on property not in the cultivator’s lawful possession / without consent of the lawful possessor is separately prohibited by NRS 678D.310(1)(c), with escalating fines (first $600, second $1,000, third gross misdemeanour, fourth+ category E felony). Source: NRS Chapter 678D (leg.state.nv.us) — accessed 2026-10-31.

12.8 Gun ownership conflicts

  • State prohibition — unlawful user: NRS 202.360(1)(c) (older text) provides a person shall not own or have in possession/custody/control any firearm if the person “is an unlawful user of, or addicted to, any controlled substance,” with “controlled substance” defined by reference to 21 U.S.C. §802(6). Penalty in the 2011 text is category B felony, 1–6 years + up to $5,000; a newer secondary restates the offence as category D. [VERIFY current penalty/category — do not publish either penalty as settled without opening current NRS 202.360 on leg.state.nv.us.] Sources: NRS 202.360 (Justia, 2011) — accessed 2026-10-31; Nevada Defense Group — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • State prohibition — possession while impaired: NRS 202.257(1) makes it unlawful for a person who has 0.08+ alcohol in blood/breath, or is under the influence of a controlled substance (or combined influence, or uses a chemical/solvent to a degree rendering them incapable of safely exercising actual physical control of a firearm), to have a firearm in actual physical possession. Exception: actual physical possession within the person’s personal residence solely for self-defence. Violation is a misdemeanour (NRS 202.257(3)); forfeiture only if, during the violation, the firearm was brandished, aimed or otherwise handled in a manner endangering others (NRS 202.257(4)). Sources: NRS 202.257 (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31; NRS 202.257 (public.law) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • NRS 678D.300(1)(c) cross-refers: adult-use exemption does not cover possessing a firearm in violation of NRS 202.257(1)(b). Source: NRS Chapter 678D (leg.state.nv.us) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • CCW permit: NRS 202.3657(3)(b) requires the applicant not be prohibited from possessing a firearm under NRS 202.360; NRS 202.3657(4)(d) requires the sheriff to deny/revoke if the applicant/permittee has habitually used intoxicating liquor or a controlled substance to the extent normal faculties are impaired — presumed if, in the immediately preceding 5 years, the person was convicted under NRS 484C.110 or committed for treatment under NRS 458.290–458.350. Sources: NRS 202.3657 (FindLaw) — accessed 2026-10-31; NRS 202.3657 (public.law) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Correction to a common overstatement: NRS 202.3657 does not, on its face, say “any medical card = automatic CCW denial.” Denial turns on NRS 202.360 prohibited-person status and/or the habitual-use/impairment test above. Secondary claims that “cardholders cannot get a CCW” should be read as shorthand for the federal/state prohibited-user analysis, not as a freestanding card rule. [VERIFY — sheriff practice may be stricter than the statute text.]
  • Federal conflict — 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3) / (d)(3), ATF Form 4473 Q.21.e, 2011 ATF Open Letter: Federal law bars an “unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance” from possessing/receiving firearms/ammunition, and bars sale to a person known or with reasonable cause to believe to be such a user. ATF’s 21 Sept 2011 Open Letter states any person who uses or is addicted to marijuana, regardless of state medical authorisation, is an unlawful user for federal firearms purposes. Form 4473 warns use/possession of marijuana remains unlawful under federal law regardless of state legalisation. Sources: Wilson v. Lynch, 9th Cir. 2016 (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31; Jones Mayer summary — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Wilson v. Lynch, 835 F.3d 1083 (9th Cir. 2016) — Nevada plaintiff: The Ninth Circuit affirmed dismissal of S. Rowan Wilson’s challenge (a Nevada medical-marijuana registry cardholder refused a firearm sale after the dealer learned of her card). It held the sale ban, regulation and Open Letter burdened the core Second Amendment right but survived intermediate scrutiny as reasonably fitted to preventing gun violence; First/Fifth Amendment claims also failed. Critically, Wilson lacked standing to challenge §922(g)(3) possession because she had not alleged unlawful use or possession — the case decides the sale ban, not that every cardholder is automatically a federal possessor. Source: Wilson v. Lynch (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Federal scheduling caveat — [VERIFY] before any firearms consequence is drawn from it: Older federal sources describe marijuana as Schedule I and one 2026 secondary claims an April 2026 move of state-licensed medical cannabis to Schedule III. That scheduling question is governed by the fixed federal baseline (state-licensed medical / FDA-approved products to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026, adult-use remaining Schedule I), not by the older sources; do not publish a Schedule III firearms consequence until the federal register / DEA rule and any ATF guidance update are opened. The firearms analysis above follows the Schedule I-era statutes and Wilson, and its current application to Schedule III medical activity is therefore flagged, not settled.

12.9 Pet / veterinary products

  • Veterinarians may administer/recommend hemp/CBD — narrowly: NRS 638.135(1) — a licensed veterinarian may (a) administer to an animal a product containing hemp or CBD if it has ≤0.3% THC on a dry-weight basis and is intended for use/consumption through means other than inhalation to treat a condition of the animal, and (b) recommend such a product to the owner to treat a condition. NRS 638.135(2) — the Board shall not take disciplinary action against the veterinarian or facility solely for administering/recommending under subsection 1. Definitions cross-refer to NRS 453.021 (administer), 453.033 (CBD), 557.160 (hemp), 453.139 (THC). Added 2021. Source: NRS 638.135 (Justia) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • What NRS 638.135 does NOT authorise: It does not authorise a veterinarian to administer, dispense or prescribe THC cannabis (above 0.3% THC), inhaled products, or medical cannabis as a controlled substance for animals, and no such veterinary cannabis licence was found. [UNAVAILABLE / VERIFY — do not extrapolate from hemp/CBD to THC pet products.]
  • Owner giving a pet THC edibles / pet THC intoxication: No Nevada pet-specific THC statute, dose limit, or “pet edible” rule was found in this run. General animal-cruelty / controlled-substance possession rules would be the only possible hooks, and neither was verified for this fact pattern. [UNAVAILABLE — mark Verify; do not invent a pet-THC offence or safe dose.]
  • Retail pet CBD: Pet hemp/CBD products sold at retail fall under the hemp 0.3% THC definition, not under CCB cannabis licensing, in the sources searched — but no Nevada pet-CBD labelling/registration rule was opened. [VERIFY.]

12.10 Medical cannabis in schools

  • K–12: no Nevada statute authorising school administration was found. There is no “school administration of medical cannabis” section in NRS Chapter 678C as summarised in the sources searched, and no Nevada Department of Education guidance authorising K–12 school staff to administer medical cannabis was located in this run. [UNAVAILABLE — do not import other states’ school-administration laws (e.g., Colorado/Illinois models) into Nevada.]
  • What the law does say — possession on school property loses protection:
    • Medical: NRS 678C.300(1)(d)(2) — a registry-card/letter holder is not exempt and may not raise an affirmative defence for possessing cannabis in violation of NRS 453.336 or paraphernalia in violation of NRS 453.560/453.566 if possession occurs on school property; “school property” = grounds of a public school described in NRS 388.020 and a private school as defined in NRS 394.103. Source: NRS 678C.300 (FindLaw) — accessed 2026-10-31.
    • Adult-use parallel: NRS 678D.300(1)(d)(2), same school-property carve-out. Source: NRS Chapter 678D (leg.state.nv.us) — accessed 2026-10-31.
    • Smoking ban: NRS 202.2483(2) — smoking in any form is prohibited without exception within school buildings and on school property (tobacco-smoking statute, cited for the school-property ban, not as a cannabis authorisation). Source: NRS 202.2483 (FindLaw) — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • Higher education — NSHE policy (not K–12 law): Nevada System of Higher Education policy prohibits use, possession or cultivation of marijuana, including for medical purposes, on any NSHE / NSHE-foundation owned or leased property or at any NSHE-sponsored/authorised activity, citing federal law for federally funded campuses; students required to live on campus may petition for release from housing contracts without financial penalty if they prove state-law compliance. Sources: NSHE Medical Marijuana attachment, 14 July 2014; CSN catalog — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • UNR School of Medicine research programme — repeal noted: AB 365 (2025) is described as repealing the NRS 678C.700 requirement that UNR School of Medicine establish a medical-cannabis evaluation/research programme and report quarterly, on the basis the school was not conducting such research. [VERIFY — bill status / chapter number not confirmed on leg.state.nv.us in this run.] Sources: The Nevadan, 2025 session cannabis bills — accessed 2026-10-31; Assembly Education hearing minutes, 83rd Session — accessed 2026-10-31.
  • District-level K–12 student medical-cannabis policy (e.g., Clark County School District): No current district policy text authorising or prohibiting parent-administered non-smoked medical cannabis on campus was located; the CCSD material found concerned employee drug testing (Reg. 4231), not student administration. [UNAVAILABLE — do not state a CCSD student-administration rule without opening the current CCSD policy manual.]

Research notes — covering both runs (Run 1, Parts 0–6 + Run 2, Parts 7–12)

Scope and rule for this section. No item from either run is dropped without a disposition. Items marked Resolved in Run 2 were settled, narrowed, or confirmed by Run 2 research. Items marked Still open were not resolved in either run and remain [VERIFY] / [UNAVAILABLE]. Where Run 2 changed a Run 1 position, the change is stated; where Run 2 confirmed Run 1, that is stated too. This section does not upgrade any flagged item to confirmed.

A. Items resolved, narrowed, or confirmed in Run 2

  1. USDA hemp plan status — RESOLVED in Run 2 (§7.5). Run 1 §5.11 had flagged plan status as needing re-verification. Run 2 opened the USDA AMS approved-plans list directly (updated 17 Feb 2026): “Nevada | NV Approved Plan.” Nevada therefore runs its own USDA-approved state plan (revised 25 May 2021, USDA approval 28 May 2021 per the NDA release) and does not use USDA direct HeMP licensing for Nevada producers. The Walker River Paiute Tribe’s separate USDA Hemp Producer License entry does not change that.
  2. NDA hemp registry — CONFIRMED UNAVAILABLE on re-check (§7.6). Run 1 §5.8 found no public list. Run 2 searched again and found none; the NDA hemp hub and forms hub are not registries. The one remaining check is the hub’s “Program Data and Resources” section, which was not opened directly (rate limit) — see item B.5.
  3. Hemp registration types and calendar — CONFIRMED and detailed (§§7.2–7.3). Exactly three types (grower, handler, producer), producer only alongside grower/handler registration, applications on/before 1 July, expiry 31 December, 30-day surrender notice, separate site certification, fingerprints/background, child-support statement, and NRS 557.240/557.270 inspection/testing duties are now stated from NRS Chapter 557 (opened directly).
  4. CCB Bulletin 2026-01 — CONFIRMED by direct open (§11.1). Run 1 flagged the 28 Oct 2026 bulletin’s CCB PDF URL as unverified. Run 2 opened it directly: Orange Juice Mac 3.5g, batch F1H8OJM, package 1A4040300007081000009370, failed for Enterobacteriaceae and Total Coliforms after post-sampling alteration. Final enforcement outcome is still open (B.18).
  5. Pre-2021 DUI figures — CORRECTED (§12.2). Run 1 flagged current per se thresholds in NRS 484C.110 as [VERIFY] and warned against pre-2021 figures. Run 2 establishes the structure: AB 400 (effective 1 July 2021) removed per se cannabis limits for misdemeanour (first/second) DUI; per se blood thresholds (2 ng/ml delta-9-THC, 5 ng/ml metabolite) survive only for felony DUI (third or subsequent within 7 years). The direct leg.state.nv.us NRS 484C.110 page was still not opened, so verbatim subsection wording remains [VERIFY] (B.19).
  6. Hotel/casino ban — CONFIRMED as no single statute (§12.6). Run 1’s position stands: the ban operates through the public-place/vehicle ban (NRS 678D.310(3)), owner rights (NRS 678D.510(1)(c)), and Gaming Control Board policy since 2014, not a single “hotel ban statute.” Hotel-room “public place” characterisation remains a secondary conflict (B.20).
  7. Transfer, renewal, relocation, monopoly-cap rules — DETAILED (§11.2). Run 1’s “no timeline for transfer approval” finding stands; renewal (annual, Accela, from 60 days before expiry, 90 days to renew or deemed surrendered), relocation (same local jurisdiction only, local approval first), the 10% monopoly cap in counties ≥100,000 with the retail/lounge exception, and the 2-year independent-lounge transfer lock are now stated with citations.
  8. Testing framework — DETAILED (§11.1). Regulation 11 test categories, limits excerpt, COA filing, proficiency testing, SB 157 (2025) lot definitions (15 lb flower / 45 lb other plant matter / 150 lb wet extraction material) and ASTM D8334/D8334M alignment, the 50-lot annual retest cap, and the pesticide-retest-to-NDA rule are now stated. Pending R104-26 sampling changes are not law (B.17).
  9. Ballot history — PARTIALLY ADVANCED, NOT RESOLVED (§9.3). Secondary percentages are now tabulated (1998 Q9 58.7%, 2000 Q9 65.4%, 2002 Q9 defeated 61% opposed, 2006 Q7 defeated 56% opposed, 2016 Q2 54% / 54.47%), with the 2002 measure distinguished from the 1998/2000 measure. Official Secretary of State results remain outstanding (B.2).
  10. Tribal operations — PARTIALLY ADVANCED, NOT RESOLVED (§§10.2–10.3). Las Vegas Paiute compact lines 0–422 are now summarised; Ely Shoshone (Tsaa Nesunkwa), Yerington Paiute (including the distinct city–tribe compact), Pyramid Lake (compact reported, text not found), Moapa (Mint, opened 20 June 2026, compact not found), Lovelock and Walker River (representation reported, no operation verified) are now tabulated. No compact text beyond the Las Vegas Paiute lines 0–422 was opened (B.3).
  11. Property search — ADVANCED, NOT RESOLVED (§8). Three address-identified fee-simple candidates and thirteen licence/business sales are now tabulated with evidence strength, plus explicit exclusions (including the Jackson, Michigan suite that must not be counted as Nevada) and explicit “searched and found nothing” markets. No candidate has both confirmed live status on 31 Oct 2026 and official zoning verification (B.7).

  25. What this combined research does NOT do. It does not give legal advice, does not advise transporting cannabis across state lines or onto federal land (which §10.7 states is federally prohibited even between legal states), does not state that any flagged item is settled, and does not present a proposed bill, amendment, proposed regulation or failed measure as current law. Nevada has no regular session in 2026; the next regular session is 2027, and reintroductions get new bill numbers. Any figure, fee, licence count or operating status flagged [VERIFY] or [UNAVAILABLE] in either file must be re-checked on the official source named before being used in a filing, application, or publication.
